Overview
A mentorship-style AWS curriculum

Go from zero cloud knowledge to Principal Architect, one production decision at a time.

This academy is built around a single running case study — ShopSphere, a production-grade e-commerce platform — instead of disconnected toy examples. Every AWS concept is taught the way a Principal Solutions Architect would explain it on the job: what problem it solves, why you'd choose it over the alternatives, what breaks, what it costs, and how it earns its place in ShopSphere's architecture.

The case study: ShopSphere

ShopSphere starts as a single server and evolves, module by module, into a globally distributed, multi-region, event-driven e-commerce platform — the same way a real product does. Customer-facing features (catalog, cart, checkout, payments, order tracking, recommendations) and backend domains (Identity, Inventory, Pricing, Order, Payment, Shipment, Notification, Analytics) are introduced as the architecture needs them, not all at once.

ap-south-1primary region
ap-southeast-1DR region
99.99%target availability

How this academy works

Modules marked available below are fully built: concept, diagrams, ShopSphere decisions, trade-offs, a hands-on lab and interview questions. Modules marked planned show exactly what's coming — this mirrors the live mentorship, where each module is built out as we work through it together, rather than dumped all at once.

Reference videos link out to YouTube rather than playing inline — that's a platform constraint of this page, not a design choice. Click through and watch on YouTube itself.

MAP The full learning path

Ten levels, built on the same ShopSphere system end to end. Hover any box on a larger screen, or just read top to bottom — the path is strictly sequential, each level assumes the ones above it.

Level 1 · Cloud & Distributed Systems Fundamentals

Cloud Computing Fundamentals

● ~20 min read● Foundational, no prerequisites● ShopSphere: origin story

Before any AWS service, you need the vocabulary and economics that every architecture decision rests on. Bottom line: cloud computing replaces owning fixed infrastructure with renting elastic, metered infrastructure — and almost every AWS design decision you'll make in this academy is a refinement of that one idea.

01 What is cloud computing?

Cloud computing is on-demand, self-service access — over the internet — to computing resources (servers, storage, databases, networking, software) owned and operated by a third party (AWS) and offered as a metered utility, the way electricity is.

ShopSphere, before and after

2010, no cloud: launching ShopSphere means guessing a year's traffic, buying physical servers sized for that guess plus a safety margin, building or renting a data-center room, waiting 6–12 weeks for hardware, and paying for all of it in cash before a single order ships. Guess low, and the site crashes on your best sales day. Guess high, and the extra capacity sits idle 364 days a year.

Today, on AWS: ShopSphere provisions a virtual server in minutes, pays per second it runs, and during a flash sale requests more capacity programmatically from AWS's effectively unlimited pooled fleet — then releases it, and stops paying, the moment demand drops.

The five characteristics every definition of cloud computing rests on (NIST model)

CharacteristicWhat it meansShopSphere example
On-demand self-serviceYou provision resources yourself, via console/CLI/API, no human at AWS involvedAn engineer spins up a database at 2am with no approval call
Broad network accessReachable over standard internet protocols from any deviceOps team manages infrastructure from a laptop or phone
Resource poolingAWS's physical hardware serves many customers at once, strongly isolatedShopSphere's VM may share a physical host with a competitor, safely
Rapid elasticityCapacity scales out and in automatically, often within secondsAuto Scaling adds checkout servers during a flash sale, removes them after
Measured serviceUsage is metered and billed like a utilityShopSphere's AWS bill moves with real traffic, not a fixed lease

02 CAPEX vs OPEX — the financial shift

CAPEX (capital expenditure): large upfront spend on long-lived assets, depreciated over years — buying servers, building data centers. OPEX (operating expenditure): ongoing cost expensed as incurred — your AWS bill, like electricity or water.

This is why cloud adoption is as much a CFO decision as a CTO decision: it trades a large, risky, upfront bet for a variable cost that scales with the business. A startup launches ShopSphere for a few hundred dollars a month instead of raising capital to buy a data center's worth of hardware; if the business doesn't take off, spend simply stops, instead of five years of depreciating idle servers.

03 Elasticity vs scalability — precisely, not interchangeably

Scalability is a design property: can the system handle more load by adding resources? Vertical scaling makes one server bigger (more CPU/RAM) — simple, but capped by the largest instance size and usually needs downtime. Horizontal scaling adds more servers behind a load balancer — effectively unlimited, and the pattern that also buys you fault tolerance, since no single instance is a point of failure.

Elasticity is scalability plus automation plus the ability to shrink back down. A system that scales out but never scales in just gets progressively more expensive — the "scale in" half is the part beginners forget.

ShopSphere example

During a flash sale, ShopSphere's Auto Scaling Group detects CPU above 70% and launches 8 more checkout instances within minutes — elasticity. At 2am, with traffic down, it terminates those same 8 instances automatically — the scale-in half that makes it elastic rather than merely scalable.

04 Availability, reliability, durability, fault tolerance

Four words used loosely in English, precisely defined in distributed systems — and tested constantly in architecture interviews.

TermPrecise meaningExample figure
Availability% of time the system responds, expressed in "nines"99.99% ("four nines") ≈ 52 min downtime/year
ReliabilityThe system works correctly and recovers well, over time — not just "is it up"Orders process correctly, not merely "server returns 200"
DurabilityProbability stored data is not lost, independent of availabilityS3 Standard: 99.999999999% ("11 nines")
Fault toleranceThe system keeps working, with no visible impact, despite a component failingOne AZ fails; checkout keeps working from the other two

Where people mix these up

S3 can be briefly unavailable (a request fails to get a response) while the underlying data stays perfectly durable (not lost) — the two properties are achieved by different mechanisms and fail independently. Don't conflate "the API answered" with "the data is safe."

05 High availability vs disaster recovery vs geographic redundancy

High availability (HA): architecting across multiple AZs, usually within one region, so a single component failure (server, AZ, network path) doesn't cause an outage — automatic failover in seconds. Disaster recovery (DR): the plan for recovering from a catastrophic event (a whole region down), defined by two numbers you must know cold — RTO (Recovery Time Objective: how long can we be down?) and RPO (Recovery Point Objective: how much data can we afford to lose, in time?). Geographic redundancy is the underlying technique both rely on: spreading copies across physically distinct locations so one localized failure doesn't destroy every copy.

06 The infrastructure abstraction spectrum

This is the single most important model for choosing AWS compute later. As you move along it, you trade control for AWS managing more of the undifferentiated heavy lifting.

LayerYou manageAWS managesAWS service
Bare metalEverything — hardware, OS, runtimeNothingRare; specialized licensing cases
Virtual machinesOS, runtime, patching, scalingPhysical hardware, hypervisorEC2
ContainersApp + dependenciesOS-level isolation, orchestration planeECS / EKS
ServerlessJust your codeServer, OS, runtime, scaling, patchingLambda

ShopSphere starts on EC2 so the fundamentals of compute, networking and scaling are explicit and visible. Later, pieces (image processing, order-confirmation notifications) move to Lambda, and core services containerize onto ECS/EKS as the system evolves toward microservices — a deliberate, informed choice on this spectrum, not a default.

07 Managed services

A managed service is one where AWS operates, patches, scales and ensures the durability/availability of the underlying infrastructure — you interact only through an API or console. Amazon RDS vs. self-managed MySQL on EC2 is the canonical example: RDS handles patching, backups, replication and failover; self-managed means you own all of it. The trade-off repeats throughout this academy — less operational burden and faster time-to-value, for less low-level control and sometimes higher cost per unit of raw compute.

08 Hands-on lab

  1. Map the spectrum. List five everyday tools or apps you use, and place each on the bare-metal→serverless spectrum based on how much infrastructure you think you manage versus the provider.
  2. Do the CAPEX/OPEX math. A used physical server costs $4,000 upfront and lasts 4 years. An equivalent EC2 instance costs $90/month. At what month does the cloud option become more expensive in raw dollar terms — and why might ShopSphere still prefer it even past that point?
  3. Classify a failure. ShopSphere's checkout API returns HTTP 200 for every request but silently charges customers twice. Is this an availability problem, a reliability problem, or both? Justify your answer using the Module 1 definitions.

09 Interview questions

Explain the difference between scalability and elasticity to someone who thinks they're the same thing.
Scalability is the system's capability to grow (vertically or horizontally) — a static design property. Elasticity adds automation in both directions: the system scales out under load and back in when load drops, without a human deciding each time. A system can be scalable without being elastic (manually resized); elasticity implies scalability but not the reverse.
Can a system be highly available but not durable? Give a concrete example.
Yes. An in-memory cache cluster spread across three AZs can be extremely available (always responds) while being completely non-durable (a full restart loses everything, because nothing was ever written to persistent storage). Availability is about responding; durability is specifically about not losing stored data over time.
Why might a CFO prefer OPEX over CAPEX even if the 5-year total cost is identical?
Cash flow and risk. CAPEX requires a large cash outlay before any revenue is generated and locks in a bet regardless of how the business performs. OPEX scales with actual usage/revenue in real time, and if the business fails or pivots, spend simply stops instead of leaving five years of depreciating, idle, already-paid-for hardware on the balance sheet.

Level 2 · AWS Fundamentals

AWS Global Infrastructure

● ~22 min read● Builds on Module 01● ShopSphere: picks its home region

Bottom line: region and AZ choice is one of the first architecture decisions on any real workload, and it's rarely revisited — it sets latency to your users, which compliance regimes apply, which services you can even use, and your disaster-recovery topology.

01 Regions — the top-level container

A Region is a fully independent, geographically isolated area containing multiple Availability Zones. AWS operates 39 launched Regions (2 more announced) as of late 2026 — always check AWS's own infrastructure page for the live count. Naming follows <geo>-<direction>-<number>: us-east-1 (N. Virginia, AWS's oldest/largest), ap-south-1 (Mumbai), eu-west-1 (Ireland).

Regions are isolated by design

No data or API calls cross a region boundary unless you explicitly configure it (S3 Cross-Region Replication, a multi-region database). This is a deliberate fault-isolation boundary: a bad deployment or outage in ap-south-1 cannot cascade into eu-west-1. It's also usually how compliance questions get answered — "our data never leaves India" means simply never provisioning outside ap-south-1/ap-south-2.

Not every service exists in every region — confirm availability via AWS's Regional Services List before committing an architecture to a region.

02 Availability Zones

One or more discrete data centers with redundant power, networking and cooling, physically separated from other AZs in the same region by a few to a few tens of kilometers. AWS runs 124 AZs (7 more announced), 3–6 per region — 3 is AWS's minimum standard, because it's the smallest number that lets a quorum-based system survive losing one AZ while keeping a majority.

AZs sit far enough apart to survive a localized disaster, but close enough (single-digit-millisecond private-fiber latency) to run synchronous replication between them — exactly what makes Multi-AZ RDS practical.

Gotcha: AZ names vs AZ IDs

ap-south-1a in your account is not guaranteed to be the same physical location as ap-south-1a in someone else's — AWS randomizes the mapping per account to spread load evenly. For a stable cross-account reference, use the immutable AZ ID (e.g. aps1-az1) via aws ec2 describe-availability-zones.

03 Region → AZ → edge, assembled

04 Edge Locations & Points of Presence

Regions/AZs are where you run compute. Points of Presence get content and DNS physically close to the user, independent of where your servers live — AWS runs 410+ PoPs (400+ Edge Locations + 13 regional caches) across 90+ cities in 48+ countries. Three services live here: CloudFront (CDN — caches ShopSphere's product images near each shopper), Route 53 (DNS answered from the nearest PoP), and Global Accelerator (routes even non-cacheable traffic onto the AWS backbone as early as possible).

05 Local Zones & Wavelength Zones

Local Zones (~33) extend a parent Region into a metro area without a full Region, for single-digit-ms latency to that city. Wavelength Zones (~30) embed AWS compute directly inside telco 5G networks, for mobile-first, latency-critical workloads. Neither is on ShopSphere's critical path today — file them away for a future latency-sensitive mobile feature.

06 Choosing a region — the decision framework

FactorWhy it dominates
1. Compliance & data residencyOften non-negotiable by law — can override every other factor
2. Latency to usersPhysical distance = round-trip time; very visible on checkout flows
3. Service & feature availabilityNew services land in a handful of regions first
4. CostCompute/transfer pricing varies by region; rarely the deciding factor alone

ShopSphere's decision

Primary: ap-south-1 (Mumbai) — lowest latency to ShopSphere's core Indian customer base, satisfies data-residency expectations, full service availability. DR: ap-southeast-1 (Singapore) — distant enough to avoid Mumbai's regional risk profile, close enough in-continent for acceptable replication latency.

07 Hands-on lab

  1. Run aws ec2 describe-regions (or browse the AWS console's region picker) and list every region enabled on a fresh account.
  2. Run aws ec2 describe-availability-zones --region ap-south-1 and note both the ZoneName and the stable ZoneId for each AZ.
  3. ShopSphere is expanding to serve customers in Brazil. Using the four-factor framework above, name the region you'd pick and justify it on each factor.

08 Interview questions

Why does AWS randomize the AZ-name-to-physical-AZ mapping per account?
To spread load evenly across all AZs in a region. If "AZ a" always mapped to the same physical location for every account, that one AZ would attract disproportionate traffic from everyone defaulting to it first, unbalancing the region's physical capacity.
A startup wants "99.99% availability" but is deployed in a single AZ. What's the honest answer?
A single AZ cannot deliver 99.99% in any meaningful production sense — AZs do fail (power, networking, physical disasters), and a single-AZ design has no redundancy against that. 99.99% requires, at minimum, a Multi-AZ architecture within a region; true resilience against a region-level event requires a DR strategy across regions.
When would you choose a Local Zone over simply accepting higher latency from the nearest Region?
When a specific feature has a hard latency budget the nearest full Region can't meet for a particular metro area — e.g. real-time AR try-on, live multiplayer, or trading systems — and standing up a full new Region there isn't justified by overall demand. Local Zones trade a smaller service catalog for proximity in exactly that gap.

Level 2 · AWS Fundamentals

Shared Responsibility & IAM Foundations

● ~24 min read● Builds on Module 02● ShopSphere: secures its first account

Bottom line: most real-world cloud breaches trace back to one of two failures — not knowing which side of the shared responsibility line you own, or a misconfigured root/IAM identity. Get both right before ShopSphere touches a single resource.

01 The Shared Responsibility Model

AWS is responsible for security of the cloud — physical data centers, hardware, network infrastructure, the hypervisor, and (for managed services) the underlying OS/platform. You are responsible for security in the cloud — your data, your IAM configuration, your application layer, your network traffic protection, your firewall rules. One constant with zero exceptions: you always own your data, and you always own IAM.

02 AWS account fundamentals

An AWS account is the basic container for resources, billing and security isolation — every resource belongs to exactly one account, identified by a unique 12-digit Account ID. Every account has one root user, created automatically, tied to the account's email, with complete unrestricted control — including closing the account.

03 Root user — maximum power, maximum danger

Current policy — MFA on root is now mandatory

AWS requires MFA on the root user for every account type — standalone, Organizations management, and member accounts alike. If not already configured, AWS requires registration within 35 days of first console sign-in. (This was only "strongly recommended" in older material — don't trust a tutorial that treats it as optional.)

Recommended MFA, strongest first: FIDO hardware security keys (phishing-resistant), hardware TOTP tokens, virtual authenticator apps. Register more than one device so a lost phone doesn't lock you out.

Root should be used only for a short list of root-exclusive tasks (account recovery, closing the account, certain billing/support-plan changes) — never for day-to-day work. Never create root access keys. Use a shared business email alias, not a personal inbox, and split control of the email inbox and the MFA device between two people so no one person can unilaterally take over root.

04 IAM core concepts

Building blockWhat it is
IAM UserIdentity for a person/app with long-term credentials (password or access keys) — minimize use
IAM GroupNamed collection of users sharing the same permissions
IAM RoleIdentity with no long-term credentials, assumed temporarily for short-lived, auto-rotating credentials via STS
IAM PolicyJSON document defining Allow/Deny on Actions against Resources, optionally under Conditions

Least privilege is the organizing principle: grant exactly what a task needs, nothing more. Attaching AdministratorAccess everywhere because it's easiest is the anti-pattern that turns one leaked credential into a catastrophe instead of a contained incident.

{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{
    "Effect": "Allow",
    "Action": ["s3:GetObject", "s3:PutObject"],
    "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::shopsphere-product-images/*",
    "Condition": { "StringEquals": { "aws:RequestedRegion": "ap-south-1" } }
  }]
}

An explicit Deny anywhere in policy evaluation always wins over any Allow — the single most important rule for reasoning about conflicting policies.

05 IAM roles — how delegation actually works

A role carries two documents: a trust policy (who can assume it) and a permission policy (what it can do once assumed). ShopSphere's checkout service on EC2 gets an instance role with a trust policy allowing the EC2 service, and a permission policy scoped to exactly the S3 actions/bucket it needs — delivered as auto-rotating temporary credentials via the instance metadata service. No access key ever sits in code, a config file, or a Git repo — hardcoded long-term keys are one of the most common real-world breach vectors, and this pattern eliminates them entirely.

06 The current recommended identity pattern

Who/what needs accessMechanismCredential type
Humans (you, your team)IAM Identity CenterTemporary, auto-rotated
Workloads on AWS (EC2, Lambda, ECS)IAM RolesTemporary, delivered automatically
Workloads outside AWSRoles Anywhere / AssumeRoleWithSAML / AssumeRoleWithWebIdentityTemporary via STS
Legacy exceptions onlyIAM UsersLong-term — minimize, rotate

This is a real shift from older material where "create an IAM user" was step one. ShopSphere's team signs in through IAM Identity Center, never by creating individual IAM user passwords; every piece of compute uses roles, never embedded keys.

07 When NOT to rely on IAM alone

Trade-off

IAM governs who can call which AWS API — it says nothing about application-layer authorization (can this customer see that order?) or about protecting against application bugs like SQL injection. IAM is necessary infrastructure-layer security, not sufficient application-layer security — ShopSphere still needs its own authorization logic and input validation regardless of how tight IAM is.

08 Hands-on lab

  1. Write an IAM policy JSON that allows reading (but not writing or deleting) objects in a bucket named shopsphere-invoices, scoped to a single AWS account via a Condition.
  2. Diagram, on paper, the trust policy and permission policy you'd write for a Lambda function that needs to read from a DynamoDB table and nothing else.
  3. List three root-user actions that genuinely cannot be delegated to any IAM role, and explain why each is root-exclusive.

09 Interview questions

Where does the shared responsibility line sit differently for EC2 versus S3?
On EC2, you own the guest OS up — patching, firewall rules, everything you install. On S3, AWS owns almost the entire stack; your remaining responsibility narrows to access policy configuration and data classification — which is exactly why misconfigured public buckets, not infrastructure failures, are the dominant real-world S3 breach pattern.
Why do IAM roles use temporary credentials instead of long-term access keys?
A leaked long-term key is valid until someone notices and rotates it — potentially months. A leaked temporary credential from an assumed role expires automatically, typically within an hour, sharply limiting the blast radius of any single leak without requiring anyone to notice first.
What's wrong with granting a developer AdministratorAccess "just for now, we'll tighten it later"?
"Later" rarely arrives, and in the meantime a single compromised or misused credential has unrestricted account access instead of a scoped blast radius. Least privilege should be the starting point, built up via IAM Access Analyzer's actual-usage data, not a broad grant walked back after the fact.

Level 2 · AWS Fundamentals

AWS Organizations & Multi-Account Strategy

● ~20 min read● Builds on Module 03● ShopSphere: structures its account landscape

Bottom line: production AWS is never one account. The account boundary is a stronger security and blast-radius control than any IAM policy — design the multi-account structure before building on top of it, because retrofitting it later onto a live business is painful.

01 Why one account doesn't scale

Mixing dev experimentation and production customer data in one account means a compromised low-privilege dev credential, or a scripting bug, is one mistake away from touching production — no IAM policy closes that gap as reliably as a hard account boundary does. The same fault-isolation principle from Module 2's Availability Zones applies one layer up the stack: AZs isolate infrastructure failures, separate accounts isolate security, billing and operational failures.

02 AWS Organizations & consolidated billing

AWS Organizations centrally manages multiple accounts. The management account creates the org and holds ultimate administrative control — and should host zero workloads, since its compromise endangers every account beneath it. Member accounts are isolated by default and governed centrally. Consolidated billing rolls every account's usage into one bill, with AWS automatically applying volume discounts and Reserved Instance/Savings Plan sharing org-wide, while still breaking out per-account cost.

03 Organizational Units & ShopSphere's account tree

AWS's recommended baseline: Security OU (Log Archive + Audit accounts, centralizing CloudTrail and GuardDuty/Security Hub); Infrastructure OU (shared networking, CI/CD); Workloads OU, split into Prod and Non-Prod; Sandbox OU for free experimentation with zero path to real customer data; Suspended OU as a holding area before closing an account.

04 Service Control Policies — the rule beginners get wrong

SCPs never grant. They only set a ceiling — even over root.

An SCP attached to an OU/account defines the maximum available permissions for every identity inside, including that account's own root user. Even if an IAM policy inside the account explicitly allows an action, an SCP above it that doesn't allow it blocks the action. The final permission is always the intersection of both — IAM policies grant, SCPs constrain.

Typical SCP guardrails, almost always written as explicit Deny: block leaving the Organization; restrict which regions can be used (enforcing the Module 2 ap-south-1/ap-southeast-1 decision org-wide, not by convention); block disabling CloudTrail/GuardDuty/Config; deny root actions entirely in workload accounts; cap spend in the Sandbox OU.

05 Resource Control Policies — closing the gap SCPs leave

SCPs restrict what a principal (your user/role) can do. Resource Control Policies (RCPs) restrict what a resource-based policy (an S3 bucket policy, a KMS key policy) is allowed to grant — regardless of which account the request comes from, including accounts outside your org entirely. An SCP can't stop someone writing a bucket policy that grants access to an external account; an org-wide RCP can enforce "no bucket or key policy may ever grant access outside our organization," directly preventing the leaked-public-bucket scenario from Module 3's Shared Responsibility discussion as an unbypassable, org-wide guardrail.

06 AWS Control Tower

Hand-rolling the OU tree, SCPs, RCPs and centralized logging/security accounts is slow and error-prone. Control Tower automates this baseline — called a Landing Zone — and ships an Account Factory for self-service, pre-governed account creation. Real production build-outs use Control Tower rather than hand-rolling the structure; the manual model above is the mental model needed to understand what it's doing, which matters for troubleshooting and interviews.

07 Hands-on lab

  1. Sketch an OU tree for ShopSphere once it splits into Order, Payment and Catalog domain teams, each needing their own prod and non-prod accounts.
  2. Write (in plain English, then as SCP-style pseudo-JSON) a guardrail that blocks every account in the Sandbox OU from launching an instance type larger than t3.medium.
  3. Explain, in one paragraph, why an RCP and not an SCP is the right tool to prevent any S3 bucket in the organization from ever being made public.

08 Interview questions

Why shouldn't the management account host any workloads?
It's the single most sensitive account in the organization — compromising it threatens every member account beneath it. Keeping it limited to governance and billing minimizes its attack surface and the number of people/processes that ever need to touch it.
An SCP denies a service in an OU, but a developer's IAM policy explicitly allows it. What happens?
The action is denied. SCPs set the ceiling of what's possible in an account; IAM policies can never grant beyond that ceiling, no matter how permissive they are. The effective permission is the intersection of the SCP and the IAM policy, and an SCP's absence of an Allow (or presence of a Deny) wins.
Give a real scenario an RCP catches that an SCP cannot.
An engineer in your account writes an S3 bucket policy granting read access to a specific external AWS account (perhaps by mistake, perhaps via a copy-pasted policy from a tutorial). The request to read that bucket comes from outside your organization, so your SCPs on your own principals never evaluate it. An RCP on the bucket's resource-based policy can block that grant regardless of who is making the request.

Level 2 · AWS Fundamentals

Amazon EC2 & Compute Fundamentals

● ~26 min read● Builds on Module 04● ShopSphere: launches its first server● AWS service

Bottom line: EC2 is a virtual machine rented by the second, running on AWS-managed physical hardware. It's the most flexible compute option in this academy and, precisely because of that flexibility, the easiest one to misconfigure, oversize, or overpay for — this module is as much about the decision framework as it is about the service itself.

01 Problem

ShopSphere's checkout service needs somewhere to actually run: a process that holds application code, listens on a port, and talks to a database. Module 1's abstraction spectrum named four options; EC2 is the first one this academy builds with, because it makes every moving part — the OS, the network interface, the disk, the firewall — explicit and inspectable before any of it gets hidden behind a managed service.

02 Concept

Amazon EC2 (Elastic Compute Cloud) provides resizable virtual servers — instances — running on AWS's virtualization layer, the Nitro System. Nitro offloads networking, storage and security functions from the host CPU onto dedicated hardware, which is why modern EC2 instances perform close to bare metal despite being virtualized, and why AWS can enforce strong isolation between tenants sharing the same physical host (the resource-pooling idea from Module 1, made concrete).

Every instance is launched from an AMI (Amazon Machine Image) — a template containing an OS, pre-installed software, and configuration — and is sized by an instance type, which fixes its vCPU count, memory, network bandwidth and (for some types) local storage.

03 How it works

Instance type anatomyExample: m7g.xlarge
Familym — general purpose (balanced CPU:RAM)
Generation7 — 7th generation of this family
Processorg — Graviton (AWS's Arm-based chip); no letter = Intel, a = AMD
Sizexlarge — 4 vCPU / 16 GiB RAM on this family's ratio

Family letters you'll meet constantly: t (burstable, cheap, for variable/low average load), m (balanced general purpose), c (compute-optimized, high CPU:RAM ratio), r (memory-optimized), i/d (storage-optimized, local NVMe), g/p (GPU, for ML/graphics). Current-generation families increasingly offer a Graviton (Arm) variant — e.g. m8g, r8g — which typically costs meaningfully less per unit of compute than the equivalent x86 instance, provided your software stack supports Arm (most modern runtimes do).

04 ShopSphere use case

ShopSphere's first production instance

The checkout service launches on an m7g.large (general purpose, Graviton, 2 vCPU/8 GiB) in a private subnet of ap-south-1's AZ-A, built from a custom AMI baked with the application runtime pre-installed, a 30 GiB gp3 EBS root volume, a Security Group allowing inbound traffic only from the Application Load Balancer, and an IAM instance role scoped to exactly the S3 bucket and DynamoDB table checkout needs — no broader.

05 Functional & non-functional requirements

FunctionalEnabled by
Run the checkout application process continuouslyA persistently running EC2 instance
Scale out during a flash saleLaunch Template + Auto Scaling Group (Module 06)
Reach the database and S3VPC networking + IAM instance role
Non-functional dimensionImpact of EC2
AvailabilityA single instance is a single point of failure — needs Multi-AZ + Auto Scaling to mean anything in production
ScalabilityHorizontal, via Auto Scaling; vertical, via stopping, resizing and restarting (brief downtime)
PerformanceDirectly bound by chosen instance type's vCPU/memory/network allocation
ReliabilityAWS replaces failed underlying hardware, but your OS/app-level failures are entirely yours to detect and recover from
SecurityFull guest-OS responsibility (Module 03's shared responsibility line) — patching, hardening, firewall rules
MaintainabilityHighest operational burden of the four compute options on Module 1's spectrum
ObservabilityNothing automatic beyond basic host metrics — application logging/metrics must be instrumented explicitly
CostBilled per second while running, regardless of utilization — an idle instance still costs full price

06 Alternatives

EC2ECS/EKSLambda
Unit of deploymentWhole virtual machineContainerFunction
You patch the OS?YesNo (on Fargate) / Yes (on EC2 launch type)Never
Scale-to-zero?No — minimum one running instance costs moneyPossible with Fargate + scale-to-zero configYes, natively
Cold startMinutes (full boot)SecondsMilliseconds–seconds
Best fitLong-running, stateful, or needs OS-level controlContainerized microservices at steady loadEvent-driven, spiky, short-lived work

Full head-to-head comparison, with ShopSphere scenarios for each, in Module 09 — Compute Deep Dive.

07 When NOT to use it

EC2 is the wrong default for…

• Spiky, infrequent workloads (a report generated twice a day) — Lambda avoids paying for 23.9 idle hours.
• Teams that don't want OS ownership — a managed container platform or serverless removes patching entirely.
• Workloads needing true scale-to-zero — an always-on EC2 fleet has an availability floor that spiky traffic doesn't justify paying for.
• Pure object storage or database needs — don't run your own database server on EC2 when RDS/Aurora/DynamoDB solve it with less operational burden (Module 08).

08 Trade-offs

EC2 trades operational simplicity for control. You get full access to the guest OS, any runtime version, any kernel tuning — and you own patch management, security hardening, capacity planning and the failure modes that come with a long-lived process (memory leaks, disk fill-up, zombie processes). It's the most portable option (fewer AWS-specific APIs baked into your app) and the easiest to reason about for engineers coming from traditional infrastructure — at the cost of being the most manual option to operate well at scale.

09 Failure scenarios

FailureWhat happensMitigation
Single instance crashesCheckout goes down entirely if it's the only instanceAuto Scaling Group with a minimum of 2+ instances across AZs
Underlying physical host failsInstance becomes unreachable; AWS does not auto-restart it on new hardware by defaultAuto Scaling Group health checks replace it; or enable instance auto-recovery via CloudWatch alarm
Entire AZ failsEvery instance in that AZ is gone at onceSpread instances across ≥2 AZs (Module 26, Multi-AZ Architecture)
Sudden 20x traffic spikeCPU saturates, latency climbs, requests start timing outAuto Scaling policy tuned to react before saturation, not after
EBS volume failsInstance loses its disk; app and possibly OS become unusableRegular snapshots; stateless app design so a lost instance is simply replaced, not recovered

10 Security

  • IAM instance role, never embedded access keys — exactly the pattern from Module 03.
  • Security Groups as a stateful, instance-level firewall: default-deny inbound, explicitly allow only what's needed (e.g. port 443 from the ALB's security group only, not 0.0.0.0/0).
  • No SSH key pairs for production access. Current AWS guidance favors AWS Systems Manager Session Manager for shell access: no open inbound port 22 at all, every session is logged to CloudTrail, and access is governed entirely by IAM — eliminating both key-management overhead and an open attack-surface port.
  • EBS encryption at rest via KMS, and encryption in transit for anything crossing the network (TLS termination at the load balancer, or end-to-end for PCI-sensitive payment paths).
  • Patch management via Systems Manager Patch Manager rather than manual, ad-hoc patching.

11 Production configuration

DevelopmentProduction
Instance count1, manually managed2+ across AZs, in an Auto Scaling Group
AccessSSH key pair, maybeSession Manager only, no open port 22
PatchingManual, wheneverSystems Manager Patch Manager on a schedule
AMIStock AMI, configured by handCustom "golden" AMI, built and tested via a pipeline (Module 24)
MonitoringBasic CloudWatch host metricsCustom app metrics, log aggregation, alarms tied to SLOs
PurchasingOn-DemandSavings Plan for baseline + Spot/On-Demand for burst

12 Monitoring

CloudWatch collects host-level metrics automatically (CPU, network, disk I/O for most instance types) at a 5-minute default granularity, or 1-minute with detailed monitoring enabled. Memory and disk-space utilization are not collected by default — they require the CloudWatch agent installed on the instance, a detail that catches teams off guard when "monitoring is on" but memory pressure goes undetected until an OOM kill. Pair host metrics with application-level logs (shipped to CloudWatch Logs or an aggregator) and set alarms tied to an actual SLO (e.g. p95 latency < 300ms), not just raw CPU%, which is a poor proxy for user-facing health.

13 Cost

OptionCommitmentTypical discount vs On-DemandUse for
On-DemandNoneBaseline (0%)Unpredictable or short-lived workloads
Savings Plans1 or 3 years, $/hour commitmentUp to ~72%Steady baseline usage, flexible across instance families
Reserved Instances1 or 3 years, specific instance configUp to ~75%Known, stable long-term capacity
SpotNone — can be reclaimed with 2 min warningUp to ~90%Fault-tolerant, interruptible work (batch jobs, CI runners)

The most common EC2 cost mistake

Leaving development/staging instances running 24/7 on On-Demand pricing. A scheduled stop/start (via Instance Scheduler or a simple Lambda+EventBridge rule) for non-prod instances outside working hours routinely cuts that slice of the bill by 60–70% with zero functional impact — the single highest-leverage, lowest-risk EC2 cost optimization most teams skip.

14 Hands-on lab

  1. In the AWS console (or on paper if you don't have an account yet), choose an instance type for a workload that needs 4 vCPU, 32 GiB RAM, and benefits from Arm compatibility. Name the exact instance type and justify the family/generation/processor/size choice.
  2. Write the Security Group rules (as a short table: type, port, source) for ShopSphere's checkout instance, assuming it only ever receives traffic from an Application Load Balancer and only ever needs outbound HTTPS.
  3. Calculate: a workload needs 4 m7g.large instances running 24/7, steady, for the next year. Roughly compare the annual cost under On-Demand vs. a 1-year Savings Plan, and state which you'd recommend and why.

15 Interview questions

Why does AWS recommend Session Manager over SSH key pairs for production EC2 access?
SSH requires an open inbound port (usually 22) as an attack surface, and key-pair management (rotation, revocation, who has which key) is a manual, error-prone process. Session Manager needs no open inbound port at all, authenticates and authorizes purely through IAM, and logs every session to CloudTrail automatically — closing the attack surface and the audit gap at the same time.
A team says "we enabled CloudWatch monitoring" but didn't catch an out-of-memory crash. What's missing?
Default CloudWatch EC2 metrics cover CPU, network and disk I/O, but not memory or disk-space utilization — those require the CloudWatch agent to be installed and configured on the instance. "Monitoring is on" without the agent still leaves memory pressure completely invisible.
When would you choose Reserved Instances over Savings Plans, given Savings Plans are more flexible?
Savings Plans' flexibility (applying across instance families/sizes/regions in some cases) is also why their discount ceiling is slightly lower than a narrowly-scoped Reserved Instance in some cases. When a workload's instance type and region truly will not change for the commitment period, a Reserved Instance can squeeze out marginally more discount; for anything likely to evolve, that flexibility is usually worth more than the small difference in discount depth.

Level 2 · AWS Fundamentals

VPC & Core Networking

● ~26 min read● Builds on Module 05● ShopSphere: builds the network its servers actually live inside● AWS service

Bottom line: EC2 instances don't float in space — every one of them lives inside a Virtual Private Cloud, a software-defined network you design before a single server launches. Get the CIDR ranges, subnet placement and route tables wrong on day one, and you're re-architecting network addressing under a live production system later — one of the most disruptive retrofits in cloud operations.

01 Problem

Module 5 launched ShopSphere's checkout instance "in a private subnet of ap-south-1's AZ-A" without explaining what a subnet actually is, why it's private, or how traffic gets to it at all. A VM by itself has no network identity beyond what the surrounding network grants it. Before any instance, database or load balancer exists, ShopSphere needs an isolated, addressable network — and a deliberate decision about which parts of that network can be reached from the public internet and which parts never can.

02 Concept

Amazon VPC (Virtual Private Cloud) is a logically isolated, software-defined network within an AWS Region — your own private slice of AWS's network fabric, with full control over IP addressing, subnetting, routing and traffic filtering. It's the networking expression of Module 1's resource-pooling idea: the physical network is shared across every AWS customer, but each VPC is isolated from every other customer's as if it were on its own separate wire.

A VPC is carved up into subnets, each pinned to exactly one Availability Zone, and each subnet's traffic is governed by a route table that answers one question for every packet leaving it: where does this go next?

03 How it works — CIDR, subnets and route tables

A VPC is assigned an IPv4 address range as a CIDR block (Classless Inter-Domain Routing), e.g. 10.0.0.0/16 — the /16 meaning the first 16 bits are fixed, leaving roughly 65,000 addresses to carve up. Each subnet gets a smaller slice of that range, e.g. 10.0.1.0/24 (256 addresses, a handful reserved by AWS). A subnet is public if its route table sends 0.0.0.0/0 (everything not otherwise matched) to an Internet Gateway; it's private if that default route instead points to a NAT Gateway, or has no route to the internet at all.

ShopSphere CIDR planRangeContains
VPC10.0.0.0/16The whole private network, ~65,000 addresses, sized for years of growth
Public subnet — AZ-A10.0.0.0/24ALB nodes only
Public subnet — AZ-B10.0.1.0/24ALB nodes only
Private app subnet — AZ-A10.0.10.0/24Checkout EC2 instances
Private app subnet — AZ-B10.0.11.0/24Checkout EC2 instances
Private data subnet — AZ-A10.0.20.0/24RDS primary
Private data subnet — AZ-B10.0.21.0/24RDS standby

Size the VPC CIDR generously, up front

Shrinking a VPC's CIDR block later is disruptive — in practice it usually means standing up a new VPC and migrating. AWS does let you add secondary CIDR blocks to an existing VPC, but planning a range large enough for every AZ, every subnet tier, and years of new domains (Inventory, Pricing, Promotion…) up front avoids that migration entirely. A /16 costs nothing extra to reserve versus a /20.

04 ShopSphere use case — the network diagram

Notice there's a NAT Gateway per AZ, not one shared NAT Gateway for the whole VPC. A single NAT Gateway in AZ-A would mean every outbound request from AZ-B's private subnet crosses an AZ boundary to reach it — adding a cross-AZ data transfer charge on top of the NAT Gateway's own per-GB charge, and making AZ-B's egress depend on AZ-A staying up. One NAT Gateway per AZ keeps each AZ self-contained for both failure isolation and cost.

05 Why databases and the checkout instance sit in private subnets

A public subnet means "can be given a route to the internet," not "is exposed"

The common beginner mistake is believing a resource needs a public subnet to be reachable by customers at all. It doesn't. ShopSphere's ALB sits in the public subnets and is the single public entry point; the checkout instances and the database sit in private subnets with no public IP and no route via an Internet Gateway, and are reached only through the ALB's internal routing. A direct internet path to a database is pure downside — it adds an attack surface with zero functional benefit, since the database only ever needs to talk to the application tier sitting right next to it in the VPC.

This is Module 3's least-privilege principle applied to topology rather than IAM: don't grant network reachability any resource doesn't need. A compromised public-facing ALB can be contained to the ALB layer; a database with a public IP and an open security group turns the same compromise into a direct data breach.

06 Functional & non-functional requirements

FunctionalEnabled by
Customers reach ShopSphere's checkout over HTTPSPublic subnet + Internet Gateway + ALB
Checkout instances call third-party payment APIs outboundPrivate subnet + NAT Gateway
Checkout instances read/write the databaseRoute table entries within the VPC — no gateway needed, it's all internal
Non-functional dimensionImpact of VPC design
AvailabilitySubnets and NAT Gateways spread across 2+ AZs remove the network itself as a single point of failure
ScalabilityA generously sized CIDR block avoids running out of IPs as fleets grow
SecurityPrivate subnets plus Security Groups/NACLs form the first and second layers of network defense
PerformanceSame-AZ NAT Gateway usage avoids cross-AZ latency and transfer charges
CostNAT Gateway is billed per hour and per GB processed — one of the most underestimated recurring line items in a VPC bill
MaintainabilityA documented, generously sized CIDR plan avoids a painful re-addressing project later

07 Security Groups vs Network ACLs

Both are firewalls inside a VPC, but they operate at different layers and with different statefulness — a distinction that shows up constantly in both real incidents and interviews.

Security GroupNetwork ACL
ScopeAttached to individual ENIs/instancesAttached to a subnet — applies to everything in it
StateStateful — a response to an allowed inbound request is automatically allowed back out, no matching outbound rule neededStateless — inbound and outbound must each be explicitly allowed, including ephemeral return-traffic ports
Rule typesAllow rules onlyAllow and explicit Deny rules
Rule evaluationAll rules evaluated; broadest match wins (effectively, any matching Allow permits it)Rules evaluated in numbered order, first match wins, then stops
DefaultDeny all inbound, allow all outboundAllow all inbound and outbound (default NACL)
ShopSphere usePrimary day-to-day control — e.g. "checkout SG accepts port 443 only from the ALB's SG"Coarse subnet-wide backstop — e.g. explicitly deny a known-bad CIDR range at the subnet boundary

The NACL gotcha that breaks things in practice

Because NACLs are stateless, allowing inbound traffic on port 443 is not enough — the response traffic goes out on a random high-numbered ephemeral port (1024–65535 typically), which must also be explicitly allowed outbound, or the connection silently hangs. Security Groups never have this problem, which is exactly why most teams leave NACLs at their permissive default and do the real enforcement in Security Groups.

08 Elastic IPs & ENIs

An Elastic Network Interface (ENI) is a virtual network card — the thing that actually carries an IP address, MAC address and Security Group attachment, and the "ENI" box in Module 5's EC2 anatomy diagram is this. An Elastic IP is a static, account-owned public IPv4 address you can attach to an ENI and move between instances — useful when something external (a partner's firewall allow-list, a DNS A record pinned to an IP) needs a public address that doesn't change if the underlying instance is replaced. ShopSphere's ALB doesn't need one (AWS manages its public-facing addresses directly), but a NAT Gateway does: each NAT Gateway is assigned an Elastic IP so outbound traffic from the private subnets always leaves from the same known, allow-list-able address.

09 Alternatives & related services

NeedOptionWhen to reach for it instead
Outbound internet from a private subnetNAT Gateway (managed) vs. a self-run NAT instance on EC2NAT Gateway almost always wins: managed, highly available within its AZ, scales automatically; a NAT instance is cheaper at very low, steady volumes but you own its patching, scaling and failover
Private access to an AWS service (S3, DynamoDB) without transiting the internetVPC EndpointAvoids NAT Gateway data-processing charges entirely for that traffic, and keeps it off the public internet — covered in depth in a later networking module
Connecting many VPCs togetherVPC Peering vs. Transit GatewayPeering for a handful of VPCs; Transit Gateway once the mesh gets large — also a later module

10 When NOT to reach for a new VPC

Don't default to "one VPC per service"

Creating a brand-new VPC for every microservice multiplies the number of NAT Gateways, route tables and peering connections you have to manage, and often isn't necessary — subnets within one well-planned VPC, isolated by Security Groups and (later) separate AWS accounts, usually achieve the same isolation with far less networking overhead. Reach for multiple VPCs when you need genuinely separate network blast-radius domains (e.g. one VPC per AWS account, per Module 4's account strategy), not as a default per-service pattern.

11 Trade-offs

A larger number of smaller subnets gives finer-grained blast-radius control and more precise routing, at the cost of more route tables and NACLs to keep consistent. Multiple NAT Gateways (one per AZ) buy AZ-independent egress at roughly double or triple the NAT Gateway hourly charge versus a single shared one. A generously over-sized CIDR block costs nothing in dollars but requires discipline up front — and an under-sized one is one of the few AWS networking mistakes that's genuinely expensive to unwind later.

12 Failure scenarios

FailureWhat happensMitigation
NAT Gateway in AZ-A failsPrivate instances in AZ-A's subnets lose outbound internet access; AZ-B is unaffected if it has its own NAT GatewayOne NAT Gateway per AZ, not a single shared one
Internet Gateway has an issueAll public-subnet inbound/outbound internet traffic in the VPC is affected — it's a single logical construct per VPCIGW is a highly available, AWS-managed construct; the mitigation is designing so a region-level event is handled by DR (Module 25), not by a redundant IGW
Route table misconfigured (missing NAT route)Private instances can't reach the internet at all — package updates and outbound payment API calls fail silentlyInfrastructure as Code (Module 23) with reviewed, versioned route tables instead of manual console edits
Security Group too permissive (0.0.0.0/0 on a sensitive port)Unintended public exposure of an internal resourceLeast-privilege SG rules; AWS Config rules or IAM Access Analyzer for network access can flag overly broad rules
Subnet runs out of free IP addressesNew instances/ENIs in that subnet fail to launchSize subnet CIDR blocks for 3–5x expected peak fleet size, not just current need

13 Security

  • Defense in depth, not either/or: Security Groups for day-to-day least-privilege rules, NACLs as a coarse subnet-level backstop, both working together rather than one replacing the other.
  • No direct public IPs on private-subnet resources — the checkout instance and RDS have none; only the ALB and NAT Gateways face the internet.
  • VPC Flow Logs capture accepted and rejected traffic metadata at the ENI, subnet or VPC level — the network-layer equivalent of CloudTrail, essential for investigating "why couldn't X reach Y" and for GuardDuty's network-based threat detection.
  • Route table reviews as part of change management — a stray 0.0.0.0/0 → igw route accidentally added to a private subnet's route table silently turns it public.

14 Production configuration

DevelopmentProduction
AZ spreadOften 1 AZ, to save cost2–3 AZs, each with its own public, app and data subnet
NAT GatewaysOne shared, or none (use a NAT instance)One per AZ
CIDR planningWhatever the console defaults toDocumented plan sized for years of domain growth
Flow LogsUsually offOn, shipped to CloudWatch Logs or S3 for GuardDuty and audit
ProvisioningManual console clicksInfrastructure as Code (Module 23), reviewed via pull request

15 Monitoring

VPC Flow Logs are the primary signal: per-ENI records of accepted/rejected traffic, queryable via CloudWatch Logs Insights or Athena once shipped to S3. CloudWatch publishes NAT Gateway metrics (BytesOutToDestination, PacketsDropCount, ErrorPortAllocation) — ErrorPortAllocation climbing is a specific, well-known signal that a NAT Gateway is running out of available ports under heavy concurrent-connection load, something worth an alarm on before it causes connection failures. Pair this with the Module 5 CloudWatch agent guidance: network configuration metrics live at the VPC layer, application-level connection failures still need to be instrumented in the app.

16 Cost

NAT Gateway: the classic "surprise" line item

NAT Gateway is billed per hour it exists, plus per GB of data it processes — not a flat fee. A workload that quietly moves a lot of outbound traffic through NAT (large API payloads, chatty third-party SDKs, container image pulls from outside AWS) can rack up a data-processing bill far larger than the hourly charge itself, and because it shows up as one undifferentiated "VPC" line in the bill, it's a frequent source of "where did this cost come from?" investigations. Routing AWS-service traffic (S3, DynamoDB) through a VPC Endpoint instead of out through NAT avoids the NAT Gateway's per-GB charge for that traffic entirely.

Other cost drivers: cross-AZ data transfer (any traffic crossing an AZ boundary, including to a NAT Gateway in a different AZ, is charged separately from same-AZ traffic), and Elastic IPs attached to nothing (an idle, unattached Elastic IP is billed; one actively in use on a running instance or NAT Gateway is not). Always verify current rates on AWS's own VPC pricing page before costing a real design.

17 Hands-on lab

  1. Design a CIDR plan for a VPC that needs 3 AZs, each with a public, private-app and private-data subnet, sized generously for growth. Write out each subnet's CIDR block starting from a 10.1.0.0/16 VPC.
  2. Write the route table entries (destination → target) for one private-app subnet that needs outbound internet access but no inbound internet access, and for one public subnet serving an ALB.
  3. A teammate proposes a single NAT Gateway in AZ-A to "save money" for a VPC spanning 3 AZs. Explain, in terms of both cost and availability, why this is a bad trade for a production workload.

18 Interview questions

What's the single most important difference between a Security Group and a Network ACL, beyond "instance-level vs subnet-level"?
Statefulness. Security Groups automatically allow return traffic for anything they let in, so you only ever write one rule per direction of intent. NACLs are stateless, so you must explicitly allow both the inbound request and the outbound response on its ephemeral port range — forgetting the ephemeral-port outbound rule is the single most common NACL misconfiguration that silently breaks connections.
Why does a production VPC typically have one NAT Gateway per AZ instead of one for the whole VPC?
A single NAT Gateway in one AZ becomes both a cross-AZ cost driver (every other AZ's outbound traffic now crosses an AZ boundary to reach it, incurring data transfer charges) and an availability dependency (if that AZ fails, every other AZ's private subnets lose outbound internet access too, even though their own AZ is healthy). One NAT Gateway per AZ keeps each AZ's egress path self-contained for both cost and fault isolation.
A database needs to be reachable by the application tier. Why wouldn't you just put it in a public subnet to make that simpler?
A public subnet's defining property is a route to an Internet Gateway, which means anything in it can be given a public IP and reached directly from the internet. The database doesn't need that reachability at all — it only ever talks to the application tier sitting in the same VPC, which routes to it over the VPC's internal network regardless of subnet visibility. Putting it in a public subnet adds a real attack surface (a misconfigured Security Group away from direct internet exposure) in exchange for zero functional benefit.

Level 2 · AWS Fundamentals

S3, EBS, EFS — Storage Fundamentals

● ~24 min read● Builds on Module 06● ShopSphere: stores product images, backs its disks, shares its files● AWS service

Bottom line: "storage" is not one thing on AWS — object, block and file storage solve different problems and are not interchangeable. Picking the wrong one doesn't just cost more, it often doesn't work at all: you cannot mount an S3 bucket as a server's boot disk, and you cannot have 50 EC2 instances write to the same EBS volume simultaneously.

01 Problem

ShopSphere's checkout instance (Module 5) needs a disk to boot from. Its product catalog needs to serve millions of product images and videos to shoppers worldwide. Some future background job needs a shared folder that several worker instances write into at once. These are three genuinely different storage problems, and AWS offers a genuinely different service for each — this module is about recognizing which is which before reaching for one by habit.

02 Concept — three storage models, not three competing products

ModelUnit of storageHow it's accessedAWS service
Object storageWhole objects (files) with metadata, in a flat bucket namespaceHTTP(S) API calls — GetObject, PutObject — never mounted as a diskS3
Block storageRaw fixed-size blocks, like a physical hard diskAttached to exactly one EC2 instance at a time, formatted with a filesystem, read/written at the OS levelEBS
File storageFiles in a shared, hierarchical directory treeMounted concurrently by many instances over NFS, with POSIX file-locking semanticsEFS

The dividing question is almost always: does this need to be mounted as a disk, and if so, by how many machines at once? Zero machines (accessed only via API) → object storage. Exactly one machine → block storage. More than one machine, concurrently → file storage.

03 How it works & ShopSphere use case

ShopSphere use caseStorageWhy this one
Product images and videos served to shoppersS3Durable, web-accessible, pay-per-GB, no server needed to serve a static image
Order invoices and seller-uploaded documentsS3Same durability and access pattern as images — write once, read often, from anywhere
Database and application backupsS311 nines durability, lifecycle rules can tier old backups to cheaper storage automatically
Checkout EC2 instance's root/boot volumeEBS (gp3, from Module 05)A boot disk is inherently single-instance — this is the only model that fits
A shared directory a future image-processing worker fleet all read/writeEFSMultiple EC2 instances need the same files visible and writable at once — S3 isn't mountable, EBS isn't shareable

04 S3 storage classes & lifecycle policies

Not every object in S3 is accessed the same way, and S3 Standard's price point assumes frequent access. Storage classes trade retrieval speed/cost for a lower storage price, for data accessed less often.

Storage classAccess pattern it fitsShopSphere example
S3 StandardFrequently accessed, needs millisecond retrievalCurrent catalog's product images, actively served to shoppers
S3 Standard-Infrequent Access (Standard-IA)Accessed less often, still needs millisecond retrieval when accessed; per-GB retrieval feeImages for discontinued products, kept for historical order pages
S3 Glacier Instant RetrievalRarely accessed archive data that still needs millisecond retrievalOlder invoices, accessed instantly but rarely, for compliance lookups
S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval / Deep ArchiveLong-term archive, retrieval measured in minutes to hours, lowest storage costYears-old order and audit records kept only for legal retention

S3 Lifecycle policies automate the movement between classes (and eventual deletion) based on an object's age — e.g. "move to Standard-IA after 90 days of no access, to Glacier Deep Archive after 2 years, delete after 7" — so nobody has to manually reclassify millions of product images by hand as they age out of the active catalog. AWS's S3 Intelligent-Tiering class automates this at the object level based on observed access patterns, trading a small monitoring fee for not having to author lifecycle rules by hand.

05 S3 versioning — why it matters for product images

Versioning, once enabled on a bucket, keeps every previous version of an object instead of overwriting it on a new PutObject — a "delete" becomes a new version marked as a delete marker, recoverable, rather than true data loss. For ShopSphere's product-image bucket, this matters concretely: a seller re-uploading a product photo, a catalog pipeline bug that pushes a corrupted image over the original, or an accidental bulk delete are all recoverable by rolling back to the prior version, instead of being a permanent loss requiring a slow restore from a separate backup.

ShopSphere's decision

Versioning is enabled on shopsphere-product-images, paired with a lifecycle rule that expires non-current (old) versions after 30 days — getting the recoverability benefit without indefinitely paying to store every historical version of every image forever.

06 Functional & non-functional requirements

Non-functional dimensionS3EBSEFS
Durability11 nines, replicated across ≥3 AZs automaticallyReplicated within its single AZ only — snapshot to S3 for cross-AZ durabilityReplicated across multiple AZs by default (for Regional file systems)
AvailabilityDesigned for 99.99% for StandardTied to its single instance's AZ being upMulti-AZ, so an AZ failure doesn't take the file system down
ScalabilityEffectively unlimited, scales automaticallyFixed size per volume, resized manually (can grow, with some limits)Grows and shrinks automatically with stored data, no provisioning
PerformanceGood throughput, higher per-request latency than a local diskLow, consistent latency — closest to local disk performanceHigher latency than EBS due to the network filesystem layer
Cost modelPay per GB stored + requests + retrieval (class-dependent)Pay per GB provisioned, whether used or notPay per GB actually stored, no provisioning

07 Alternatives

NeedFirst choiceConsider instead when
Serve static assets globally at low latencyS3Pair with CloudFront (a later module) once global shoppers need edge caching, rather than everyone hitting the bucket's home Region directly
High-performance, specialized workloads (HPC, Windows file shares, high-throughput ML training data)EFS for general shared POSIX accessFSx (for Lustre, Windows File Server, NetApp ONTAP) when the workload needs a specific, specialized filesystem feature set EFS doesn't provide — covered later
A database's own storageDon't manage EBS directly for thisRDS/Aurora manage their own underlying storage layer for you (Module 08) — reaching for raw EBS here reintroduces the self-managed operational burden Module 1 warned about

08 When NOT to use each

Common misuses

• S3 as a database. No transactions, no secondary indexes, no row-level locking — it's an object store, not a query engine. Don't build the Order domain's write path on it.
• EBS as "shared storage." An EBS volume attaches to exactly one instance at a time (multi-attach exists only for specific clustered-filesystem scenarios on specific volume types, and is the exception, not the pattern to reach for by default). Trying to share a plain EBS volume across a fleet is the wrong tool for that job — that's what EFS is for.
• EFS for a single instance's boot/root volume. It's a network filesystem with higher latency than local block storage; using it where EBS fits adds cost and latency for no benefit.
• S3 Glacier Deep Archive for anything needing fast reads. Retrieval can take hours — fine for compliance archives, unusable for anything a customer-facing request path touches.

09 Trade-offs

S3 trades per-request latency (tens of milliseconds, not sub-millisecond) for effectively unlimited scale, 11-nines durability and zero capacity planning. EBS trades the need to provision a specific size and type upfront (and pay for that provisioned size whether it's full or not) for the lowest, most predictable latency of the three. EFS trades EBS's raw performance for the operational simplicity of "many instances, one shared view of the files," and its pay-for-what's-stored model avoids EBS's "pay for what you provisioned" waste at the cost of generally higher per-operation latency.

10 Failure scenarios

FailureWhat happensMitigation
EBS volume's AZ failsThe volume is unreachable along with its instance — EBS does not replicate across AZsRegular snapshots to S3 (which is cross-AZ durable); stateless app design so the instance is simply replaced
S3 bucket accidentally made publicData exposure — the single most common real-world S3 incident (Module 3's shared-responsibility point, concretely)S3 Block Public Access at the account/bucket level, bucket policy review, access logging
Object overwritten or deleted by a bad deployWithout versioning, the previous object is gone permanentlyVersioning enabled, as in ShopSphere's product-images bucket
EFS mount target's AZ failsInstances in that AZ lose access to the file system; instances in other AZs with their own mount target are unaffectedA mount target provisioned in every AZ that has instances using the file system

11 Security

  • S3 Block Public Access enabled at the account level by default on new accounts — a deliberate, explicit action is required to ever make a bucket or object public, closing off the single most common S3 misconfiguration.
  • Encryption at rest on all three: S3 server-side encryption (SSE-S3 by default, or SSE-KMS for customer-managed keys), EBS encryption via KMS (and once a volume is created unencrypted, it cannot be encrypted in place — only via a new encrypted snapshot/volume, which is why "encrypt by default" is a day-one account setting worth turning on), EFS encryption at rest via KMS.
  • IAM policies and S3 bucket policies scope exactly which principals can read/write which prefixes — the checkout service's IAM role from Module 5 is scoped to one bucket, not s3:* on every bucket in the account.
  • EFS access points enforce a specific POSIX user/group and a root directory per application, so multiple services sharing one file system can't see each other's files even though they mount the same system.
  • VPC Endpoints for S3 (Module 06's alternatives list) keep S3 traffic off the public internet path entirely when accessed from within the VPC.

12 Production configuration

DevelopmentProduction
S3 versioningOften offOn for anything irreplaceable, paired with a lifecycle rule to expire old versions
S3 lifecycle rulesNone — everything stays in StandardTiering rules by access age, tuned per bucket's actual access pattern
EBS typegp3, default settingsgp3 or io2 sized and IOPS-provisioned to the workload, with snapshot schedules via Data Lifecycle Manager
EFS mount targetsOne AZOne per AZ with instances that need it
EncryptionSometimes skippedEncryption at rest on by default for all three, account-wide where possible

13 Monitoring

S3: CloudWatch request metrics (4xxErrors, 5xxErrors, BytesDownloaded) and S3 Storage Lens for account-wide usage and cost patterns across buckets. EBS: VolumeReadOps/VolumeWriteOps, and specifically BurstBalance for gp2 volumes (irrelevant for gp3, which has no burst bucket) — a gp2 volume with BurstBalance trending toward zero is about to get throttled. EFS: PercentIOLimit and throughput/latency metrics, important for catching a file system approaching its throughput mode's ceiling before application-level timeouts start.

14 Cost

The provisioned-vs-consumed gap

EBS bills for what you provision, not what you use — a 100 GiB volume with 10 GiB of actual data still costs the full 100 GiB rate. S3 and EFS both bill only for what's actually stored. This is why over-provisioning an EBS volume "to be safe" is a quiet, recurring waste that doesn't show up until someone audits actual disk utilization versus allocated size.

Other major drivers: S3 request pricing (a high-request-rate, low-object-size workload can rack up request charges that dwarf the storage charge itself), S3 data transfer out to the internet (mitigated later by routing through CloudFront, which has its own, often cheaper, egress pricing), and EFS's optional provisioned-throughput mode, which costs more than the default bursting mode and should only be used when the workload's throughput need is genuinely sustained rather than spiky. Always check AWS's current pricing pages before costing a real design — these rates change.

15 Hands-on lab

  1. For each of the following, name the right storage model (S3, EBS or EFS) and justify it in one sentence: (a) a nightly database backup, (b) the root volume of a new EC2 instance, (c) a configuration directory that 6 worker instances all need to read and occasionally write at once, (d) customer-uploaded return-request photos.
  2. Design a lifecycle policy in plain English for ShopSphere's product-images bucket: Standard for how long, Standard-IA for how long, and when (if ever) it should move to a Glacier tier or expire.
  3. A teammate provisions a 500 GiB gp3 EBS volume for a service that currently uses 40 GiB and grows slowly. Explain what this costs versus right-sizing, and what you'd recommend instead (including how to grow it later without downtime).

16 Interview questions

Why can't you just use S3 as a shared drive for a fleet of EC2 instances that need POSIX file semantics?
S3 is an object store accessed via an HTTP API — it has no mount, no file-locking, no partial-write/append semantics, and no directory hierarchy in the traditional filesystem sense (prefixes only simulate one). An application expecting to open(), lock, and append to a file the way a real filesystem behaves will not work correctly against S3. EFS provides exactly that POSIX-compliant, concurrently-mountable semantics over NFS, which is the actual requirement here.
What's the practical difference between EBS durability and S3 durability, and why does it matter for backup strategy?
EBS replicates within a single AZ only, so an AZ-level failure can take a volume down with it. S3 Standard replicates across a minimum of three AZs automatically. That's exactly why EBS snapshots are stored in S3 rather than treated as sufficient on their own — snapshotting moves the durability guarantee from "survives a disk failure" to "survives an AZ failure," which is the level a real backup strategy needs.
A bucket of old order invoices is rarely read but must be retained for 7 years for compliance. What's the cost-optimal storage design?
A lifecycle policy that starts objects in S3 Standard briefly (or skips straight past it), transitions to S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval or Flexible Retrieval once the access pattern drops off, and moves to Glacier Deep Archive well before the 7-year mark for the lowest per-GB cost, since compliance retrieval is rare and tolerant of a multi-hour retrieval delay. The mistake to avoid is leaving 7 years of invoices in S3 Standard by default and paying the frequent-access price for data nobody is frequently accessing.

AWS · officialWhat is Amazon Elastic File System?Concurrent multi-instance mounting, performance and throughput modes.docs.aws.amazon.com ↗
