Level 2 · AWS Fundamentals

RDS, Aurora, DynamoDB — Database Fundamentals

● ~25 min read● Builds on Module 07● ShopSphere: makes its first production database decision● AWS service

Bottom line: ShopSphere's Order domain and its Cart domain have genuinely different data needs, and this module is about recognizing that difference early rather than forcing one database model onto both. This is a first pass — the full theory (ACID/BASE/CAP, sharding, partition design) gets its own deep dive in Module 13; here the goal is simply knowing what RDS, Aurora and DynamoDB each are and which one a given ShopSphere domain should reach for.

01 Problem

An order touches money: a customer is charged, inventory is decremented, a shipment is scheduled. If any step partially fails — the charge succeeds but the order row never commits — ShopSphere has either taken money with no order to show for it, or created an order nobody paid for. The Cart domain has the opposite problem: a cart is read and written constantly, by every active shopper, usually touched by its owner alone, with no cross-record transactional requirement at all. These two domains should not reach for the same kind of database by default.

02 Concept — why Orders needs a relational database

A relational database stores data in tables with defined schemas and enforces ACID transactions — Atomicity (all-or-nothing), Consistency (never left in an invalid state), Isolation (concurrent transactions don't corrupt each other), Durability (once committed, it survives a crash). Placing an order and charging a payment method are the textbook case for this: either the order row, the payment record and the inventory decrement all commit together, or none of them do. A relational engine (MySQL, PostgreSQL, and the engines they're compatible with) gives you that guarantee natively, inside a single transaction, with decades of mature tooling and query flexibility (joins, ad-hoc reporting queries, foreign-key integrity) around it.

ShopSphere's decision

The Order domain's primary store is a relational database. Orders, order line items and their payment references live in normalized tables, written inside transactions, so "charged but no order" or "order but no charge" are structurally prevented rather than something application code has to work around after the fact.

03 How it works — RDS vs. self-managed MySQL on EC2

Amazon RDS (Relational Database Service) runs a standard database engine (MySQL, PostgreSQL, MariaDB, SQL Server, Oracle) on AWS-managed infrastructure: AWS handles patching, automated backups, and point-in-time restore, and offers Multi-AZ failover as a configuration, not something you build. This is Module 1's managed-services trade-off, made concrete for the very first time with a stateful service: you give up low-level control over the exact host and storage tuning, in exchange for not operating a database server yourself.

Self-managed MySQL on EC2Amazon RDS
Patching the OS and DB engineYou, manually, on a schedule you ownAWS, in a maintenance window you configure
BackupsYou script and verify them yourselfAutomated, with point-in-time restore built in
Failover to a standbyYou build and test the replication/failover logicA Multi-AZ configuration flag away
Root/OS-level accessFull accessNo OS access — only through the database engine itself
Where this makes senseA very specific engine version, extension or OS-level tuning RDS doesn't exposeThe default choice for the large majority of relational workloads

04 What Aurora adds over RDS

Amazon Aurora is a MySQL- and PostgreSQL-compatible relational engine built by AWS with its own distributed, log-structured storage layer, decoupled from compute — rather than running stock MySQL/PostgreSQL on top of standard EBS the way RDS for MySQL/PostgreSQL does. The practical differences: storage that automatically scales up to very large sizes without manual resizing, replication to replicas that's faster and lower-overhead than standard binlog-based replication (because Aurora replicates at the storage layer, not by re-applying logical statements), and generally higher maximum throughput ceilings than RDS running the same engine on comparable compute. Aurora exposes the same MySQL/PostgreSQL wire protocol and SQL dialect, so application code doesn't need to change to adopt it.

When the extra cost is worth it

Aurora typically costs more per hour of compute than the equivalent RDS instance size. It earns that premium when a workload is approaching RDS's practical ceilings on throughput, storage auto-scaling, or replica lag — or when the faster, storage-layer replication meaningfully improves read-replica freshness for a read-heavy workload. A new, modest-traffic domain (ShopSphere's Order domain at launch) does not need Aurora yet; it's the right migration once Order volume genuinely stresses standard RDS's limits, not a default starting point.

05 A first look at DynamoDB — why Cart might not want a relational database

DynamoDB is a fully managed NoSQL key-value database, designed for single-digit-millisecond latency at effectively any scale, with no servers to size or patch at all — it's serverless in the same sense Lambda is (Module 1's abstraction spectrum, pushed to its far end for a database). Every item is looked up by a key, not joined across tables; there's no schema enforcement beyond the key structure, and no cross-item ACID transaction by default (DynamoDB does support limited multi-item transactions, but that's not its primary access pattern).

ShopSphere's shopping cart is read and written on nearly every page view by its owner, keyed naturally by a customer or session ID, with no need to join against other customers' carts or run ad-hoc relational queries across the whole table. That access pattern — simple key-based lookups, high read/write volume, low per-item latency requirement — is exactly what a key-value store is built for, and is a much more natural fit than forcing cart reads through a relational engine tuned for joins and multi-table transactions it will never use here.

This is a first look, not the full picture

Real DynamoDB usage lives and dies by partition key design, access-pattern modeling, and understanding eventual vs. strong consistency reads — none of which this module covers yet. Treat "DynamoDB is a candidate for Cart" as a directional decision to revisit with full rigor in Module 13, not a finished design.

06 Multi-AZ RDS vs. read replicas — the confusion to clear up now

The point people get wrong constantly

Multi-AZ gives you synchronous replication to a standby in a second AZ that cannot serve read traffic and exists solely so RDS can automatically fail over to it (typically within roughly a minute or two) if the primary fails — it is a high-availability feature, not a read-scaling feature. A read replica is a separate, independently readable copy kept up to date via asynchronous replication, built specifically to offload read traffic from the primary — and it does not fail over automatically; promoting one to a standalone writable instance is a manual (or manually-triggered) action. Wanting "more read capacity" calls for a read replica. Wanting "survive losing an AZ without a human intervening" calls for Multi-AZ. Many production designs use both at once, for different reasons.

Multi-AZ deploymentRead replica
PurposeHigh availability / automatic failoverRead scaling
ReplicationSynchronousAsynchronous
Can serve read queries?No (the standard one-standby model)Yes — that's its entire purpose
FailoverAutomaticManual promotion only
Can span Regions?No — AZs within one RegionYes — cross-Region read replicas are supported

07 Functional & non-functional requirements

FunctionalEnabled by
Place an order with a guaranteed-consistent charge + order recordRDS/Aurora transactions (Order domain)
Read and update a shopping cart on nearly every page load, at low latencyDynamoDB's key-value access pattern (Cart domain, candidate)
Survive an AZ failure without the Order domain going downRDS/Aurora Multi-AZ
Non-functional dimensionImpact
AvailabilityMulti-AZ turns a single instance into an automatically-failing-over pair; without it, a primary failure is an outage until manual recovery
ScalabilityRelational scales reads via replicas and writes by scaling the primary instance up; DynamoDB scales both reads and writes near-linearly with partitioning, largely hands-off
PerformanceDynamoDB's single-digit-ms key lookups beat a relational engine on that specific access pattern; relational wins decisively on complex multi-table queries DynamoDB can't do at all
ReliabilityACID transactions make the Order write path provably consistent; a key-value model pushes more correctness responsibility onto application-level design
MaintainabilityRDS/Aurora need schema migrations managed carefully; DynamoDB's schemaless item model avoids migrations but demands careful access-pattern planning upfront instead
CostRDS/Aurora bill mostly for provisioned compute+storage; DynamoDB (on-demand mode) bills per request, which can be cheaper at spiky, low-average load and costlier at sustained, very high throughput

08 Alternatives

RDSAuroraDynamoDB
Data modelRelationalRelational (MySQL/PostgreSQL-compatible)Key-value / NoSQL
You manage the engine config?Mostly managed, standard engineMostly managed, AWS-built storage layerFully managed, no servers at all
Scaling modelVertical (instance size) + read replicasStorage auto-scales; compute sized like RDS; faster replicasHorizontal, near-automatic, partition-based
Best fitStandard relational workloads at moderate-to-high scaleRelational workloads pushing RDS's throughput/replica-lag ceilingsHigh-volume, simple-access-pattern, latency-sensitive workloads

09 When NOT to use each

Don't reach for…

• DynamoDB for Orders. Orders need multi-table consistency and ad-hoc reporting queries that a key-value model actively fights against — forcing it in means re-implementing joins and transactional integrity in application code that the relational engine would give you for free.
• RDS/Aurora for Cart at ShopSphere's intended scale. Millions of simple, high-frequency key lookups against a relational engine means paying for (and operationally managing) far more compute than the access pattern actually needs, just to do something a key-value store does natively and cheaply.
• Aurora as a default "better RDS." It costs more; adopt it when a specific RDS ceiling (throughput, storage scaling, replica lag) is the actual problem, not preemptively.

10 Trade-offs

Relational databases trade horizontal scalability for strong consistency and query flexibility — scaling writes beyond one primary instance is hard (sharding a relational database is a real engineering project, covered in Module 13). DynamoDB trades query flexibility (no joins, no ad-hoc WHERE clauses across non-key attributes without a secondary index designed in advance) for near-linear horizontal scalability and operational simplicity. Aurora trades a cost premium over RDS for higher ceilings and AWS-managed storage resilience. None of these is strictly "better" — each is the right trade for a specific access pattern, which is why ShopSphere ends up using more than one.

11 Failure scenarios

FailureWhat happensMitigation
RDS primary instance fails, no Multi-AZ configuredOrder domain is down until a manual restore/recovery completesMulti-AZ enabled, giving automatic failover in roughly a minute or two
Read replica falls behind under heavy write loadReads from the replica can return stale data — a real eventual-consistency risk, not a theoretical oneMonitor replica lag; route latency-sensitive or just-written reads to the primary instead
Someone assumes Multi-AZ means "more read capacity"They point read traffic at infrastructure that isn't built to serve it (the standard one-standby model doesn't serve reads at all)The Module 06 distinction above — Multi-AZ for HA, read replicas for read scaling, don't conflate them
A DynamoDB access pattern wasn't designed for a given query up frontThat query either requires an expensive table scan or isn't possible at all without a new secondary indexAccess-pattern-first design before the table is built — the central discipline Module 13 covers in depth

12 Security

  • Database credentials via Secrets Manager, not hardcoded connection strings — with automatic rotation, extending Module 3's "no embedded long-term credentials" principle to database passwords.
  • Private subnet placement for RDS/Aurora (Module 6) — the database has no public IP and is reachable only from the application tier's security group.
  • Encryption at rest via KMS for RDS/Aurora storage and snapshots; DynamoDB encrypts at rest by default with no configuration needed.
  • IAM database authentication (RDS/Aurora) as an alternative to password-based auth for human/application access, issuing short-lived tokens instead of long-lived passwords.
  • Fine-grained IAM policies scoped to specific DynamoDB tables and actions, the same least-privilege principle from Module 3 applied to NoSQL access.

13 Production configuration

DevelopmentProduction
RDS/Aurora Multi-AZOff — single instance, to save costOn, for any domain where downtime has a real business cost
Read replicasNoneSized to the actual read:write ratio of the workload
BackupsDefault automated backup window, short retentionRetention tuned to the domain's RPO (Module 25), tested restores
DynamoDB capacity modeOn-demand, for simplicityOn-demand for spiky/unpredictable load, or provisioned with auto-scaling for steady, well-understood load
CredentialsPlaintext in a local config fileSecrets Manager with rotation

14 Monitoring

RDS/Aurora: CloudWatch's CPUUtilization, FreeableMemory, DatabaseConnections, and critically ReplicaLag for any read replica — a climbing ReplicaLag is the earliest warning that read-after-write consistency is about to become visibly wrong to users. Performance Insights adds query-level visibility into exactly what's consuming database load. DynamoDB: ConsumedReadCapacityUnits/ConsumedWriteCapacityUnits (or request-unit equivalents in on-demand mode), and ThrottledRequests — a nonzero throttle rate means the table's capacity (or a hot partition within it) is undersized for its current traffic.

15 Cost

RDS/Aurora bill primarily for provisioned compute (instance hours) and storage (plus I/O, for some storage configurations), largely independent of how busy the database actually is — an oversized instance class is a quiet, recurring waste in the same shape as EBS over-provisioning from Module 7. Aurora's premium over equivalent RDS compute is real and should be justified by an actual throughput/scaling need, not assumed by default. DynamoDB's on-demand mode bills per million read/write request units with no capacity to size, which is simple and often cheap at variable load, but can cost more than a well-tuned provisioned-capacity table at very high, steady, predictable throughput — worth revisiting once a table's traffic pattern is well understood. Verify current rates on AWS's own pricing pages before costing a real design.

16 Hands-on lab

  1. For each of ShopSphere's domains — Order, Cart, Product Catalog metadata, Customer profile — state whether a relational database or DynamoDB is the better first-pass fit, and justify each in one sentence using this module's access-pattern framework.
  2. Write, in plain English, the sequence of a single transaction that places an order: which tables/rows change, and why all of them must succeed or all must roll back together.
  3. A teammate configures a read replica for the Order database and says "great, now we're highly available." Explain precisely what is and isn't true about that statement, and what they'd need to add to actually get automatic failover.

17 Interview questions

What's the single biggest misconception about RDS Multi-AZ that you'd want to correct in a design review?
That it provides read scaling. In the standard Multi-AZ configuration, the standby instance exists purely for automatic failover and does not serve read traffic at all while healthy. Someone pointing read queries at "the Multi-AZ standby" to relieve primary load is targeting infrastructure that isn't built to serve that traffic — read scaling requires a separate read replica, which is a different feature with asynchronous (not synchronous) replication.
Why might ShopSphere's Cart domain be a poor fit for a relational database even though the company already runs one for Orders?
Cart's access pattern is simple key-based lookups (get/update one customer's cart) at very high frequency and low per-item latency requirement, with no cross-record transactional need and no relational querying across carts. A relational engine's strengths — joins, multi-table ACID transactions, flexible ad-hoc querying — are irrelevant to that pattern, while its cost is real: more compute needed to serve the same volume of simple key lookups than a key-value store built specifically for that shape of traffic.
When would you justify moving ShopSphere's Order database from RDS to Aurora?
When RDS is demonstrably approaching one of its practical ceilings for this workload — sustained throughput near the instance class's limits, storage scaling friction, or read-replica lag that's becoming operationally painful — and the cost premium of Aurora's AWS-built storage layer and faster replication is cheaper than the alternative of re-architecting around RDS's limits. It's a reactive scaling decision tied to measured need, not a default "upgrade" made preemptively at launch.

Level 3 · Application Architecture

Compute Deep Dive: EC2 vs ECS vs EKS vs Lambda

● ~28 min read● Builds on Module 08● ShopSphere: assigns the right compute to every domain

Bottom line: "which compute service is best" is the wrong question — there is no best, only a best fit per workload. ShopSphere doesn't run on one compute service; it runs on three or four at once, each domain landing on whichever point on Module 1's bare-metal→serverless spectrum its own latency, statefulness, traffic shape and team-operational-appetite demand.

01 Problem

Module 05 built ShopSphere's checkout service on EC2 so every moving part — OS, disk, network interface, firewall — was explicit. That was deliberate pedagogy, not a production recommendation. A real ShopSphere runs Notification as short bursty event handlers, Checkout as a steady, connection-heavy service, Search as a containerized stateless API, and Recommendation batch jobs on a schedule — and picking one compute model for all four either over-operates the simple ones or under-serves the demanding ones.

02 The decision framework, applied to compute

This academy's standing decision framework (requirement → workload characteristics → consistency/latency needs → scale → availability → operational complexity → security → cost → service selection) resolves to a small number of questions that actually separate these four options:

QuestionPulls toward
Does a single unit of work ever need to run longer than 15 minutes?EC2 / ECS / EKS (Lambda's hard ceiling is 15 min for standard synchronous/async invocations)
Does the client hold a long-lived connection (WebSocket, persistent gRPC stream, SSE)?EC2 / ECS / EKS — Lambda's per-invocation model fits request/response, not long-held connections
Is traffic spiky with long idle gaps, or genuinely zero at times?Lambda — you stop paying entirely at zero traffic; a container fleet still bills its floor
Is p99 latency on the first request after idle time a hard constraint?EC2 / ECS / EKS (always-warm) over Lambda (cold starts, mitigable but not eliminated)
Does the team already run Kubernetes elsewhere, or need multi-cloud portability?EKS; otherwise this pull doesn't exist and EKS's extra operational cost buys nothing
Is the unit of deployment naturally a container image already in a registry?ECS / EKS over rewriting as discrete functions

03 Lambda: when it genuinely beats containers — and when it doesn't

Lambda wins when work is short, stateless, and event-triggered: resizing an uploaded product image, sending an order-confirmation email off an SQS message, validating a webhook from a payment provider, running a nightly reconciliation job. You pay per invocation and per millisecond of execution, scale from zero to thousands of concurrent executions without touching an Auto Scaling policy, and never patch an OS.

Lambda loses in three recurring ShopSphere situations:

Where Lambda stops being the right default

• Long-running work. Standard Lambda invocations are capped at 15 minutes. A bulk catalog re-index or a large recommendation-model batch job that runs for 40 minutes either gets force-split across invocations (added complexity) or belongs on a container/EC2 task instead. (As of September 2026 AWS introduced Lambda Managed Instances, which support up to 90-minute timeouts for async/event-source-mapping invocations by running multiple concurrent requests per instance — worth checking as this capability matures, but it changes Lambda's execution model closer to a managed container, not a free extension of the classic function model.)
• Long-lived connections. ShopSphere's real-time order-tracking updates over WebSocket, or a persistent connection to a legacy payment gateway, don't fit a model built around a bounded request/response invocation.
• Cold-start-sensitive synchronous paths. Checkout's payment-authorization call is exactly the kind of path where an occasional few-hundred-millisecond cold start (worse inside a VPC, or with a large deployment package/runtime) is unacceptable at p99 — provisioned concurrency can mask this, but at that point you're paying to keep something "always on," which erodes Lambda's core scale-to-zero cost advantage.

ShopSphere decision — Notification service

Notification (order-confirmation emails, SMS, push) is ShopSphere's canonical Lambda workload: triggered by an SQS/EventBridge message, runs for a few hundred milliseconds, fires thousands of times during a flash sale and near-zero times overnight. It is covered service-by-service in Module 21 (Serverless Architecture); here it's the shared example used to cost all four options against each other in Section 07.

04 ECS vs EKS: operational complexity vs control

Both are container orchestrators — they decide which container runs on which compute, restart failed tasks, and route traffic to healthy ones. They answer the same problem with a different amount of exposed machinery.

ECSEKS
Control planeAWS-proprietary, fully managed, freeManaged Kubernetes control plane, billed per cluster-hour
API & conceptsAWS-native: Task Definitions, Services, Clusters — a smaller surfaceFull Kubernetes API: Pods, Deployments, Services, Ingress, CRDs — much larger surface
EcosystemDeep native integration with IAM, CloudWatch, VPC, App MeshThe entire Kubernetes ecosystem — Helm charts, operators, service meshes, portable across clouds
PortabilityAWS-only; migrating off means re-platformingRuns anywhere Kubernetes runs (on-prem, other clouds) — the explicit reason teams choose it
Operational overheadLow — "the fastest path to running containers on AWS"Real: upgrade cadence, add-on management, a genuinely bigger learning curve for the team
Typical fit (AWS's own framing)Microservices, batch jobs, straightforward app migrationTeams already standardized on Kubernetes, ML pipelines needing k8s-native tooling, multi-cloud/hybrid requirements

The honest trade-off, stated plainly

EKS is not "ECS but more powerful." It's the industry-standard orchestration API, which is exactly why it's also the one with the most moving parts to operate well — node group upgrades, add-on version compatibility, IAM-to-Kubernetes-RBAC mapping, and a steeper on-call learning curve. Choosing EKS when nothing requires Kubernetes specifically (no multi-cloud mandate, no existing k8s investment, no third-party tool that only ships a Helm chart) is paying real, ongoing operational tax for optionality the team may never use.

05 Fargate — the serverless compute layer under both

ECS and EKS answer "how are my containers orchestrated," not "what do they physically run on." Both support two launch types: EC2 (you provision and patch the underlying instances, the orchestrator just schedules containers onto them) and Fargate (AWS runs the containers on infrastructure you never see, sized by exactly the vCPU/memory the task definition declares). Fargate is what turns "containers" from a server-management problem back into a serverless one — closer to Lambda's operational profile, but still addressable as a long-running container with no 15-minute ceiling and full support for persistent connections.

ShopSphere's pattern

Search and Recommendation — stateless APIs behind ShopSphere's ALB, running steadily but scaling with traffic — run as ECS Services on Fargate: container images from Module 20's pipeline, zero EC2 instances to patch, Service Auto Scaling reacting to request count per target. The EC2 launch type is reserved for workloads needing something Fargate doesn't expose (GPU instance types for a specific ML inference container, or very large persistent local caches) — a narrowing, not a default.

06 The quadrant: operational control vs scaling automation

07 Worked cost comparison — the same workload, four ways

Take ShopSphere's Notification service: assume, for illustration only, a steady-state average of 150 messages/second with bursts to 3,000/second during a flash sale, each invocation doing ~150ms of real work at a 256 MB memory footprint. Verify current AWS pricing before costing a real design — the ordering below is the durable lesson, the exact dollar figures are not.

OptionWhat you pay forAt this traffic shape
LambdaPer-invocation charge + GB-seconds of actual execution, nothing at idleLowest cost at steady-state; cost scales almost perfectly with real usage — the flash-sale burst costs more in absolute terms but doesn't require pre-provisioning for it
ECS/EKS on FargatevCPU-seconds + GB-seconds for however many tasks are running, billed down to the secondHigher than Lambda at low/idle traffic (a minimum task count is usually kept warm to avoid cold starts on a queue consumer); closes the gap as sustained throughput rises, since Fargate's per-second-while-running rate undercuts Lambda's per-invocation overhead at high, steady volume
ECS/EKS on EC2The EC2 instances in the cluster, 24/7, regardless of how busy they are, plus the orchestration being free (ECS) or a per-cluster-hour fee (EKS)Cheapest per unit of compute if utilization is kept high across many services sharing the fleet; actively expensive if this fleet is sized for the flash-sale peak and sits half-idle the rest of the month
EC2, self-managed (no orchestrator)Same instance cost as above, plus the fully-loaded engineering time to build what ECS/EKS give you for free: health checking, rolling deploys, bin-packingRarely the cheapest option once operational time is counted — included here mainly to show it's dominated by ECS/EKS-on-EC2 for any containerized workload

ShopSphere's actual answer for Notification

Lambda, triggered directly off the SQS queue that Module 10 (Messaging) fans order events into. The workload is exactly Lambda's sweet spot — short, stateless, spiky, no long-lived connection — and the cost model rewards that fit directly instead of paying for idle capacity between flash sales.

08 ShopSphere's full compute map

DomainCompute choiceWhy
CheckoutEC2 (Module 05), migrating to ECS on Fargate as the system containerizes (Module 19)Steady load, needs predictable latency; the EC2 build is this academy's teaching vehicle before Module 19's microservices migration
Search, RecommendationECS Services on FargateStateless containerized APIs behind the ALB, scaling with request volume, no OS to patch
NotificationLambdaShort, event-triggered, spiky — the textbook serverless fit, costed above
Image/catalog batch processingLambda for <15 min jobs; AWS Batch/ECS tasks on Fargate for longer bulk re-indexingSplit by the one hard constraint Lambda can't negotiate away: execution time
A future ML training/inference pipeline needing GPU + existing k8s toolingEKS, likely with EC2 GPU node groupsThe one domain where Kubernetes's ecosystem (training operators, GPU scheduling) earns its operational cost

09 Failure scenarios across the four

FailureEC2ECS/EKSLambda
Sudden 20x traffic spikeManual/ASG scale-out, minutes of lagService Auto Scaling adds tasks in under a minute (Fargate) to minutes (EC2 launch type, bound by node capacity)Scales near-instantly up to the account's concurrency limit, then throttles (429) past it
A dependency (database) becomes slowThreads/connections pile up on the instance until it falls overSame risk per task, contained to that service's tasksConcurrent invocations pile up against the account/function concurrency limit, which can itself starve other functions sharing the account
Underlying AWS infrastructure issueInstance-level; AZ-scoped if spread correctlyOrchestrator reschedules onto healthy capacity automaticallyLambda's control plane is itself a regional service — a regional Lambda incident affects every function in the region, which is the trade-off for not managing your own fleet

10 Security across the four

The IAM-role-not-access-keys pattern from Module 03 is identical everywhere compute runs: an EC2 instance role, an ECS task role, an EKS pod's IAM Roles for Service Accounts (IRSA), and a Lambda execution role all deliver the same temporary, auto-rotated credentials via STS. What changes is the network/OS boundary: EC2 exposes a guest OS you must patch and firewall (Security Groups); Fargate and Lambda remove the OS from your responsibility entirely, narrowing your security surface to the container image/function code and its IAM policy; EKS additionally layers Kubernetes RBAC on top of IAM, and a misconfigured RBAC binding is a real, EKS-specific risk the other three don't have.

11 When NOT to use each

OptionWrong default when…
EC2The workload is containerized already, or needs to scale/shrink faster than a boot cycle allows
ECSThe team needs Kubernetes-ecosystem tooling specifically, or multi-cloud portability is a real requirement, not a hypothetical one
EKSNothing requires Kubernetes and the team would otherwise run ECS — the extra control plane cost and operational surface buys nothing
LambdaExecution regularly exceeds ~10–15 minutes, the workload holds a long-lived connection, or the function needs to stay provisioned-warm so often that it's effectively a container anyway

12 Production configuration

DevelopmentProduction
ContainersSingle ECS task, no autoscalingService Auto Scaling with min≥2 tasks across ≥2 AZs, target-tracking on request count or CPU
LambdaDefault concurrency, no reserved/provisioned concurrencyReserved concurrency to protect shared account limits from one noisy function; provisioned concurrency only on genuinely cold-start-sensitive paths
EKSSingle node group, manual upgradesMultiple node groups (or Karpenter/EKS Auto Mode) across AZs, a tested upgrade runbook, IRSA scoped per workload

13 Hands-on lab

  1. For each of ShopSphere's 15 backend domains listed on this academy's home page, pick EC2, ECS, EKS or Lambda and justify it in one sentence using Section 02's questions — don't default to "whatever Checkout uses."
  2. Recommendation's nightly batch job currently runs 22 minutes on a single large instance. Design two ways to make it Lambda-compatible (splitting the job, or accepting Fargate/Batch instead), and state which you'd actually ship and why.
  3. Redo Section 07's cost comparison assuming Notification's traffic is flat at 500 msg/sec, 24/7, with no flash-sale bursts at all. Does the ranking change? Explain why or why not.

14 Interview questions

A candidate says "we should use EKS because Kubernetes is the industry standard." How do you respond?
Push back on the premise: "industry standard" is a reason Kubernetes exists, not an automatic reason this workload needs it. Ask what specifically requires it — multi-cloud portability, an existing Helm-chart-only tool, team expertise already in k8s. If the honest answer is "nothing, it's just well-known," ECS delivers the same orchestration outcome with meaningfully less operational surface, and that's the stronger recommendation.
Why can Lambda be cheaper than Fargate at low traffic but more expensive at high sustained traffic?
Lambda's per-invocation pricing includes a fixed request charge on top of GB-seconds, which is negligible at low volume but accumulates linearly with request count. Fargate bills purely for vCPU/memory-seconds while a task runs; at high sustained throughput, a small warm fleet processing continuously becomes cheaper per unit of work than paying Lambda's per-request overhead millions of times over. The crossover point depends on request rate, duration and memory size, which is exactly why "it depends, model it" beats a rule of thumb.
What's the single operational cost of EKS that a team moving from ECS consistently underestimates?
Kubernetes version upgrades. EKS control-plane and node versions have a support window and must be upgraded on a cadence; each upgrade can break add-ons, custom controllers or workloads relying on deprecated APIs. Teams coming from ECS (which has no analogous version-upgrade treadmill) routinely underbudget the recurring engineering time this requires.

Level 3 · Application Architecture

Elastic Load Balancing & Auto Scaling

● ~26 min read● Builds on Module 09● ShopSphere: survives a flash sale without a human touching a console● AWS service

Bottom line: a load balancer and an Auto Scaling Group are almost always designed together, not separately — the load balancer's target group is the thing the ASG actually scales, and a load balancer with nothing elastic behind it just moves the single-point-of-failure problem one hop downstream.

01 Problem

Module 05's checkout instance is a single point of failure, and it can't absorb a flash sale no matter how large you size it (Module 1's vertical-scaling ceiling). ShopSphere needs something in front of the fleet that spreads requests across multiple healthy instances, and something behind the scenes that grows and shrinks that fleet automatically as demand changes — the "horizontal scaling" and "elasticity" ideas from Module 1, made concrete.

02 Concept

Elastic Load Balancing (ELB) distributes incoming traffic across multiple targets (EC2 instances, containers, IP addresses, Lambda functions) in one or more AZs, and continuously checks each target's health so traffic only ever reaches targets that can actually serve it. EC2 Auto Scaling maintains a fleet of instances at a size you define declaratively (a range and a policy, not a fixed headcount) and replaces any instance that fails a health check. Together they turn "a server" into "a self-healing, elastic capacity pool."

03 How it works

A Launch Template is the "recipe" the ASG uses to create a new instance — AMI, instance type, Security Group, IAM instance role, user-data bootstrap script — the same ingredients Module 05 built by hand, now templated so scaling out means "launch another exact copy," never a one-off manual build.

04 ShopSphere use case

Checkout, fronted and elastic

ShopSphere's checkout fleet sits in an Application Load Balancer target group spanning all three ap-south-1 AZs, with an Auto Scaling Group of min 2 / desired 2 / max 20 m7g.large instances built from the Module 05 Launch Template. A target-tracking policy holds average CPU near 60%; during a flash sale the ASG scales toward 20 instances within minutes, and back down to 2 overnight — the elasticity example from Module 1, now with the actual mechanism behind it.

05 Functional & non-functional requirements

FunctionalEnabled by
Customer traffic reaches a healthy checkout instance, never a broken oneALB target group health checks
Checkout capacity grows during a flash sale without a human paged at 2amAuto Scaling target-tracking policy + CloudWatch alarm
An in-flight checkout request isn't dropped mid-payment during scale-inDeregistration delay (connection draining), covered in Section 08
Non-functional dimensionImpact
AvailabilityRemoves the single-instance point of failure; survives one AZ's instances failing entirely
ScalabilityTrue horizontal scaling, automated in both directions
PerformanceA bad health check (Section 06) can silently route traffic to a slow-but-"healthy" instance — the load balancer only helps if the signal it trusts is accurate
ReliabilityUnhealthy instances are automatically replaced, not paged for manual replacement
CostPay for the ALB continuously, plus exactly the instance-hours the ASG decides it needs — no permanently over-provisioned peak-sized fleet

06 Alternatives — ALB vs NLB vs Gateway Load Balancer

Application Load BalancerNetwork Load BalancerGateway Load Balancer
OSI layerLayer 7 (HTTP/HTTPS/gRPC)Layer 4 (TCP/UDP/TLS)Layers 3 & 7 — transparent network gateway
Routes onPath, host header, headers, query stringIP + port only, no content inspectionAll traffic, unmodified, to a fleet of virtual appliances
Throughput / latency profileHigh, but content inspection adds a small amount of processingExtreme throughput, ultra-low and consistent latency, static IP supportInline, built for appliance scaling, not end-user traffic shaping
ShopSphere fitDefault choice — routes /products, /cart, /orders to the right service by pathA future gRPC-heavy internal service mesh hop, or any non-HTTP TCP/UDP protocol needing a fixed IP for allowlistingInserting a third-party firewall/IDS inline in front of the VPC, transparently, without re-architecting every app

For ShopSphere's customer-facing APIs, the answer is almost always ALB — path-based routing is exactly what lets one load balancer front Catalog, Cart, Checkout and Order behind different target groups without standing up a separate load balancer per domain.

07 Target groups & health checks — what a bad one actually does

A target group's health check is a periodic HTTP request (or TCP connection) the load balancer sends to each registered target; failing it a configured number of consecutive times marks the target unhealthy and the ALB stops routing to it.

The health check mistake that looks fine in a demo

Pointing the health check at / (or any endpoint that just returns the static homepage) checks that the web server process is alive — nothing more. An instance whose database connection pool is exhausted, or whose downstream Payment dependency is timing out, can keep answering / with a 200 while every real checkout request it receives fails. The ALB, trusting the health check, keeps sending it traffic. A real readiness endpoint (/healthz or similar) should verify the dependencies that actually matter for that instance to do its job — a DB ping, a cache connectivity check — cheaply enough to run every few seconds without itself becoming a load problem.

08 Scaling policies & graceful scale-in

Policy typeHow it decidesShopSphere use
Target trackingHolds a metric (CPU%, request count per target) at a set value, scaling continuously to match — AWS's recommended defaultCheckout's primary policy: hold avg CPU near 60%
Step scalingDifferent, larger scaling steps triggered at different alarm thresholds (e.g. +2 instances at 70% CPU, +6 at 90%)A faster-reacting secondary policy for the steepest part of a flash-sale ramp, where target tracking's smoother response is too slow
Scheduled scalingA fixed capacity change at a known time, no metric involvedPre-warming the fleet 15 minutes before a publicly announced flash-sale start time, instead of reacting after the fact

Scaling out is the easy half. Scaling in correctly is where naive designs drop live traffic: when the ASG terminates an instance, the deregistration delay (connection draining) on its target group keeps routing existing in-flight connections to that instance for a configured window (300 seconds by default) while new requests go elsewhere, and only then does the instance actually terminate. Set this window shorter than your longest realistic request (a checkout submission, a large upload) and you'll cut customers off mid-transaction during every scale-in event — a failure mode that only shows up under real flash-sale load, never in a quiet staging environment.

09 When NOT to use it

ALB + ASG is the wrong default for…

• A fully serverless service (Module 09's Lambda/Fargate paths) — API Gateway or the ALB's Lambda-target integration replaces the ASG half entirely.
• Extreme-throughput, non-HTTP, static-IP-dependent traffic — reach for an NLB instead of forcing Layer-7 routing onto a Layer-4 problem.
• A workload with exactly one, permanently fixed instance count and no variability — the ASG's self-healing (replacing a failed instance) is still valuable even then, but target-tracking policies add nothing to tune.

10 Trade-offs

An ALB and ASG add a layer of indirection and configuration surface — target groups, health check tuning, scaling policies, Launch Template versioning — that a single instance never needed. That cost buys real things: no single-instance outage reaches customers, capacity tracks demand instead of a fixed guess, and a bad deploy or crashed process gets replaced automatically instead of paged. The trade-off sharpens around tuning: a health check that's too lenient hides real failures (Section 07); a scaling policy that's too conservative lags a flash sale; one that's too aggressive "flaps" (scaling out and back in repeatedly), which costs money and destabilizes connection pools on whatever's downstream.

11 Failure scenarios

FailureWhat happensMitigation
One AZ's instances all failALB stops routing there; remaining AZs absorb the traffic if sized for itSpread min capacity across ≥2 AZs so losing one still leaves a healthy majority
Health check endpoint itself is slow/flakyHealthy instances flap unhealthy and get replaced needlessly, or truly unhealthy ones look fine (Section 07)A cheap, accurate, dependency-aware readiness endpoint with sane thresholds
Scaling policy lags a sudden spikeExisting instances saturate before new capacity comes online; latency climbs, requests time outPre-warm with scheduled scaling ahead of known events; tune target-tracking headroom lower (scale earlier)
Deregistration delay set too shortIn-flight checkout/payment requests get cut off mid-transaction during every scale-inSet the delay to safely exceed the longest realistic request duration

12 Security

  • TLS termination at the ALB (ACM-issued certificate), with the option to re-encrypt to targets for end-to-end TLS on PCI-sensitive payment paths.
  • The ALB's Security Group allows inbound 443 from the internet; each target's Security Group allows inbound only from the ALB's Security Group — instances are never directly reachable, the exact pattern from Module 05.
  • AWS WAF attaches directly to the ALB (covered in depth in Module 22, Security Architecture) — this is the earliest point in the request path to block malicious traffic before it reaches any application code.
  • IAM instance role attached via the Launch Template, so every instance the ASG launches inherits least-privilege access automatically — no manual per-instance setup step to forget.

13 Production configuration

DevelopmentProduction
Min/desired/max1/1/1, no real scaling2/2/20+ across ≥2–3 AZs
Health check/, default thresholdsDependency-aware /healthz, tuned interval/threshold to balance detection speed vs false positives
Deregistration delayDefault (300s), untestedExplicitly set & tested against the longest real request the service handles
Scaling policyNone, or a single loose target-tracking ruleTarget tracking for steady response + step scaling or scheduled scaling layered on for known traffic shapes

14 Monitoring

Key CloudWatch signals: HealthyHostCount/UnHealthyHostCount per target group (a sustained unhealthy count is an early warning before customers notice), TargetResponseTime (the ALB's own latency view of the backend, independent of app-level instrumentation), RequestCountPerTarget (feeds target-tracking scaling decisions), and HTTPCode_ELB_5xx_Count vs HTTPCode_Target_5xx_Count — the distinction matters: ELB-generated 5xxs mean the load balancer itself couldn't route (e.g. no healthy targets), while target 5xxs mean your application is returning errors. Alarm on both separately; they point at different root causes.

15 Cost

ALB pricing has two components: an hourly charge for the load balancer running at all, plus Load Balancer Capacity Units (LCUs) driven by whichever of connection count, active connection duration, processed bytes or rule evaluations is highest — meaning cost scales with real traffic shape, not a flat per-request rate. ASG itself is free; you pay only for the EC2 instances (or Fargate tasks) it launches, which is why right-sizing the min capacity matters as much as the max — an over-generous minimum pays 24/7 for headroom the scaling policy would otherwise provide on demand. Verify current ELB pricing before costing a real design.

16 Hands-on lab

  1. Design the health check for ShopSphere's checkout service: name the exact endpoint, what it should verify, the interval, and the healthy/unhealthy thresholds you'd set — and justify each number.
  2. ShopSphere's checkout submission can legitimately take up to 12 seconds under load (payment provider round-trip included). Set and justify a deregistration delay value, and explain what happens to a request still in flight at the moment the delay expires.
  3. Sketch a combined scaling policy for a flash sale that starts at a publicly announced time: name what scheduled scaling, target tracking and step scaling each contribute, and why none of the three alone is sufficient.

17 Interview questions

Why would you choose an NLB over an ALB even though ALB supports more routing features?
When the workload needs something ALB structurally can't offer: a static IP address for allowlisting by a third party, raw TCP/UDP traffic that isn't HTTP, or latency/throughput at a scale where Layer-7 content inspection itself becomes the bottleneck. Routing sophistication is irrelevant if the protocol or performance requirement rules ALB out first.
An ASG shows healthy instances, but customers report dropped checkout requests specifically during scale-in events. What's the likely cause?
The target group's deregistration delay is probably shorter than the real duration of a checkout request, so the ALB stops the terminating instance before its in-flight requests finish. The fix is raising the deregistration delay to safely exceed the longest realistic request, and confirming the application itself handles SIGTERM by finishing in-flight work rather than dying immediately.
What's wrong with a health check that simply checks "/" returns 200?
It verifies the web server process is alive, not that the instance can actually do its job. An instance with an exhausted database connection pool or a failing downstream dependency can still return 200 on "/" while failing every real request. The health check should hit a readiness endpoint that checks the dependencies the service actually needs to function correctly.

AWS · officialTarget Group Deregistration DelayThe exact mechanics of graceful connection draining during scale-in.docs.aws.amazon.com ↗

Level 3 · Application Architecture

VPC Deep Dive: Subnets, Routing, NAT, Endpoints

● ~27 min read● Builds on Module 10● ShopSphere: stops paying NAT Gateway to talk to its own S3 bucket● AWS service

Bottom line: the VPC basics from Module 06 (subnets, route tables, Internet/NAT Gateways) are necessary but not sufficient — a production VPC also needs a way for private-subnet traffic to reach AWS services without leaving the AWS network at all, and a plan for connecting many VPCs that doesn't collapse under its own connection count as the Module 04 multi-account structure grows.

01 Problem

ShopSphere's checkout instances sit in a private subnet (Module 06) and reach the internet — and, by default, every AWS service including S3 — through a NAT Gateway. That works, but it's both more expensive and less secure than it needs to be: every byte of S3 traffic is billed at NAT Gateway data-processing rates and briefly transits a path that, conceptually, leaves the private network before coming back. And as ShopSphere's Module 04 account structure grows past two or three VPCs, point-to-point VPC Peering connections stop being a plan and start being a combinatorial problem.

02 VPC Endpoints — Gateway vs Interface

A VPC Endpoint lets resources in a VPC reach an AWS service privately, without the traffic ever traversing the public internet or a NAT Gateway. There are two distinct implementations, and the difference matters operationally, not just conceptually:

Gateway EndpointInterface Endpoint (PrivateLink)
MechanismAn entry added to your route table pointing at the serviceAn Elastic Network Interface with a private IP, placed directly in your subnet
Supported servicesOnly S3 and DynamoDBMost other AWS services, and third-party/partner services via PrivateLink
CostFree — no hourly or per-GB chargeHourly charge per endpoint per AZ, plus per-GB data processing
ReachabilityOnly from within the VPC whose route table references itCan be reached from on-prem/peered networks too, since it's just an ENI with a routable IP
DNSNo DNS change needed — the service's standard endpoint now simply resolves to a private routeRequires private DNS (usually automatic) so the service's standard hostname resolves to the ENI's private IP instead of a public one

03 ShopSphere use case — routing S3 off the NAT Gateway

ShopSphere's decision

Checkout's private subnet route table gets a Gateway Endpoint for S3 (product images, invoices) and one for DynamoDB (the cart table from Module 08) — both free, both a route-table change with no application code change. Every other AWS API the checkout service calls (Secrets Manager, KMS, CloudWatch Logs) goes through Interface Endpoints as the architecture matures past this module, closing off the NAT Gateway as a path for AWS-internal traffic almost entirely; it's left handling only genuine third-party internet calls, like the payment provider.

04 VPC Peering vs Transit Gateway

VPC PeeringTransit Gateway
TopologyPoint-to-point, one connection per pair of VPCsHub-and-spoke — every attached VPC/VPN/Direct Connect connects once, to the hub
Scaling to N VPCsUp to N(N−1)/2 connections — grows quadraticallyN attachments, linear, regardless of how many VPCs eventually join
Transitive routingNot supported — VPC A peered to B and B peered to C does not let A reach CSupported natively — any attached network can reach any other through the hub, subject to route tables
Cross-accountSupported, but each connection is a separate thing to track and secureDesigned for it — a natural fit for Module 04's many-OU, many-account structure
CostNo hourly charge; pay only for data transferHourly charge per attachment plus data processing — real, ongoing cost for the simplicity it buys

Where peering quietly becomes the wrong tool

Two or three VPCs that need to talk to each other is a fine fit for Peering — no hourly charge, nothing to over-engineer. The moment ShopSphere's Workloads OU splits into per-domain accounts (Module 04's eventual Order/Payment/Catalog team accounts) each needing shared access to Infrastructure OU services, Peering's lack of transitive routing and its N(N−1)/2 connection growth stop being a minor inconvenience and become an operational liability — that's the point to move to Transit Gateway, before the mesh gets hard to reason about, not after.

05 The full hop-by-hop path

HopWhat it doesWhy it's there
Internet → CloudFrontTerminates the connection at the nearest Edge Location, serves cached product images/static assets directlyLowest possible latency to the shopper; absorbs most traffic volume before it ever reaches ShopSphere's own infrastructure
CloudFront → WAFInspects the request against managed/custom rules (SQLi, XSS, rate-based rules)Blocks malicious or abusive requests at the edge, before they cost a single unit of application compute
WAF → ALBRoutes by path/host to the correct target group (/products, /cart, /orders)One entry point, many backend services — Module 10's load balancing and health checking
ALB → application (ECS/EC2, private subnet)The actual business logic runs here — Module 09's compute decision per domainNever directly reachable from the internet; only the ALB's Security Group is allowed in
Application → database (private subnet)Reads/writes against RDS/Aurora/DynamoDBDatabase subnets have no route to the internet at all — not even via NAT — the strongest isolation boundary in the whole path

06 Functional & non-functional requirements

Non-functional dimensionImpact of endpoints & TGW
SecurityTraffic to S3/DynamoDB never traverses the public internet; Transit Gateway route tables let you segment which spokes can reach which, centrally
CostGateway Endpoints remove NAT Gateway data-processing charges for S3/DynamoDB entirely; Transit Gateway trades a per-attachment hourly fee for avoiding a peering mesh's operational cost
ScalabilityTransit Gateway scales to hundreds of VPCs/accounts linearly; Peering does not scale past a handful cleanly
MaintainabilityOne hub to reason about (TGW) beats an ever-growing mesh of bespoke peering connections and manual route table entries

07 When NOT to use it

Don't reach for the heavier tool by default

• Transit Gateway for two VPCs that will never grow past two — its hourly attachment fee buys scalability you don't need yet; Peering is strictly simpler and cheaper there.
• Interface Endpoints for S3/DynamoDB specifically — the free Gateway Endpoint already covers exactly those two services; paying an hourly Interface Endpoint fee for them is pure waste.
• VPC Endpoints as a substitute for a real Security Group/NACL strategy — they remove the path to the public internet for specific services, they don't replace least-privilege access control on what's allowed to call those services.

08 Trade-offs

Gateway Endpoints are close to a free lunch — no cost, a route-table change, immediate security and cost benefit for S3/DynamoDB traffic. Interface Endpoints trade a real hourly-plus-per-GB cost for private connectivity to the much larger set of services Gateway Endpoints don't cover; worth it per-service once that service's traffic volume or security posture justifies it, not applied blanket to every AWS API by default. Transit Gateway trades Peering's zero hourly cost and operational simplicity (at small scale) for linear growth and centralized route control at larger scale — the crossover point is almost always "more VPCs than you can comfortably enumerate from memory."

09 Failure scenarios

FailureWhat happensMitigation
NAT Gateway in an AZ failsEvery private-subnet instance in that AZ loses outbound internet access; other AZs are unaffected if each has its own NAT GatewayOne NAT Gateway per AZ, never a single shared one — a classic cost-cutting mistake that reintroduces a single point of failure
Gateway Endpoint route missing after a route table changeS3/DynamoDB traffic silently falls back to the NAT Gateway path — still works, just loses the cost/security benefit, often unnoticed for monthsMonitor NAT Gateway data-processing cost/volume for unexpected S3/DynamoDB-shaped traffic as a signal
Transit Gateway route table misconfiguredA spoke VPC that should be isolated can reach another spoke it shouldn't, or a spoke that should have access can't reach a shared serviceTreat TGW route tables with the same change-review rigor as IAM policies — they are a security boundary, not just a routing convenience

10 Security

  • Gateway and Interface Endpoints both support endpoint policies — a resource-policy-style document restricting exactly which S3 buckets/DynamoDB tables/API actions are reachable through that endpoint, independent of IAM identity policies (the same "narrowest applicable layer wins" principle from Module 04's RCPs).
  • Database subnets have no route to an Internet Gateway or NAT Gateway at all — the strongest network-layer isolation available, enforced by routing, not just Security Groups.
  • Transit Gateway route tables are themselves a segmentation control: a Sandbox-OU VPC attached to the TGW can be given a route table that simply has no route to Production VPCs, independent of any Security Group.
  • PrivateLink-based Interface Endpoints mean third-party SaaS integrations can be reached without ever assigning the VPC a public IP or routing through the internet at all.

11 Production configuration

DevelopmentProduction
NAT GatewayOne, shared across AZs (cheaper, single point of failure)One per AZ, matched to that AZ's subnets
S3/DynamoDB accessVia NAT Gateway, unexaminedGateway Endpoint in every VPC that touches S3/DynamoDB — a near-zero-cost, near-zero-risk change
Multi-VPC connectivityAd-hoc Peering as neededTransit Gateway once VPC/account count passes single digits, with route tables reviewed like IAM policy

12 Monitoring

VPC Flow Logs (captured to CloudWatch Logs or S3) record accepted and rejected traffic at the ENI, subnet or VPC level — the primary tool for confirming an endpoint is actually being used (traffic to S3's IP ranges should disappear from the NAT Gateway's flow logs once the Gateway Endpoint route is in place) and for investigating a Transit Gateway routing problem after the fact. Monitor NAT Gateway BytesOutToDestination/BytesInFromDestination in CloudWatch as a direct cost signal, and Transit Gateway's per-attachment BytesIn/BytesOut to see which spoke is driving data-processing cost.

13 Cost

NAT Gateway charges an hourly rate plus a per-GB data-processing fee on every byte that passes through it — which is exactly the charge a Gateway Endpoint eliminates for S3/DynamoDB traffic, at zero cost to add. Interface Endpoints add their own hourly-per-AZ plus per-GB charge, which only pays for itself once a service's traffic volume or the security requirement for private connectivity justifies it. Transit Gateway bills per attachment-hour plus data processed through the hub — real money that buys avoiding the operational cost of a peering mesh, not a free scaling tier. Verify current NAT Gateway, VPC Endpoint and Transit Gateway pricing before costing a real design.

14 Hands-on lab

  1. List every AWS service ShopSphere's checkout service calls (S3, DynamoDB, Secrets Manager, KMS, CloudWatch Logs). For each, state whether a Gateway Endpoint, an Interface Endpoint, or neither is the right choice, and why.
  2. Draw the VPC Peering mesh ShopSphere would need if its Order, Payment, Catalog and Infrastructure accounts each needed to reach every other one directly. Count the connections, then redraw it as a Transit Gateway hub-and-spoke and count the attachments.
  3. A teammate proposes removing WAF from the hop-by-hop path "to reduce latency since CloudFront already caches most traffic." Explain, using Section 05's table, exactly what protection this removes and what kind of request it would newly expose the ALB and application to.

15 Interview questions

Why is a Gateway Endpoint free while an Interface Endpoint is not, and why does that map to which services each supports?
A Gateway Endpoint is just a route table entry pointing traffic at a service AWS already operates at massive shared scale (S3, DynamoDB) — there's no new network interface or metered resource to bill for. An Interface Endpoint provisions an actual ENI with a private IP in your subnet for every other AWS/PrivateLink service, which is a real, metered resource, hence the hourly and per-GB charge.
Why doesn't VPC Peering support transitive routing, and why does that matter at scale?
Each peering connection is a strictly independent routing relationship between exactly two VPCs; AWS deliberately does not propagate routes across a chain of peering connections, to keep each connection's blast radius and routing behavior predictable and explicit. At scale this means a hub VPC peered to ten spokes does not let those ten spokes reach each other through it — you'd need 45 additional direct connections for full mesh connectivity, which is precisely the problem Transit Gateway was built to remove.
A team says "we don't need VPC Endpoints, NAT Gateway already lets us reach S3 fine." What's missing from that reasoning?
Functionally true but economically and architecturally wrong: it's paying NAT Gateway's per-GB data-processing rate for traffic that could travel over a free, more secure, AWS-internal path instead. "It works" and "it's the right design" aren't the same claim — the question is whether that traffic should ever have been routed through infrastructure built for general internet egress in the first place.

Level 3 · Application Architecture

Storage Deep Dive: S3, EBS, EFS, FSx

● ~26 min read● Builds on Module 11● ShopSphere: stops paying Standard-storage rates for photos nobody's viewed in a year● AWS service

Bottom line: Module 07 established that ShopSphere uses S3, EBS and EFS for different storage shapes. This module is about what happens to that data over its entire lifetime — moving it automatically to cheaper tiers as access frequency drops, replicating it for disaster recovery, and backing it up so "we lost data" is never the answer to a production incident.

01 Problem

A product image uploaded today is viewed thousands of times this week and almost never again after the listing is retired. An order invoice is read constantly for 30 days (customer support, returns) and then, by regulation, must simply exist for years without ever being deleted — and almost never being read. Storing both at S3 Standard rates forever is correct on day one and a slowly compounding waste every day after that. Module 07 picked the right storage service; this module picks the right storage class, and automates the move between classes as access patterns change.

02 Concept — the storage class spectrum

Every S3 storage class stores the same object with the same 11-nines durability (Module 1's definition, still true here) — what changes across classes is availability SLA, retrieval latency, minimum storage duration, and price. The spectrum trades millisecond access for a dramatically lower per-GB storage cost, at the price of either slower retrieval or a minimum commitment period that penalizes early deletion.

03 S3 storage classes, compared

Storage classRetrieval timeMin. storage durationTypical use
S3 StandardMillisecondsNoneActively-sold product images, current catalog data
S3 Intelligent-TieringMilliseconds (frequent/infrequent tiers); minutes–hours if objects move into its archive tiersNoneData with unpredictable access patterns — AWS automatically moves objects between cost tiers for you
S3 Standard-IAMilliseconds30 daysDiscontinued-product images, 60–90-day-old order invoices still occasionally pulled by support
S3 One Zone-IAMilliseconds30 daysEasily-recreatable secondary copies — ~20% cheaper than Standard-IA, but durable in only one AZ
S3 Glacier Instant RetrievalMilliseconds90 daysRarely-viewed but still occasionally-needed archive images with an instant-access requirement
S3 Glacier Flexible RetrievalMinutes to hours (free bulk retrieval in 5–12 hours)90 daysOld order invoices and audit trail data, read almost never and never urgently
S3 Glacier Deep Archive12–48 hours180 daysLong-term regulatory/compliance retention — AWS's lowest-cost storage tier

The minimum-duration trap

Deleting or transitioning an object out of an IA or Glacier class before its minimum storage duration elapses still bills you for the full minimum duration. Lifecycle rules that transition objects too aggressively — say, to Glacier Deep Archive after only 10 days — can cost more than simply leaving them on Standard a little longer, if there's any realistic chance they'll be deleted or re-accessed (and re-transitioned) before the 180-day floor is up.

04 Lifecycle policies on ShopSphere's data

DataLifecycle ruleReasoning
Product images (active listing)Standard → Intelligent-Tiering at 30 daysAccess pattern is genuinely unpredictable (a discontinued product can suddenly spike from a review or recall) — let AWS's automatic tiering handle it rather than guessing a fixed schedule
Product images (delisted >365 days)→ Glacier Instant Retrieval, then → Glacier Deep Archive at 2 yearsEssentially never viewed again, but retained for historical/catalog-audit reasons at the lowest sustainable cost
Order invoicesStandard → Standard-IA at 30 days → Glacier Flexible Retrieval at 180 daysHeavy access in the first 30 days (support, returns), rare-but-real access through 180 days, then pure regulatory retention
Application/ALB access logsStandard → expire (delete) at 90 daysOperational logs have a short useful life once aggregated into CloudWatch/an observability pipeline (Module 24); indefinite retention is pure cost with no benefit

05 Cross-region replication for DR

S3 Cross-Region Replication (CRR) asynchronously copies new objects (and, optionally, existing ones via a batch job) from a bucket in one region to a bucket in another, automatically and continuously. This is the S3-specific instance of the geographic-redundancy principle from Module 1: ShopSphere's product-image bucket in ap-south-1 replicates to a standby bucket in ap-southeast-1, so a regional failure doesn't mean every product image goes dark along with the primary region — directly supporting the DR region decision made back in Module 02. Replication is asynchronous, so there is always a small, non-zero RPO (Module 1's Recovery Point Objective) between the two buckets — covered quantitatively in Module 26's Disaster Recovery module.

06 FSx — specialized file-system workloads

FSx is a managed file-system service for workloads EFS (Module 07's shared Linux file storage) doesn't fit. FSx for Windows File Server provides a fully-managed native Windows file system (SMB protocol, Active Directory integration) for any .NET-based ShopSphere tooling that genuinely requires Windows file semantics. FSx for Lustre provides a high-performance parallel file system built for the kind of throughput machine-learning training or large-scale batch analytics needs — a future fit for the Recommendation domain's model-training pipeline (Module 28), not a day-to-day ShopSphere storage need. Neither is on ShopSphere's critical path today; they're specialized tools for specialized workloads, not EFS replacements.

07 Backup strategy — EBS snapshots + S3 lifecycle + RDS backups

LayerMechanismShopSphere application
EBSPoint-in-time snapshots to S3, schedulable via Data Lifecycle Manager or AWS BackupCheckout instance root volumes — though stateless, replaceable instances (Module 09/10) reduce how much this matters in practice
S3Versioning (protects against accidental overwrite/delete) + Lifecycle transitions (Section 04) + CRR (Section 05)Product images and invoices — the actual business-critical durable data
RDS/AuroraAutomated daily backups with point-in-time recovery inside the retention window, plus manual snapshots before risky changesThe Order database (Module 08) — the single most important recoverability guarantee in the whole platform

These three mechanisms aren't redundant with each other — they cover genuinely different failure modes (a bad deploy corrupting an instance disk; an accidental DELETE on an S3 object; a schema migration gone wrong on the Order database) and a real backup strategy runs all three simultaneously, not one chosen as "the backup plan."

08 Functional & non-functional requirements

Non-functional dimensionImpact
CostDominant lever in this module — moving aged, rarely-read data to IA/Glacier classes routinely cuts storage spend by 60–90% on that data with zero functional impact
DurabilityIdentical (11 nines) across every S3 storage class — moving to a cheaper tier never trades away durability
AvailabilityDiffers slightly by class (One Zone-IA is intentionally lower, since it's single-AZ) — read the SLA per class before choosing it for anything customer-facing
PerformanceRetrieval latency is the real trade-off axis — milliseconds (Standard/IA/Glacier Instant Retrieval) vs minutes-to-hours (Glacier Flexible/Deep Archive)

09 Alternatives & decision framework

The decision is almost entirely about access pattern, not raw data type: known-frequent access stays on Standard; known-infrequent-but-urgent moves to an IA class; known-rare-and-not-urgent moves to a Glacier class; genuinely unpredictable access gets Intelligent-Tiering so you never have to guess. The one case Intelligent-Tiering doesn't remove the need to think about is deliberate long-term archival (compliance retention with a known, rarely-if-ever-accessed future) — that's a direct, explicit transition to Glacier Deep Archive, not something to leave to automatic tiering.

10 When NOT to use it

Lifecycle policies and Glacier are the wrong default for…

• Data with any realistic chance of being read urgently — don't put anything a customer-facing flow might need instantly into Glacier Flexible Retrieval or Deep Archive; use Glacier Instant Retrieval or stay on IA instead.
• Small objects with a short total lifetime — transitioning objects you'll delete in 20 days into a class with a 90-day minimum duration guarantees you pay for the full minimum anyway.
• FSx for Windows/Lustre when EFS's simpler, cheaper, Linux-native model already fits — reach for FSx only when a specific protocol (SMB) or performance profile (Lustre's parallel I/O) is a real, not hypothetical, requirement.

11 Trade-offs

Lifecycle automation trades a small amount of design-time thinking (what's the real access pattern? what's the regulatory retention floor?) for a large, compounding, ongoing cost reduction with no downside to durability. The real trade-off surfaces in retrieval latency and the minimum-duration penalty (Section 03's callout): getting the transition timing wrong either leaves savings on the table (too conservative) or creates a real operational risk of a slow, multi-hour retrieval landing on a path that assumed millisecond access (too aggressive).

12 Failure scenarios

FailureWhat happensMitigation
A support workflow needs an invoice that's already in Glacier Flexible RetrievalRequest blocks for minutes to hours instead of returning instantlyKeep the first 30–90 days (the realistic support window) on Standard-IA, only moving older invoices to Glacier
CRR's destination region has an outage at the moment of a primary-region failureDR failover has nothing current to fail over to for S3 contentMonitor replication lag (Section 13) as a first-class DR health signal, not an afterthought
RDS automated backup retention window is too shortA data-corruption bug discovered after the retention window has elapsed is unrecoverable via point-in-time recoverySet retention to cover the realistic detection lag for the kind of bug that matters (often longer than the default), supplemented by periodic manual snapshots before risky migrations

13 Security

  • S3 Bucket Policies and IAM scoped per bucket/prefix — the Module 03 least-privilege pattern applies identically regardless of storage class.
  • Server-side encryption (SSE-KMS) on every bucket, including replication destinations — CRR can be configured to re-encrypt with a destination-region KMS key rather than assuming the source key is reachable cross-region.
  • S3 Versioning plus MFA Delete on buckets holding anything regulator-sensitive (invoices), so a compromised credential can't simply delete history.
  • RDS automated backups and manual snapshots are encrypted automatically when the source database is encrypted — encrypting the Order database from day one (Module 08) is what makes this inherit correctly, rather than needing a separate decision later.

14 Production configuration

DevelopmentProduction
Lifecycle rulesNone — everything stays on StandardExplicit rules per data type (Section 04's table), reviewed as access patterns actually evolve
ReplicationSingle region onlyCRR to the DR region for anything the DR plan (Module 26) depends on
BackupsDefault RDS retention, no manual snapshotsRetention tuned to realistic detection lag + pre-migration manual snapshots as standard practice

15 Monitoring

S3 Storage Lens gives account/organization-wide visibility into which buckets/prefixes are actually benefiting from lifecycle transitions versus sitting needlessly on Standard. CloudWatch's S3 replication metrics (ReplicationLatency, bytes pending replication) are the direct signal for CRR's real-time RPO — alarm on replication lag growing, since a silent backlog there is exactly the scenario that turns a regional failover into a data-loss event. For RDS, track backup job success/failure explicitly; a backup that's been silently failing for weeks is a far worse discovery than no backup configured at all, because it creates false confidence.

16 Cost

The dominant cost lever is simply how aggressively (and correctly) lifecycle rules move aged data down the class spectrum — the retrieval-time/cost table in Section 03 is, in practice, the single highest-leverage cost-optimization decision in this entire module, routinely cutting the storage line item for infrequently-accessed data by the majority of its cost with no functional downside, provided the minimum-duration trap (Section 03) is respected. CRR adds its own replication data-transfer and destination-storage cost on top of the source bucket's cost — a real, ongoing DR expense, not a one-time setup fee. Verify current S3 storage-class and data-transfer pricing before costing a real design.

17 Hands-on lab

  1. Write a lifecycle policy (in plain English, then as rule pseudo-JSON) for ShopSphere's order-invoice bucket: Standard for 30 days, Standard-IA for 150 more days, then Glacier Flexible Retrieval. Check it against the minimum-duration trap — does any transition risk violating it?
  2. A regulator requires invoices be retrievable within 1 hour of a request, for 7 years. Which storage class(es) satisfy this for the full 7 years, and which popular "cheapest option" would actually violate the requirement?
  3. CRR's replication lag has been climbing for 6 hours and nobody noticed. Write the CloudWatch alarm (metric, threshold, reasoning) that should have caught this before it became a DR-plan risk.

18 Interview questions

Why does moving data to a cheaper S3 storage class never reduce its durability, even though it changes availability and retrieval time?
Durability (the probability an object is never lost) and availability/retrieval latency (how readily it can be served right now) are different engineering guarantees achieved by different mechanisms — the same distinction Module 1 draws between durability and availability generally. AWS replicates every S3 storage class's data across multiple AZs (except One Zone-IA, which trades this away explicitly) to the same 11-nines durability target; what differs between classes is purely how "warm" and instantly-servable that data is kept, which is a cost and latency lever, not a data-loss risk.
What's the risk of being too aggressive with lifecycle transitions, beyond the minimum-duration cost trap?
A data access pattern can change in ways a lifecycle rule, once set, doesn't adapt to automatically (Intelligent-Tiering aside) — a delisted product that suddenly goes viral, or an invoice unexpectedly needed for active litigation, can land in Glacier Flexible or Deep Archive and force a multi-hour-to-day wait exactly when speed matters most. The mitigation is matching the transition schedule to the realistic worst case for re-access, not just the average case.
Why run EBS snapshots, S3 lifecycle/versioning, and RDS automated backups as three separate mechanisms instead of one unified "backup system"?
They protect against genuinely different failure modes with different recovery semantics: EBS snapshots recover a corrupted or lost instance disk; S3 versioning recovers an accidentally deleted or overwritten object; RDS point-in-time recovery recovers from data corruption introduced by a bad deploy or migration, to a specific second. Collapsing them into "one backup plan" usually means whichever mechanism was chosen as "the" backup doesn't actually cover the failure that occurs.
