Level 3 · Application Architecture

Database Architecture: SQL, NoSQL, Caching, Search

● ~30 min read● Builds on Modules 08 & 12● ShopSphere: gives every domain the right database

Bottom line: Module 08 told you Orders goes relational and hinted DynamoDB might fit Cart. This module tells you why, from the distributed-systems theory up — ACID vs BASE, replication, partitioning, and the single most common real-world DynamoDB mistake (a hot partition), and it adds the two pieces Module 08 never touched: a dedicated search engine for Search, and an in-memory cache for Cart's latency budget.

01 Why Module 08 wasn't the whole story

Module 08 made one decision — Orders needs ACID transactions, so it runs on Aurora — and sketched Cart as a DynamoDB candidate without justifying it. That's deliberate: a first database decision should be made before the theory, the same way you drove a car before you understood a combustion engine. This module supplies the engine. By the end you should be able to defend, from first principles, why ShopSphere's Order, Catalog, Cart, Search and Analytics domains each land on a different storage engine — and why that's a feature of the architecture, not an inconsistency in it.

02 ACID vs BASE — two answers to "what can I rely on?"

ACID (Atomicity, Consistency, Isolation, Durability) is the guarantee a relational database makes about a transaction: it fully happens or fully doesn't (Atomicity), it never leaves data in a state that violates defined rules (Consistency), concurrent transactions don't see each other's half-finished work (Isolation), and once committed, it survives a crash (Durability). This is exactly what "charge the card, decrement inventory, create the order row" needs — partial completion is a corrupted order, not an edge case.

BASE (Basically Available, Soft state, Eventually consistent) is the trade distributed NoSQL stores typically make instead: the system stays available even when parts of it can't immediately agree, "soft state" means data can change without a write (as replicas catch up), and reads may not reflect the very latest write for a short window. DynamoDB's default read is eventually consistent; it offers strongly consistent reads as an option, within a single region, at a performance cost — it is not eventually consistent by accident, it's a deliberate availability/performance trade.

Neither is "better" — they answer different questions

ACID answers "can I trust this is correct right now?" BASE answers "will this still respond under partial failure, and can I live with data catching up a moment later?" Payment state demands the first answer. A product's view count demands the second. Using the wrong model for either is the actual mistake — not picking BASE, and not picking ACID.

03 Replication: leader/follower, synchronous vs asynchronous

Every production database (relational or not) survives node failure by keeping multiple copies of the data, written by one leader (sometimes called primary/writer) and copied to one or more followers (replicas/readers). How that copying happens is the whole story:

This is the mechanism underneath two things you'll keep meeting: Multi-AZ failover (synchronous, for durability and HA) and read replicas (asynchronous, for read scaling) — which Module 08 introduced and the next section deepens.

04 Sharding & partitioning — splitting data, not just copying it

Replication copies the same data to survive failure and spread reads. Partitioning (sharding is partitioning across separate database instances, rather than within one managed cluster) splits different data across nodes so no single node has to hold or serve all of it — the only way to scale writes past what one machine can handle. A partition key (DynamoDB's term) or shard key determines which partition a given item lands on, almost always via a hash function, so that similar keys spread evenly rather than clustering on one node.

Aurora scales reads via replicas (Module 08) but a single Aurora cluster's write capacity is still bounded by one writer instance — this is precisely why a workload with Cart-scale write volume and simple, key-based access patterns is a candidate for a partitioned NoSQL store instead: DynamoDB spreads both reads and writes across many partitions automatically, at the cost of the relational guarantees Section 02 described.

05 Read replicas vs Multi-AZ, deepened

Module 08 drew the distinction; here's the mechanics that matter in an incident. A Multi-AZ standby is not readable, serves no application traffic, exists purely as a synchronously-replicated failover target, and promotion to new writer typically completes in under a minute with Aurora's architecture — the application reconnects via the same cluster endpoint, which repoints automatically. A read replica is readable, deliberately lags the writer by a variable amount under load (replica lag), and promoting one to writer is a separate, slower, more manual-feeling operation not designed for routine failover.

The mistake this causes in production

Reading your own write immediately after committing it — "show order confirmation right after checkout" — against a read replica can show stale or missing data if replica lag spikes under load. ShopSphere's order-confirmation read goes to the writer (or a replica explicitly confirmed caught up), not to whichever replica the load balancer happened to pick.

06 Hot partitions & DynamoDB partition key design

DynamoDB divides a table into physical partitions behind the scenes, and a table's throughput capacity is spread across them. If your partition key has low cardinality — few distinct values, or a few values that get disproportionate traffic — most requests land on the same one or two partitions while the rest sit idle. That's a hot partition: the table's aggregate capacity looks fine on a dashboard while the hot partition itself throttles, and latency spikes for exactly the customers hitting it.

The mistake ShopSphere must not make

Keying the Cart table by cart_status ("active" / "abandoned" / "checked_out") puts essentially every active cart in the country behind one partition key value — a textbook hot partition, guaranteed to throttle the moment traffic grows past a single partition's capacity. The fix: partition key = customer_id (or cart_id) — high-cardinality, one value per customer, so load spreads naturally across the keyspace. A status field becomes a sort key or an attribute with a secondary index, never the partition key.

DynamoDB's adaptive capacity absorbs some unevenness automatically, and newer capacity models continue to improve this — but adaptive capacity mitigates a skewed access pattern, it doesn't fix a badly chosen key. Design the key for even access first; let adaptive capacity be the safety margin, not the plan.

07 OpenSearch — why Search needs a dedicated engine

ShopSphere's Search domain answers queries like "running shoes under ₹3000, size 9, highly rated" with typo tolerance, relevance ranking, and faceted filters — and needs to answer them in double-digit milliseconds against millions of listings. Two things beginners reach for instead both fail at this:

ApproachWhy it fails for product search
SQL LIKE '%shoes%' on the Catalog databaseNo relevance ranking (every match is equally "correct"), no typo tolerance, full-table scans that get slower as the catalog grows, and it competes for the same database capacity Orders and Catalog writes need
DynamoDB queriesDynamoDB is built for exact-match/range lookups on a known key — it has no concept of relevance scoring, fuzzy text matching, or faceted aggregation across arbitrary attributes

Amazon OpenSearch Service runs a managed OpenSearch cluster — a distributed engine built around an inverted index (mapping every term to the documents containing it, the opposite of a normal row-store index) and relevance scoring (BM25-family ranking), so "best match" is a first-class concept, not something you bolt on. ShopSphere streams product changes from the Catalog's database into OpenSearch (via Module 14's messaging, or a managed data pipeline) so Search has its own purpose-built, independently-scaled copy of the catalog — it never queries OpenSearch to decide what's true about a product, only to decide what's relevant to a query.

08 ElastiCache (Redis) — why Cart needs sub-millisecond latency

Every page view, every "update quantity" click, every checkout-page load reads the cart. If that read costs even a middling 10–20ms database round trip, it's on the critical path of nearly every customer interaction, multiplied by every item added or removed. Amazon ElastiCache (for Redis) is an in-memory data store — no disk I/O on the read/write path — that serves simple key-based reads and writes in sub-millisecond time, which is the actual reason ShopSphere's cart and session data sit there rather than only in a durable database.

ShopSphere's pattern: the cart's durable record lives in DynamoDB (survives a cache node restart, is the source of truth); ElastiCache holds the hot, frequently-read copy keyed the same way (by customer_id/cart_id), with a short TTL. A cache miss falls through to DynamoDB and repopulates the cache — the cache-aside pattern that Module 14 (Caching Strategies) covers in full, including stampede and invalidation. This module's job is narrower: justify why Cart is the domain that needs this at all — it's the domain with the tightest, most-repeated latency budget in the entire customer journey.

09 ShopSphere's domain-to-database map

DomainDatabaseWhy
OrderAurora (relational)Multi-row ACID transactions; money and inventory correctness outrank raw write throughput
CatalogAurora today, DynamoDB a real candidateMostly key-based reads ("get this product") at huge, spiky read volume; relational wins while the team values flexible queries (joins, ad-hoc filters) over raw scale
CartDynamoDB + ElastiCacheSimple, high-volume key-based access; needs horizontal write scaling (DynamoDB) and sub-ms reads (ElastiCache) more than it needs joins or transactions
SearchOpenSearchRelevance ranking, typo tolerance, faceted filtering — a fundamentally different query model than a row-store or key-value store supports
AnalyticsData lake (S3) + Redshift (forward reference: Module 31)Aggregating millions of historical events for BI shouldn't run against the same database serving live checkout traffic, and column-oriented, massively-parallel query engines fit "scan everything, aggregate" far better than a row store tuned for single-row lookups

The question every one of these answers

Why relational for Orders? Because a lost or half-applied transaction is a financial and inventory bug, not an inconvenience — ACID is the requirement, not a preference. Why might DynamoDB fit Catalog or Cart? Because their dominant access pattern is "fetch by key, at huge scale," which is exactly what a partitioned key-value store is built to serve cheaply and horizontally. Why OpenSearch for Search? Because ranking and fuzzy text matching aren't things a row-store or key-value store does at all, dedicated or not. Why Redshift/a data lake for Analytics? Because "scan years of history and aggregate" is a different workload shape than "fetch one row fast," and running it on the operational database would compete with the traffic actually running the business — see Module 31 for the full pipeline.

10 Trade-offs across the stack

Every domain that leaves a single relational database adds an operational seam: more systems to patch, monitor and secure; data that must be kept in sync across stores (Catalog's source of truth in Aurora, its searchable copy in OpenSearch) instead of being trivially consistent by virtue of living in one table; and engineers who now need to reason about which store answers a given question correctly. The payoff is real — each store does its one job far better than a single general-purpose database could do all of them — but "polyglot persistence" is a complexity cost paid up front for a scalability and fit benefit realized later. A team that doesn't yet have Cart's or Search's scale problem can legitimately defer this split; Module 08's simpler starting point is a valid architecture for a while, not a mistake to be corrected on day one.

11 Failure scenarios

FailureWhat happensMitigation
Aurora writer failsWrites stall until a new writer is promotedMulti-AZ standby promotion, typically under a minute; application retries with backoff
Read replica lag spikes under loadReads against that replica return stale dataRoute latency-sensitive "read your own write" paths to the writer; monitor ReplicaLag and fail reads over if it exceeds a threshold
DynamoDB partition key is low-cardinalityOne partition throttles while the table's aggregate metrics look healthy — confusing to diagnoseCorrect key design up front (Section 06); CloudWatch's per-partition throttling signals, not just table-level ones
OpenSearch domain becomes unavailableSearch results fail or degrade; the catalog itself (source of truth) is unaffectedFallback to a simplified "browse by category" path that doesn't depend on search; OpenSearch runs across multiple AZs
ElastiCache node failsCache misses spike, hitting DynamoDB directly — higher latency, not data loss, since the cache is never the source of truthMulti-AZ Redis replication group with automatic failover; design the app so a fully-cold cache degrades gracefully instead of falling over

12 Hands-on lab

  1. ShopSphere's Wishlist feature needs: fetch by customer, high read volume, no transactions, no complex queries. Pick a database for it from this module's options and justify the choice in three sentences.
  2. Design a DynamoDB partition key and sort key for the Cart table that avoids the hot-partition mistake in Section 06, and explain what query pattern each key component supports.
  3. A teammate proposes running product search with SELECT * FROM products WHERE name LIKE '%query%' against Aurora to "avoid adding another service." Write the two-paragraph case against it, citing both a functional gap and an operational risk.

13 Interview questions

A table's aggregate DynamoDB metrics show 20% utilization, but customers report throttling. What's your first hypothesis, and how do you confirm it?
Hypothesize a hot partition: aggregate capacity is spread across many partitions, so healthy-looking aggregate metrics can hide one overloaded partition serving a disproportionate share of traffic. Confirm by checking per-partition (not table-level) throttling signals and by auditing the partition key's cardinality and access distribution — a low-cardinality or skewed key is the usual root cause.
Why is a Multi-AZ standby not simply a "free" read replica?
It's synchronously replicated specifically so failover can happen with zero data loss and in well under a minute — serving read traffic from it would add load and latency variability that works against that guarantee. Read replicas are a separate, asynchronous mechanism built for read scaling, with lag as an explicit, accepted trade-off that a failover target cannot accept.
Why can't ShopSphere just run product search queries against the Catalog's DynamoDB table directly, skipping OpenSearch?
DynamoDB queries require an exact match (or range/prefix) against a known key; they have no concept of relevance ranking, typo tolerance, or free-text matching across multiple attributes at once. Search needs "find the best matches for a fuzzy query," which is a different computational problem than "find the item with this exact key" — solving it well requires an inverted-index, relevance-scoring engine like OpenSearch, not a faster key-value store.

Level 3 · Application Architecture

Integration & Messaging: SQS, SNS, EventBridge, Kinesis

● ~28 min read● Builds on Module 13● ShopSphere: decouples Order Created from everything downstream

Bottom line: the moment "place an order" needs to trigger payment, inventory, notification, shipment and analytics, calling all five synchronously from the Order service is a reliability trap — one slow or down dependency stalls checkout for everyone. Asynchronous messaging breaks that coupling, but it hands you a new, non-optional job: AWS's messaging services default to at-least-once delivery, so idempotency and ordering become your application's responsibility, not something the platform quietly guarantees.

01 The problem: synchronous coupling doesn't scale

If the Order service calls Payment, Inventory, Notification, Shipment and Analytics one after another and waits for each to respond before replying to the customer, checkout's latency is the sum of all five, and checkout's availability is the product of all five — one flaky dependency (Notification being slow is the classic example) drags down the one thing that must never be slow: taking the customer's money and confirming their order.

02 Synchronous vs asynchronous communication

Synchronous (request/response)Asynchronous (message-based)
Caller waits for an answer?Yes — blocks until the callee respondsNo — hands off a message and moves on
CouplingTight: caller fails if callee is down or slowLoose: caller succeeds once the message is durably accepted
Best fitThe caller genuinely needs the answer right now (e.g. "is this card valid?")The caller just needs the work to happen, eventually and reliably (e.g. "send a confirmation email")
ShopSphere exampleCheckout calling the Payment gateway for an authorization resultCheckout publishing "Order Created" for Inventory, Notification, Shipment and Analytics to react to

Payment authorization has to stay synchronous — the customer is waiting to know if checkout succeeded. Everything after "the order now exists" doesn't need the customer to wait for it, which is exactly where asynchronous messaging earns its place.

03 Four shapes of asynchronous communication

Before the AWS service names, four distinct communication shapes, because picking the wrong shape is the most common messaging mistake:

ShapeWhat it means
Queue (point-to-point)One message, consumed by exactly one worker from a pool — built for work distribution
Pub/sub (fan-out)One message, delivered to every subscriber independently — built for "notify everyone interested"
Event bus (routed pub/sub)Pub/sub plus content-based routing: each subscriber declares a pattern of events it wants, not just a topic
Stream (ordered log)An ordered, replayable sequence of records, read by consumers at their own pace — built for high-throughput, order-sensitive data

04 Amazon SQS — the queue

Amazon SQS (Simple Queue Service) is a fully managed, pull-based queue: producers send messages, a pool of consumers polls and processes them, and each message is handled by (in the common Standard queue case) one consumer at a time via a visibility timeout that hides a message from other consumers while it's being processed, then returns it to the queue if the consumer never confirms completion. Standard queues are near-unlimited throughput with best-effort ordering; FIFO queues trade some throughput for strict ordering within a message group and exactly-once processing within a deduplication window.

ShopSphere use: the Inventory service doesn't call anything synchronously to decrement stock — it drains an SQS queue of "reserve inventory" messages at whatever pace its database can sustain, which is also how SQS absorbs a traffic spike: the queue simply grows, instead of Inventory's database falling over.

05 Amazon SNS — pub/sub fan-out

Amazon SNS (Simple Notification Service) is managed pub/sub: a publisher sends one message to a topic, and SNS pushes a copy to every current subscriber — which can be SQS queues, Lambda functions, HTTP endpoints, email, or SMS, in any combination. Unlike a single SQS queue, every subscriber gets its own copy; nothing is deleted after one subscriber consumes it.

ShopSphere use: the classic fan-out pattern — SNS topic "Order Created" with four SQS queues subscribed (Payment's own queue, Inventory's, Notification's, Shipment's), each domain consuming its copy at its own pace, completely unaware of the others. One publish, four independent, decoupled consumers.

06 Amazon EventBridge — the event bus

Amazon EventBridge is a managed event bus: producers publish structured events, and consumers subscribe via rules that match on event content (an event pattern), not just a topic name — "give me every event where detail.orderTotal > 10000," for instance, something SNS topics alone can't express. EventBridge adds a schema registry that can discover and version event shapes automatically, an archive-and-replay capability for reprocessing past events, and first-class integrations with hundreds of AWS services and SaaS partner event sources.

ShopSphere use: once the platform has more than a handful of event types and consumers, EventBridge replaces a sprawl of SNS topics with one bus and content-based routing rules — e.g. only the Fraud domain's rule matches high-value orders, without Order having to know Fraud exists.

07 Amazon Kinesis Data Streams — the ordered stream

Amazon Kinesis Data Streams is a different shape entirely: a high-throughput, ordered, replayable log, partitioned into shards. Records with the same partition key always land on the same shard and are delivered to consumers in the order they arrived on that shard; multiple independent consumers can each read the whole stream at their own position, and (within the stream's retention window, extendable well beyond the default) can replay history — something queues and topics, which delete a message once consumed, don't offer.

ShopSphere use: clickstream and page-view events feeding real-time analytics and the recommendation pipeline, where both the volume (every click, from every shopper) and the need for ordered, replayable processing point at a stream rather than a queue or topic. Order Created itself doesn't need this shape — it needs reliable fan-out, not a replayable ordered log.

08 Decision table

SQSSNSEventBridgeKinesis
ShapeQueuePub/subRouted pub/subOrdered stream
Consumption modelPull; one consumer per messagePush; every subscriber gets a copyPush; rule-matched subscribers get a copyPull; every consumer reads the whole stream independently
OrderingBest-effort (Standard) or strict per group (FIFO)Best-effort (Standard) or strict (FIFO topics)Not guaranteed across rulesStrict within a shard
Replay past messages?No — consumed messages are goneNoYes, via Archive & ReplayYes, within the retention window
Routing logicNone — one queue, one purposeTopic-level onlyContent-based (event patterns)None — partition key only
ShopSphere fitWork distribution (Inventory reservation queue)Simple fan-out (Order Created → 4 queues)Many event types, selective routing, SaaS integrationsHigh-volume ordered event data (clickstream, metrics)

09 ShopSphere's Order Created fan-out

What this buys ShopSphere

Order publishes one event and returns — checkout latency no longer includes Payment's gateway call chain to four other domains, or Notification's email provider being slow. If Shipment's queue backs up during a flash sale, orders still get created and paid for; shipments just catch up once Shipment's consumers drain the backlog. This is the direct, concrete payoff of asynchronous messaging that Section 01's problem statement promised.

10 Dead-letter queues & poison-message handling

A dead-letter queue (DLQ) is a second queue a primary queue is configured to send a message to after it has failed processing (and been returned to the queue) a configured number of times. Without one, a poison message — one that will never process successfully, perhaps due to a bug or malformed data — gets redelivered and reprocessed forever, consuming consumer capacity and hiding genuinely new failures in the noise of a repeating one.

ShopSphere configures a DLQ on every consumer queue in the fan-out: Notification's queue sends a message to its DLQ after, say, 5 failed delivery attempts, firing a CloudWatch alarm so an engineer investigates — rather than that one malformed notification quietly consuming retry capacity indefinitely while looking, on a dashboard, like normal queue activity.

11 At-least-once delivery, idempotency & ordering — your job, not the platform's

The default every one of these services shares

SQS Standard, SNS Standard, EventBridge and Kinesis all deliver at-least-once by default: a message can be delivered more than once (a consumer that crashes after processing but before acknowledging will see its message redelivered), and Standard queues/topics don't guarantee order. Exactly-once-feeling behavior (SQS/SNS FIFO's deduplication, Kinesis's per-shard order) is real but scoped — it doesn't extend across the whole system end to end. The platform's default assumption is: duplicates can happen, your consumer handles that.

This means every consumer in the fan-out must be idempotent: processing the same "Order Created" message twice must produce the same end state as processing it once — Inventory decrements stock by checking "have I already reserved stock for this order ID?" before acting, rather than blindly decrementing again. Where order matters (rare in this fan-out, common in event-sourced state machines), use FIFO queues/topics or a Kinesis shard keyed consistently, and still don't assume duplicates are impossible — assume they're rare and handle them anyway.

12 Retry, backoff & jitter

A consumer that fails to process a message should retry — many failures (a downstream dependency briefly unavailable) are transient. Retrying immediately and repeatedly, though, can turn a brief blip into a self-inflicted overload (every failed consumer hammering the same struggling dependency at once). Exponential backoff (wait longer between each retry) combined with jitter (randomizing the wait slightly so many consumers don't retry in lockstep) is the standard fix, and it's exactly what sits between "transient failure" and "DLQ after N attempts" in Section 10's flow.

13 Failure scenarios

FailureWhat happensMitigation
A consumer is completely downIts queue backs up; messages wait, nothing is lostSQS retains messages up to its retention period; the consumer catches up once healthy, no customer-facing impact if it's not on checkout's critical path
A message is malformed (poison message)Infinite redelivery and reprocessing without a DLQDLQ after N attempts, with an alarm — Section 10
Flash sale: Order Created volume spikes 50xSNS/SQS scale automatically; a slow downstream consumer's queue simply growsDesign consumers to scale independently (e.g. Lambda concurrency or an Auto Scaling worker fleet) rather than assuming fixed capacity
A consumer processes the same message twiceNon-idempotent logic double-charges, double-decrements, or double-sendsIdempotent consumer logic keyed on a stable message/order ID — Section 11, non-negotiable
SNS topic or EventBridge bus itself degradesNew events may be delayed or (rarely) dropped at the publish stepThese are managed, multi-AZ services with high published availability targets; design the publisher to retry the publish call itself with backoff

14 Hands-on lab

  1. Design the SQS DLQ redrive policy (max receive count, DLQ name) for ShopSphere's Payment consumer queue, and justify the number you chose for max receive count.
  2. Write the pseudocode for an idempotent Inventory consumer that processes "Order Created" messages, including the check that prevents a double-decrement on redelivery.
  3. ShopSphere adds a Fraud domain that only cares about orders over ₹50,000. Decide whether SNS or EventBridge fits better, and write the routing rule/subscription filter that implements it.

15 Interview questions

Why can't you treat "my queue has a DLQ" as proof your system handles duplicate messages correctly?
A DLQ catches messages that repeatedly fail to process — it says nothing about a message that succeeds twice. At-least-once delivery means a consumer can receive and successfully process the same message more than once (e.g. it processed fine but crashed before acknowledging); a DLQ never sees that message at all, because from the queue's point of view nothing failed. Idempotent processing logic is the only thing that actually prevents a double-charge or double-decrement from a successful duplicate delivery.
When would you choose EventBridge over simply adding more SNS topics?
Once you need routing based on event content rather than just event type/topic — e.g. "route to Fraud only if order total exceeds a threshold" — SNS topics can't express that; EventBridge's event patterns can. EventBridge also adds schema discovery/versioning and archive-and-replay, which matter once you have many event types and need to reprocess history, neither of which SNS provides.
ShopSphere's Order Created fan-out uses SNS→SQS rather than publishing straight to Kinesis. Why?
The fan-out's requirement is "deliver this event reliably to five independent, decoupled consumers, each at its own pace" — exactly what pub/sub plus per-consumer queues provides. Kinesis solves a different problem: high-throughput, strictly ordered, replayable streaming, typically for one or a few consumer types reading at high volume (like clickstream analytics). Reaching for Kinesis here would add shard management and consumer complexity with no matching benefit, since fan-out doesn't need strict cross-domain ordering or replay.

AWS · officialWhat Is Amazon EventBridge Schemas?The schema registry capability referenced in Section 06.docs.aws.amazon.com ↗

Level 3 · Application Architecture

API Architecture: REST, API Gateway, GraphQL

● ~26 min read● Builds on Module 14● ShopSphere: designs its public API surface

Bottom line: ShopSphere's API is the contract every client — web, mobile, future partners — depends on, and most of what makes an API "production-grade" isn't the URL shape, it's what happens on retry, under load, and across versions. The single highest-stakes detail in this module: a retried POST /orders or POST /payments must not double-charge a customer — and the only thing that guarantees that is an idempotency key, not hope that the network behaves.

01 The problem: an endpoint list isn't an API design

It's easy to wire up routes that technically work and still ship an API that breaks the moment it meets real-world conditions: a mobile client on a flaky connection that retries a timed-out checkout request, a client that needs page 40 of a million-row product list, a partner integration built against v1 that can't tolerate a breaking change. This module designs ShopSphere's actual public surface against those conditions, not just against the happy path.

02 ShopSphere's public API surface

RoutePurposeBacking domain
GET /productsBrowse/search the catalog, paginatedCatalog / Search
GET /products/{id}Product detailCatalog
POST /cartAdd an item to the cartCart
POST /ordersPlace an order (checkout)Order
GET /orders/{id}Order status / trackingOrder
POST /paymentsAuthorize/capture a paymentPayment

Each of these looks simple as a one-line route. The rest of this module is what turns each one into something safe to run in production.

03 Idempotency keys — the non-negotiable one

The failure this prevents

A customer taps "Place order." The request reaches ShopSphere, the order is created and the card is charged — but the response is lost to a network blip before the client sees it. The client, seeing no response, retries. Without protection, that's a second order and a second charge for one tap. This is not a rare edge case at any real scale — mobile networks drop responses constantly, and checkout is exactly the endpoint customers retry when unsure it worked.

The fix: the client generates a unique idempotency key (a UUID) once per checkout attempt and sends it in a header on POST /orders and POST /payments. The server, before doing any work, checks whether it has already seen that key; if so, it returns the original result instead of creating a second order or issuing a second charge. The server stores the key-to-result mapping for a bounded window (hours, not forever) — long enough to cover realistic retries, short enough not to accumulate unbounded state.

04 Pagination for GET /products

A naive ?page=40&size=20 (offset pagination) gets slower as the offset grows (the database still has to skip past everything before it) and can return duplicate or skipped items if the underlying data changes between page requests — a new product inserted on page 2 shifts everything after it. Cursor-based (keyset) pagination — "give me items after this specific key" — avoids both problems and is the standard choice for a catalog that's both large and constantly changing, which ShopSphere's is.

05 API Gateway vs. putting an ALB directly in front

Both can sit in front of ShopSphere's services and route HTTP requests; what differs is what you get out of the box versus what you build yourself.

Amazon API GatewayApplication Load Balancer (ALB)
ThrottlingBuilt in — per-account, per-stage, per-method, token-bucket with burstNot native; needs WAF rate-based rules bolted on
API keys & usage plansBuilt in — issue keys, set per-key quotas/rate limitsNot available; you'd build this in the application layer
Request validationBuilt in — reject malformed requests before they reach your backendNone; your application validates everything
LatencyAn extra network hop and processing step — measurably higherLower — a thinner layer 7 proxy
CostPer-request pricing on top of computePer-hour plus per-LCU; typically cheaper at very high, steady volume
Best fitPublic APIs needing throttling, API-key management, or Lambda-native integrationHigh-volume internal or public traffic where you already own rate limiting and validation

ShopSphere's public-facing /products, /cart, /orders and /payments routes sit behind API Gateway specifically for the throttling and usage-plan controls Section 06 needs on a surface third-party clients and mobile apps can hit directly; a purely internal service-to-service call between two ShopSphere domains, by contrast, is a reasonable candidate for a plain ALB, since both ends are already trusted and rate limiting is coordinated elsewhere.

06 Rate limiting & throttling

Throttling protects the backend by capping the request rate API Gateway will forward, using a token bucket: a steady refill rate plus a burst allowance for short spikes above it. Configured at the account level (a global ceiling), the stage level (per deployment environment), and optionally per method or per API key via a usage plan — which lets ShopSphere give a trusted seller-integration partner a higher quota than an anonymous public client, on the same API.

07 API versioning — URI vs header

URI versioning (/v1/products)Header versioning (Accept: application/vnd.shopsphere.v1+json)
VisibilityObvious from the URL aloneHidden in a header; easy to miss in logs/docs
CachingCache-friendly — different URL, different cache entryNeeds cache keys to account for the header explicitly
Client ergonomicsTrivial to test with a browser or curlRequires setting a header on every request
"Purity"Arguably violates REST's idea that a URI identifies one resource, not one-per-versionKeeps one URI per resource; often preferred by REST purists

ShopSphere uses URI versioning (/v1/orders) — the practical, cache-friendly, easy-to-debug choice most production APIs converge on, even though header versioning has a defensible theoretical case.

08 GraphQL — when ShopSphere would reach for it

REST's shape is one resource per endpoint; a mobile product-detail screen needing the product, its reviews, its current price, and three recommended items means either four round trips or a bespoke "detail" endpoint that over-fetches fields other screens don't need. GraphQL (on AWS, via AWS AppSync) flips this: one endpoint, and the client specifies exactly the fields it needs across multiple underlying resources in a single request — solving the mobile product-detail screen's problem directly.

What GraphQL doesn't solve for free

A flexible query language makes it easy for a client to accidentally request an expensive, deeply-nested query (reviews, whose authors, whose other reviews…) — the N+1 query problem, usually solved with a batching/dataloader pattern in the resolvers. HTTP-level caching, trivial for REST's "one URL, one resource" shape, is harder when every request can shape its own response. ShopSphere reaches for GraphQL where REST's fixed shape genuinely hurts (the mobile detail screen); it keeps REST everywhere that one resource per endpoint already fits, rather than replacing the whole API surface on principle.

09 WebSockets — real-time order tracking

Order tracking ("preparing → shipped → out for delivery → delivered") is naturally push-based: the server knows the moment status changes, and polling GET /orders/{id} every few seconds wastes requests and still shows stale data between polls. API Gateway WebSocket APIs keep a persistent, bidirectional connection open per client, with dedicated routes for $connect and $disconnect and a connection-ID-keyed way to push a message to a specific client — letting Shipment push a status update the instant it happens, with no polling at all. The trade-off is state: ShopSphere has to track which connection ID belongs to which customer/order (typically in DynamoDB), and billing is per connection-minute and per message rather than the simple per-request model REST clients use.

10 ShopSphere's API Gateway architecture

11 When NOT to put API Gateway in front of everything

API Gateway is the wrong default for…

• Pure internal service-to-service traffic between two ShopSphere domains that already trust each other — an ALB (or direct service mesh call) avoids the added latency and per-request cost for no corresponding benefit.
• Extremely high, steady volume where per-request pricing dominates the bill — at large enough scale, ALB's per-hour-plus-LCU model can cost meaningfully less for traffic that doesn't need API Gateway's request-shaping features.
• Workloads needing the lowest possible added latency — a trading-style or real-time bidding path might not tolerate the extra hop at all.

12 Failure scenarios

FailureWhat happensMitigation
Client retries a timed-out POST /ordersWithout an idempotency key, a second order and charge are createdIdempotency key, checked before any side effect — Section 03
A client/bot sends requests far above its usage planThrottled with HTTP 429, protecting the backendThis is the intended behavior; the client implements backoff on 429
A breaking v2 change ships without a version bumpEvery v1 client (mobile apps already in app stores, partner integrations) breaks simultaneouslyURI versioning with old versions kept running until clients migrate — Section 07
A WebSocket connection drops mid-sessionThe client stops receiving tracking push updates silentlyClient reconnects and the server treats a fresh connection as "catch me up," re-sending current status on $connect

13 Security

  • TLS everywhere — every route is HTTPS-only; API Gateway terminates TLS at the edge.
  • Authentication/authorization on every non-public route — POST /orders and POST /payments require a valid customer identity (Module 17's Cognito/JWT pattern), checked before the idempotency-key lookup even runs.
  • API keys identify a client, not a user — a seller-integration partner's API key scopes and rate-limits that partner's traffic; it is not a substitute for authenticating the individual customer behind a request.
  • Input validation at the gateway — reject malformed payloads before they reach a backend service, narrowing the attack surface for injection-style issues.
  • Request/response logging to CloudWatch, scrubbed of payment card data and other sensitive fields, for audit and incident investigation.

14 Hands-on lab

  1. Design the idempotency-key flow for POST /payments end to end: what the client sends, what the server stores, and what the server returns on a duplicate request with the same key.
  2. Write the cursor-based pagination response shape for GET /products, including the field that tells the client how to fetch the next page.
  3. A partner wants to integrate with ShopSphere's catalog at 10x the rate limit of a normal public client. Design the usage-plan change that grants this without raising the limit for everyone else.

15 Interview questions

Why is an idempotency key necessary even though HTTP already has "safe" and "idempotent" method semantics defined for things like PUT?
Those semantics are a convention about what a method should mean, not a mechanism that makes an operation actually idempotent in practice — POST is defined as non-idempotent by the HTTP spec precisely because "create a new thing" naturally isn't repeatable without side effects. An explicit idempotency key is the application-level mechanism that makes a POST safe to retry, by letting the server recognize and deduplicate a repeated request regardless of what the HTTP method itself implies.
When would you choose ALB over API Gateway for a public-facing endpoint, given API Gateway's extra features?
When those features (API keys/usage plans, request validation, built-in throttling) aren't needed and the added latency or per-request cost isn't worth paying for them — e.g. a very high, steady-volume public endpoint where you're willing to implement rate limiting via WAF and validation in the app layer yourself, in exchange for a thinner, cheaper, lower-latency path.
A mobile team wants GraphQL for the entire ShopSphere API, not just the product-detail screen. What would you push back on?
GraphQL's benefit is concentrated where clients need flexible, cross-resource queries in one round trip; most of ShopSphere's API (place an order, add to cart) is already a clean one-action-per-endpoint fit for REST, where GraphQL adds resolver complexity, N+1 query risk, and harder HTTP caching for no corresponding benefit. The right call is usually GraphQL where it solves a real over/under-fetching problem, REST everywhere else — not an all-or-nothing migration.

Level 4 · Production Architecture

Security Architecture

● ~30 min read● Builds on Module 15● ShopSphere: hardens its public attack surface

Bottom line: Module 03 drew one line — AWS secures of the cloud, you secure in it. This module is what sits entirely on your side of that line, made concrete: a layered defense ShopSphere wraps around its edge, network, identities, data and monitoring, so that no single control failing is the whole story. None of this replaces shared responsibility — it's the homework shared responsibility assigned you.

01 Security is architecture, not a checklist bolted on at the end

Module 03 established the shared responsibility line and the IAM foundations — who can call which API. That's necessary but nowhere near sufficient. IAM says nothing about whether an attacker can inject SQL through ShopSphere's search box, flood the checkout API until it falls over, or read a payment-provider API key straight out of a config file. Those are the gaps this module closes, and they're all still squarely on your side of the shared-responsibility line.

Defense in depth, not a single strong wall

No single control in this module is assumed to be perfect. WAF rules get bypassed, a misconfigured Security Group ships to production, a credential leaks. The architecture is designed so that when one layer fails, the next one still catches the problem — which is why ShopSphere stacks five independent layers rather than betting everything on one.

02 The five layers, assembled

LayerAWS service(s)Stops / detectsShopSphere example
EdgeWAF, ShieldMalicious requests and volumetric floods before they reach the VPCSQLi on /search?q=, a bot scraping the catalog, a DDoS flood
NetworkSecurity Groups, NACLs, private subnetsLateral reachability — what can talk to whatThe Order database is unreachable from anything but the Order service's SG
IdentityIAM (Module 03)Who can call which AWS API, and with what permissionsCheckout's instance role can read one S3 bucket, nothing else
DataKMS, Secrets Manager, Certificate ManagerConfidentiality at rest, in transit, and of credentialsOrder data encrypted at rest; payment API key never in a config file
MonitoringCloudTrail, GuardDuty, Security Hub, ConfigDetect what got through, and configuration driftAn anomalous API call from an unfamiliar region raises a GuardDuty finding

03 Edge — AWS WAF

AWS WAF (Web Application Firewall) inspects HTTP(S) requests against a Web ACL — an ordered list of rules — before they reach the ALB or API Gateway in front of ShopSphere's APIs. Rather than writing every rule by hand, ShopSphere attaches AWS Managed Rule Groups: the Core Rule Set (common web exploits), the SQL Database rule group (SQL-injection patterns), and the Known Bad Inputs rule group, plus a custom rate-based rule that blocks any single IP exceeding a request-rate threshold — the control that actually matters against a bot scraping ShopSphere's catalog or hammering the login endpoint with credential-stuffing attempts.

WAF conceptShopSphere application
Web ACLOne ACL attached to the ALB in front of all public ShopSphere APIs
Managed rule group — SQL databaseBlocks SQL-injection payloads submitted through the product-search box
Managed rule group — Core Rule SetCatches common XSS and generic web-exploit patterns in review/comment fields
Rate-based ruleBlocks an IP issuing >2,000 requests/5 min against /login or /search
Bot Control (managed rule group)Distinguishes verified good bots (e.g. search-engine crawlers) from scrapers

WAF is a filter, not a fix

A managed rule group catches known attack signatures. It does not replace parameterized queries in ShopSphere's own search code, and a sufficiently novel payload can still slip through. WAF buys time and blocks the bulk of automated noise; it is not a substitute for fixing the underlying injection vulnerability in the application layer (Section 12).

04 Edge — AWS Shield

Shield Standard is automatic, free, and active on every AWS account — it absorbs common network- and transport-layer (L3/L4) DDoS patterns (SYN floods, reflection attacks) at the edge, before they reach ShopSphere's load balancer. Shield Advanced is a paid tier that adds L7 (application-layer) DDoS protection tightly integrated with WAF, near-real-time attack visibility, cost protection against scaling charges incurred during an attack, and 24/7 access to the AWS Shield Response Team (SRT) for a large, sustained attack during ShopSphere's highest-traffic events (a flash sale is exactly when an attacker would try).

Shield StandardShield Advanced
CostIncluded, automaticPaid subscription
Layer protectedL3/L4 (network/transport)L3/L4 + L7 (application), via WAF integration
Cost protectionNoYes — credits for scaling charges during an attack
Response team accessNoYes (SRT)
ShopSphere fitBaseline, always onWorth it once checkout outages during an attack have a real revenue cost

05 Network — Security Groups, NACLs, private subnets, recapped and deepened

Module 03 introduced Security Groups as a stateful, instance-level firewall. In a full security architecture they're one of two network controls, not the only one. Network ACLs (NACLs) sit at the subnet boundary, are stateless (return traffic must be explicitly allowed, unlike a Security Group), and evaluate rules in numbered order with explicit allow/deny — useful as a coarse, subnet-wide backstop (e.g. explicitly denying a known-bad CIDR block org-wide) layered behind the finer-grained Security Groups. Private subnets remain the structural control underneath both: ShopSphere's Order, Payment and Inventory databases simply have no route to the internet at all, so no firewall rule misconfiguration can expose them directly — the failure has to happen at two layers simultaneously, not one.

ShopSphere's layering, concretely

The checkout ALB sits in a public subnet. Its Security Group allows 443 from 0.0.0.0/0 — that's intentional, it's the public entry point. The checkout EC2 fleet's Security Group allows 443 only from the ALB's Security Group, not from the internet directly. The Order database's Security Group allows its port only from the Order service's Security Group. Each hop narrows reachability; nothing downstream is one misconfigured rule away from being internet-facing.

06 Data — KMS and envelope encryption

AWS KMS (Key Management Service) manages encryption keys and the permissions to use them. Almost no AWS service encrypts large volumes of data directly with a KMS key — KMS keys are deliberately limited to encrypting small payloads (up to 4 KB). Instead, AWS services use envelope encryption: KMS generates a unique data key, the data key encrypts the actual payload locally (fast, no size limit), and only that small data key is then encrypted ("wrapped") by the KMS key and stored alongside the ciphertext. To decrypt, the service asks KMS to unwrap the data key, then uses the unwrapped key locally — the plaintext data key never leaves memory, and the KMS key itself never directly touches the bulk data.

This is exactly what happens under the hood when ShopSphere enables default encryption on its Aurora Order database, its S3 product-image bucket, and its EBS volumes — one KMS key per data-sensitivity tier (a dedicated key for payment-adjacent data, scoped by its own key policy, separate from the general-purpose key for product images), so a compromise of one key's access doesn't expose everything.

07 Data — Secrets Manager

ShopSphere's checkout service has to call an external payment provider's API, which means holding that provider's API key somewhere. AWS Secrets Manager stores it encrypted (via KMS), hands it to the application at runtime through an API call authorized by IAM, and — the feature that actually earns its keep — can automatically rotate it on a schedule using a rotation Lambda function, without a human touching the secret or redeploying the application.

The alternative is a real, common breach pattern

Hardcoding the payment provider's API key in a config file or environment variable means it ends up in version control history, in CI logs, in a container image layer, or on a developer's laptop — and it never expires on its own. Secrets Manager's rotation means a leaked credential has a shelf life measured in the rotation window (e.g. 30 days), not "until someone notices," the same risk-reduction logic as Module 03's temporary IAM credentials, applied to third-party secrets IAM can't reach.

For configuration values that aren't actually secret (feature flags, non-sensitive settings), AWS Systems Manager Parameter Store is the cheaper alternative — Secrets Manager's cost and built-in rotation machinery earn their place specifically for credentials that need automatic rotation, like this one.

08 Data — Certificate Manager

AWS Certificate Manager (ACM) provisions and auto-renews public TLS certificates at no additional charge, and integrates directly with the ALB and CloudFront so the certificate's private key never leaves AWS-managed infrastructure — ShopSphere's team never downloads, copies, or manually renews a .pem file. The ALB terminates TLS using an ACM certificate for shopsphere.com; for the payment path specifically, ShopSphere additionally keeps the connection encrypted end-to-end from the ALB to the checkout instances, not just at the edge, given the PCI-sensitivity of that traffic.

09 Monitoring — CloudTrail: the audit trail

CloudTrail records every API call made against the account — who (which IAM identity), what action, against which resource, from which IP, and when — and delivers it durably to S3. This is the forensic record that answers "who deleted that S3 bucket policy at 2am" after the fact, and it's also the raw log stream that GuardDuty analyzes for threats. ShopSphere enables an organization trail (Module 04's Security OU, now explained) so every member account's activity lands in one centrally owned, tamper-resistant log archive account that workload-account admins can't modify or disable — an SCP denies disabling CloudTrail account-wide, closing the obvious "attacker disables logging first" move.

10 Monitoring — GuardDuty: threat detection

GuardDuty is a managed threat-detection service that continuously analyzes CloudTrail management events, VPC Flow Logs, and DNS query logs (plus optional S3, EKS and RDS-specific data sources) using threat intelligence feeds and machine learning — with no agent to install and no logs to configure by hand. It surfaces findings ranked by severity: an EC2 instance suddenly querying a known cryptocurrency-mining pool, an IAM credential being used from a geographically implausible location, or unusual API call patterns consistent with reconnaissance. GuardDuty answers "is something actively wrong right now," where CloudTrail only answers "what happened."

11 Monitoring — Security Hub: aggregating findings

Once GuardDuty, AWS Config, and other security tools are all producing findings independently, someone still has to look at all of them in one place. Security Hub aggregates and normalizes findings across GuardDuty, Config, Inspector, and partner security tools into one dashboard, and continuously checks the account against compliance standards (the AWS Foundational Security Best Practices standard, CIS AWS Foundations, and PCI DSS — directly relevant given ShopSphere's payment path). It's the single pane of glass, not a detector in its own right — it has nothing to show without GuardDuty and Config feeding it.

12 Monitoring — AWS Config: configuration compliance

AWS Config continuously records the configuration state of AWS resources and evaluates them against Config Rules — "is this S3 bucket's public-access block enabled," "is this Security Group missing an unrestricted inbound rule," "is this EBS volume encrypted." Unlike GuardDuty (behavioral threat detection), Config is about configuration drift: a resource that was compliant yesterday and isn't today, regardless of whether anyone is actively attacking it. Non-compliant findings can trigger automated remediation via Systems Manager Automation — e.g. automatically re-attaching the public-access block the moment someone removes it, closing the gap before a human even sees the alert.

ServiceAnswersInput
CloudTrailWhat API call happened, by whom, when?Every API call, raw
GuardDutyIs something actively malicious happening right now?CloudTrail + VPC Flow Logs + DNS logs + ML/threat intel
ConfigIs this resource's configuration still compliant?Resource configuration snapshots + Config Rules
Security HubWhat's our overall security posture, in one view?GuardDuty + Config + Inspector + partner findings, aggregated

13 Application-layer security: OWASP risks, mapped to ShopSphere

Every control above operates at the infrastructure or network layer. None of them fixes a vulnerability written into ShopSphere's own application code — that's squarely application-layer security, and it's where a large share of real breaches actually happen.

OWASP Top 10 (2021) categoryShopSphere scenarioPrimary fix
A03: InjectionA crafted string in the product-search box (/search?q=) is concatenated directly into a SQL or OpenSearch queryParameterized queries / query builders in application code — WAF (Section 03) is a second layer, not the fix
A07: Identification & Authentication FailuresThe login endpoint has no rate limiting or account lockout, enabling credential-stuffing against reused passwordsMFA, rate limiting, breached-password checks — covered concretely with Cognito in Module 17
A10: Server-Side Request Forgery (SSRF)A "fetch product image from URL" seller feature is tricked into requesting http://169.254.169.254/... (the EC2 instance metadata endpoint) instead of an imageAllow-list outbound destinations, use IMDSv2 (token-required), isolate the fetcher in a network path with no route to internal metadata endpoints

Why SSRF is dangerous enough to name explicitly

A successful SSRF against the instance metadata service can return the temporary credentials for whatever IAM role that instance is running — turning one unvalidated URL field into a stolen set of AWS credentials, entirely bypassing every network control in this module. IMDSv2 (session-token-required metadata requests) closes the easiest version of this attack and should be enforced account-wide, not left as an opt-in instance setting.

14 Hands-on lab

  1. Write the Web ACL rule list (in order) you'd attach to ShopSphere's public ALB: name each managed rule group you'd include and the one custom rate-based rule, and explain why order matters in a Web ACL.
  2. Diagram the envelope-encryption flow for a new ShopSphere feature that stores scanned ID documents for seller verification: which KMS key, which service calls GenerateDataKey, and where the wrapped data key is stored relative to the ciphertext.
  3. GuardDuty raises a finding: an IAM role normally used only by the Recommendation service's Lambda function just called s3:GetObject against the Order service's private bucket, from an AWS region ShopSphere has never deployed to. Walk through which of the five layers in Section 02 should have caught this earlier, and why they didn't.

15 Interview questions

WAF blocked the attack. Why does ShopSphere still need to fix the application code?
WAF managed rules match known attack signatures — they're a probabilistic filter, not a proof of safety. A sufficiently novel or obfuscated injection payload can bypass a signature match entirely, and WAF provides no protection at all for a vulnerability it was never configured to look for. Parameterized queries eliminate the injection class of vulnerability structurally, at the source; WAF reduces the volume of attempts that ever get that far. Defense in depth means treating WAF as a reduction in risk, never as the fix itself.
Why does KMS use envelope encryption instead of encrypting data directly with the KMS key?
KMS keys are deliberately capped at encrypting small payloads (a few KB) and every direct KMS encrypt/decrypt call is a network round trip to the KMS service, which doesn't scale to encrypting gigabytes of data efficiently. Envelope encryption does the expensive bulk-data encryption locally and fast with a data key, and only ever sends the small, already-generated data key to KMS to be wrapped or unwrapped — getting KMS's centralized key management and access control without paying a network round trip per byte of data.
What's the difference between what GuardDuty and AWS Config each catch, and why do you need both?
GuardDuty is behavioral: it looks for signs of active compromise in logs and network traffic — an attacker doing something. Config is structural: it checks whether a resource's current configuration matches a defined compliant state — a misconfiguration existing, regardless of whether anyone is exploiting it yet. A public S3 bucket with no traffic against it yet triggers a Config finding but no GuardDuty finding; an attacker actively exfiltrating data through a correctly-configured but compromised credential triggers GuardDuty but no Config finding. Each catches a failure mode the other is blind to.

Level 4 · Production Architecture

Identity: Cognito, OAuth2, OIDC, JWT

● ~26 min read● Builds on Module 16● ShopSphere: logs its first customer in● AWS service

Bottom line: Module 03's IAM Identity Center answers "which engineer can touch which AWS resource" — workforce identity. This module answers a completely different question: "which shopper is this, and what are they allowed to do in ShopSphere" — customer identity. They are separate systems solving separate problems, and conflating them is a common, costly design mistake.

01 Authentication vs. authorization, precisely

Authentication answers "who are you" — proving an identity, typically via a password, MFA code, or federated login. Authorization answers "what are you allowed to do" — given a known identity, which resources and actions are permitted. A customer authenticating successfully to ShopSphere says nothing on its own about whether they're allowed to view a specific order that isn't theirs — that's a separate authorization check, every time, on every request.

02 Workforce identity vs. customer identity — two different systems, on purpose

Don't reach for IAM Identity Center here

Module 03's IAM Identity Center manages ShopSphere's engineers signing into the AWS console and AWS APIs — a small, trusted population, provisioned by an admin, governed by IAM policies. ShopSphere's shoppers are a different population entirely: millions of untrusted strangers, self-registering, who need to authenticate to the application, not to AWS itself. That's customer identity and access management (CIAM), and on AWS it's a different service — Amazon Cognito — built for exactly this scale and threat model.

Workforce identity (Module 03)Customer identity (this module)
WhoShopSphere's engineers and operatorsShopSphere's shoppers
AWS mechanismIAM Identity CenterAmazon Cognito
Authenticates againstAWS itself (console, CLI, APIs)ShopSphere's own application
ScaleDozens to low thousandsMillions, self-service sign-up
Trust modelPre-provisioned, vettedUntrusted until proven otherwise

03 Amazon Cognito User Pools

A Cognito User Pool is a managed customer directory: it handles sign-up, sign-in, password policies, email/phone verification, and MFA (SMS, TOTP authenticator apps, or email OTP) for ShopSphere's shoppers, without ShopSphere ever storing a password itself. It ships a customizable Hosted UI (a Cognito-hosted login page ShopSphere can skin) and supports federation — letting a shopper sign in with Google or Apple instead of creating a new password, through the same pool. On successful authentication, the User Pool issues JWTs (Section 08): an ID token, an access token, and a refresh token.

04 Amazon Cognito Identity Pools

A Cognito Identity Pool (also called Federated Identities) solves a different problem: once a shopper is authenticated, how do they get temporary AWS credentials to call an AWS service directly — without ShopSphere's backend being in the middle of every request? An Identity Pool exchanges a validated token (from a User Pool, a social login, or another IdP) for short-lived AWS credentials via STS, scoped to an IAM role, exactly like Module 03's EC2 instance role, just assumed by an end-user identity instead of a compute resource.

ShopSphere's concrete use case

A logged-in customer uploads a profile photo. Routing that file through ShopSphere's own backend just to forward it to S3 wastes backend capacity on a pure pass-through. Instead, the client exchanges its Cognito User Pool token for temporary credentials via an Identity Pool, scoped by an IAM role to s3:PutObject on exactly that customer's own prefix in the profile-photos bucket — and uploads directly to S3, bypassing the backend entirely while IAM still enforces exactly what that one customer can touch.

User Pools answer "who is this shopper, inside my application." Identity Pools answer "what can this already-authenticated shopper do directly against AWS." Most ShopSphere requests only need the first; the direct-S3-upload pattern is the clean case for the second.

05 OAuth2, in plain language

OAuth2 is an authorization framework (not authentication, by design) built around four roles: the resource owner (the shopper), the client (ShopSphere's web or mobile app), the authorization server (Cognito), and the resource server (ShopSphere's API). Its purpose is letting the client get limited, delegated access without ever handling the shopper's actual password itself. The authorization code flow is the right grant type for ShopSphere's web app — a confidential client that can keep a secret, as opposed to a pure single-page app or mobile app, which should add PKCE on top.

06 The authorization code flow, end to end

  1. The client redirects the shopper's browser to Cognito's /oauth2/authorize endpoint with the client ID and redirect URI.
  2. The shopper authenticates on Cognito's Hosted UI — ShopSphere's own servers never see the password.
  3. Cognito redirects back to ShopSphere with a short-lived, single-use authorization code in the URL.
  4. ShopSphere's backend exchanges that code, server-side, at Cognito's /oauth2/token endpoint for an ID token, access token and refresh token — this exchange never happens in the browser, so the tokens never transit through client-side JavaScript or browser history.
  5. Every subsequent API call includes the access token as a Bearer token; API Gateway's built-in Cognito authorizer validates it before the request ever reaches application code.

07 OIDC: identity, layered on top of OAuth2's authorization

OAuth2 on its own only standardizes delegated access — it never formally defines what "being logged in" means. OpenID Connect (OIDC) is a thin identity layer on top of OAuth2 that adds exactly that: a standardized ID token (always a JWT) carrying the shopper's identity claims, plus a /userinfo endpoint. The distinction that trips people up: the access token authorizes calling an API (it's a credential, not meant to be inspected by the client); the ID token asserts who the user is (it's meant to be read by the client, never sent to an API as a Bearer token). Cognito issues both from a single login.

08 Inside a JWT: header, payload, signature

A JSON Web Token (JWT) is three base64url-encoded segments joined by dots: header.payload.signature. Anyone can decode the header and payload — they are not encrypted, only encoded — so a JWT must never carry a secret in its payload. What makes it trustworthy is the third segment: a cryptographic signature over the first two, computed with a key only the issuer (Cognito) controls.

SegmentContainsShopSphere example
HeaderSigning algorithm (alg) and key id (kid){"alg":"RS256","kid":"abc123"}
PayloadClaims — sub (subject/user id), iss (issuer), aud (audience), exp (expiry), custom claims{"sub":"cust_8821","iss":"https://cognito-idp...","exp":1735700000}
SignatureHMAC or RSA/ECDSA signature over header+payload, using the issuer's keyVerified against Cognito's public signing key, fetched from its JWKS endpoint

The mistake that breaks everything: trusting the decoded payload without verifying the signature

Because the payload is only base64-encoded, not encrypted, it is trivial for any client — including an attacker — to decode a JWT and edit its claims ("sub":"cust_8821" → "sub":"cust_0001") before sending it on. A service that decodes a JWT and reads its claims without cryptographically verifying the signature against the issuer's public key first will accept that forged token as genuine. Every ShopSphere service that consumes a JWT must verify the signature, the issuer, the audience and the expiry — every single time — before trusting a single claim inside it.

09 Propagating authorization across microservices

As ShopSphere's Order, Payment and Inventory domains split into separate services (Module 19 onward), a naive design has every downstream service independently re-validate the shopper's login — re-checking the signature, calling Cognito again, repeating work on every hop. The better pattern: validate once, at the edge, and propagate trust forward. API Gateway's authorizer validates the JWT's signature and claims exactly once, at the front door; the validated claims (customer id, scopes) are then passed downstream — either as the same JWT (each service still verifies the signature cheaply, since it's a local cryptographic check, not a network call to Cognito) or exchanged for a short-lived internal service token scoped to that one request.

What this does not mean

"Trust the gateway's validation" is not the same as "stop verifying signatures downstream." Internal services should still verify the JWT's signature locally (a fast, offline cryptographic check against a cached public key) rather than blindly trusting an internal header that claims "already validated" — a header is just a string an attacker who's gotten past one hop can also set. What's eliminated is the network round trip back to Cognito on every hop, not signature verification itself.

10 Token lifecycle: expiry, refresh, revocation

Cognito access and ID tokens are short-lived by design (commonly minutes to an hour); the longer-lived refresh token lets the client silently obtain a new access token without forcing the shopper to log in again, up to the refresh token's own, much longer expiry. This bounds the damage from a leaked access token to its short lifetime, at the cost of needing a refresh flow in the client. True immediate revocation (e.g. "sign this customer out everywhere, right now, because their account was compromised") requires explicitly revoking the refresh token and, for standing sessions, a server-side check — a JWT's signature being valid does not by itself mean the session hasn't been revoked, since the token keeps verifying successfully until it naturally expires.

11 Hands-on lab

  1. Decode (by hand, using any base64url decoder) a sample JWT's header and payload, and identify the alg, exp, and sub claims. Explain, concretely, why editing the payload and re-encoding it would make the token fail verification.
  2. Design the IAM role and its trust/permission policy for ShopSphere's direct-to-S3 profile-photo upload (Section 04), scoped so customer cust_8821 can only write under profile-photos/cust_8821/*.
  3. ShopSphere's Order service receives a request carrying a JWT whose signature is valid, issuer is correct, but exp is 10 minutes in the past. Should it accept the request? Justify your answer, then describe the client-side experience that should follow a correct rejection.

12 Interview questions

Why are User Pools and Identity Pools two separate Cognito concepts instead of one?
They solve different problems. User Pools are an identity directory and authentication service — they answer "is this a real, authenticated shopper, and who are they." Identity Pools are a credential-exchange service — they answer "given an already-authenticated identity (from a User Pool or elsewhere), what temporary AWS credentials should it get." Most requests only need the first. Conflating them would force every authenticated session to also carry AWS credentials it usually doesn't need, widening the blast radius of a leaked token for no benefit.
A JWT's payload shows "role":"admin". Can your service trust that claim?
Only after independently verifying the token's signature against the issuer's actual public signing key, confirming the issuer and audience match what's expected, and confirming it hasn't expired. The payload is base64-encoded, not encrypted or tamper-proof on its own — anyone can decode and rewrite it. The signature is what proves the claims haven't been altered since the issuer signed them; skipping that check and trusting the decoded payload directly is the single most common JWT implementation mistake.
Why does OIDC exist if OAuth2 already has access tokens?
OAuth2 was designed purely for delegated authorization — letting a client access a resource on a user's behalf — and deliberately left "what does being logged in mean" unspecified; access tokens are opaque-by-convention credentials, not meant to carry identity claims a client should parse. OIDC adds a standardized ID token (always a JWT, always meant to be read by the client) plus a consistent way to fetch profile information, turning OAuth2's authorization mechanics into a complete authentication protocol on top.

IETF · standardRFC 7519 — JSON Web Token (JWT)The canonical specification for JWT structure, claims and signing.rfc-editor.org ↗
