Level 4 · Production Architecture

Caching Strategies: CloudFront & ElastiCache

● ~28 min read● Builds on Module 17● ShopSphere: stops hitting the database for everything

Bottom line: a cache is a bet that recently-read data will be read again soon, traded against the risk of serving data that's gone stale. ShopSphere stacks three independent cache layers between a shopper and the database — each one a separate bet, with its own TTL, its own invalidation story, and its own failure mode when the bet is wrong.

01 Three layers, one request path

LayerAWS serviceCachesTypical latency
1 — EdgeCloudFrontWhole HTTP responses, close to the shopperSingle-digit ms, often no backend hop at all
2 — ApplicationElastiCache (Redis)Computed/assembled data the app would otherwise rebuild per requestSub-millisecond, in the same region/VPC
3 — Source of truthAurora / DynamoDBNothing cached — the authoritative dataSingle-digit to tens of ms, plus query cost

02 Layer 1 — CloudFront edge caching, recapped and deepened

Module 02 introduced CloudFront as "content close to the shopper." As a cache, it works through a cache policy: which parts of the request (headers, cookies, query strings) form the cache key, and a TTL (minimum/default/maximum) controlling how long an object is served from an edge location before CloudFront re-checks the origin. It's the first and cheapest layer precisely because a hit here means ShopSphere's application, ElastiCache and database never see the request at all — product images, CSS/JS bundles, and even cacheable API responses (e.g. a public, rarely-changing product-listing page) are served entirely from the edge.

Invalidating a CloudFront cache entry early (before its TTL expires) is a real, billed operation and not instant across every edge location — which is why ShopSphere favors short, deliberate TTLs plus versioned object keys (product-images/sku123-v2.jpg) for anything that changes, over relying on manual invalidation as the normal workflow.

03 Layer 2 — ElastiCache application caching

ElastiCache for Redis is an in-memory data store ShopSphere's application talks to directly, sitting between the application and Aurora/DynamoDB. Where CloudFront caches whole HTTP responses for anonymous, cacheable traffic, ElastiCache caches whatever the application chooses, at whatever granularity it chooses — a single product's assembled catalog entry, a customer's shopping cart, a session, a computed recommendation list — and it's just as useful for personalized, non-cacheable-at-the-edge requests as for public ones.

04 Three caching patterns

PatternHow it worksTrade-off
Cache-aside (lazy loading)App checks cache first; on a miss, reads the database, then writes the result into the cache before returning itSimple, resilient to cache data loss (just rebuilds on next read) — but the first request after any miss pays full database latency
Write-throughEvery write goes to the cache and the database together, synchronouslyCache is never stale after a write — but every write now pays the latency of both, and data the app never reads still gets cached
Write-behind (write-back)Writes go to the cache immediately and are flushed to the database asynchronously, in the backgroundFastest writes — but a cache failure before the flush completes means a real, committed-looking write is actually lost

ShopSphere's default: cache-aside

Cache-aside is the pattern ShopSphere reaches for by default, for exactly one reason: it fails safe. If the ElastiCache cluster is flushed, restarted, or simply never warmed, cache-aside degrades to "every read hits the database," which is slow but correct. Write-behind's failure mode is losing data that looked committed — a much worse trade for most of ShopSphere's data, and reserved only for cases explicitly designed to tolerate it.

05 TTLs and invalidation

A TTL (time-to-live) is the simplest invalidation strategy: data simply expires after a fixed window, no matter what. It's easy to reason about and needs no explicit invalidation logic — but it means ShopSphere is always serving data that is, at worst, exactly one TTL window stale. Event-driven invalidation trades that simplicity for freshness: when the underlying data actually changes, the writer explicitly deletes or updates the corresponding cache key immediately, rather than waiting for a TTL to lapse. The right choice is specific to what's being cached — which is exactly why Sections 09–12 treat ShopSphere's domains differently rather than applying one caching policy everywhere.

06 Cache stampede

A cache stampede (also called a dog-piling problem) happens when a single, heavily-requested key expires, and a sudden burst of concurrent requests all miss the cache at once — and all independently hammer the database to recompute the same value simultaneously, exactly when the database is least prepared for a spike. It's a caching-layer failure that manifests as a database-layer outage.

MitigationHow it works
Jittered TTLAdd a small random offset to each key's TTL (e.g. 300s ± 30s) so many keys don't expire at exactly the same instant
Request coalescing / mutex lockThe first request to miss acquires a short-lived lock and recomputes; concurrent requests wait briefly or serve stale data instead of each independently hitting the database
Early refreshProactively recompute a hot key slightly before its TTL expires, so it's never actually missing when traffic arrives

ShopSphere's flash-sale scenario

The featured flash-sale product's catalog entry is, by definition, the single most-requested key in the system at that exact moment. Letting its cache entry expire naturally, with no jitter and no lock, is precisely the setup for a stampede that takes Aurora down right when ShopSphere can least afford it — this is a real, named failure mode, not a hypothetical.

07 Hot keys

A hot key is a single cache key receiving disproportionate traffic — the viral product everyone is suddenly looking at. Unlike a stampede (a burst at expiry), a hot key is a sustained problem: Redis serves a given key from one specific shard, so an extremely hot key can saturate that one shard's network or CPU capacity while the rest of the cluster sits idle, no matter how large the cluster is overall. Mitigations include client-side (in-process) caching of the hottest keys for a few seconds to shed load before it even reaches Redis, or explicitly replicating a known-hot key across multiple shard-local copies.

08 Stale data risk

Every cache is a trade against freshness by construction. The question that actually matters isn't "is this cache ever stale" (it always, structurally, can be) — it's "what's the cost of this specific piece of data being stale for this specific TTL window." That cost is wildly different across ShopSphere's domains, which is exactly why one blanket caching policy is the wrong design.

09 Applied: product catalog

A product's title, description and images change rarely. ShopSphere caches catalog entries cache-aside, with a medium TTL (minutes, not seconds) — the cost of a shopper seeing a product description that's a few minutes out of date is close to zero, and the read volume savings against Aurora are large, since the same handful of popular products are requested constantly.

10 Applied: pricing

Stale prices are a business and legal risk, not just a UX nuisance

Showing a shopper a price that's already changed — especially showing an old, lower price after an increase — creates real customer-trust and, in some jurisdictions, legal exposure around honoring advertised prices. Pricing therefore gets either a deliberately short TTL (seconds, not minutes) or, where ShopSphere can wire it up, event-driven invalidation: the Pricing service explicitly invalidates a product's cached price the moment it changes, rather than waiting out a TTL window at all. This is a case where the "cost of staleness" calculus from Section 08 comes out completely differently from the catalog.

11 Applied: inventory availability

Showing "in stock" on a product that just sold out is a direct, visible customer-experience failure — a shopper adds it to cart, reaches checkout, and the order fails. Inventory availability therefore needs careful, event-driven invalidation tied to the actual stock-level change (an order being placed, a return being processed), not a TTL alone; a TTL can be layered on top as a safety net for the rare case an invalidation event is missed, but it should not be the primary mechanism for data this sensitive to being wrong.

12 Applied: sessions and cart state

ShopSphere's checkout fleet runs multiple EC2 instances behind an ALB (Module 05), and a given shopper's requests can land on any of them. Storing cart state in one instance's local memory means the cart effectively disappears the moment a request is routed to a different instance — the classic failure of treating a horizontally-scaled, stateless-by-design fleet as if it had sticky local state. ShopSphere stores logged-in cart and session state in ElastiCache Redis, shared across every instance, so any instance can serve any shopper's request and see the same cart — the concrete, worked reason Module 01's "stateless architecture" principle matters, not an abstraction.

Why not just use cookies alone?

A client-side cookie can hold a session identifier safely, but holding the full cart contents client-side invites tampering (a shopper editing their own cookie to change a price or quantity) and bloats every request with data the server needs to re-verify anyway. ElastiCache holds the authoritative cart server-side; the cookie just carries the session id that looks it up — exactly the separation Module 17's JWT discussion made for identity claims, applied here to cart state.

13 Hands-on lab

  1. For each of ShopSphere's catalog, pricing, inventory and session data, write down: cache-aside/write-through/write-behind, TTL (or "event-driven"), and the single worst consequence of that data being stale for one extra minute.
  2. Design a mitigation for a specific flash-sale stampede: 50,000 requests arrive in the same second for a product whose cache entry just expired. Specify exactly what happens on request #1 versus requests #2 through #50,000.
  3. A product goes viral and its cache key becomes a hot key, saturating one Redis shard while the rest of the cluster is idle. Propose two distinct mitigations and explain the trade-off each one makes.

14 Interview questions

Why does ShopSphere default to cache-aside instead of write-through, given write-through keeps the cache always fresh?
Cache-aside fails safe: if the cache is lost or never populated, the system degrades to slower-but-correct database reads. Write-through fails by caching data on every write whether or not it's ever read again, paying write latency on both systems for no guaranteed benefit, and it still doesn't protect against a cache outage the way cache-aside's natural fallback does. Cache-aside is the right default; write-through earns its place only where read-after-write freshness is specifically required.
What's the actual difference between a cache stampede and a hot key?
A stampede is a burst, time-bound event: many concurrent requests all miss the same key at once, typically right after it expires, and all hit the database simultaneously. A hot key is sustained, ongoing disproportionate traffic to one key that's still cached and being served correctly, but concentrated on a single Redis shard regardless of overall cluster size. A stampede is solved by controlling expiry (jitter, locks, early refresh); a hot key is solved by spreading or absorbing load (client-side caching, shard-local replication), since the data isn't actually missing from the cache at all.
Why can't ShopSphere just store the shopping cart in the browser cookie and skip ElastiCache entirely?
A cookie is client-controlled, so cart contents stored there directly can be tampered with by the shopper before the next request — changing a price or quantity client-side. It also doesn't solve the underlying problem: the server still needs an authoritative, verifiable version of the cart to validate against at checkout. Keeping cart state server-side in ElastiCache, shared across every checkout instance, with only a session identifier in the cookie, keeps the authoritative data under the server's control while still working correctly behind a load-balanced, horizontally-scaled fleet.

Level 4 · Production Architecture

Scalability: From 1,000 to 10,000,000 Users

● ~26 min read● Builds on Module 18● ShopSphere: redesigned four times, on purpose

Bottom line: ShopSphere is redesigned four times in this module, once per order of magnitude of traffic — and each redesign exists because a specific, nameable bottleneck in the previous architecture would otherwise break production. The skill this module teaches isn't "how to scale," it's knowing which bottleneck you're actually fighting at your current scale, so you don't pay for Scenario 4's complexity while still running Scenario 1's traffic.

01 The capacity-planning mindset: don't over-engineer

The most expensive mistake in this module isn't under-scaling — it's a team building Scenario 4's queue-based, multi-region, sharded architecture for a ShopSphere pilot doing 1,000 orders a day. Every layer of resilience and scale has an operational cost: more services to monitor, more failure modes to reason about, more engineers needed to run it. The right architecture is the simplest one that meets today's actual (and near-term forecast) load, with a clear, named next step for when a specific metric crosses a specific threshold — not the most impressive one on a diagram.

The rule this module follows

At each scenario below, we name the exact bottleneck that would break the previous scenario's architecture under the new load, and add nothing more than that. We don't add CloudFront in Scenario 2 "because it's best practice" — we add it in Scenario 3 because, by then, origin fetches for product images are the thing actually falling over.

02 Scenario 1 — 1,000 users/day

1,000 users/day is roughly 0.01 requests/second averaged across a full day — even with a generous 10× daytime peak and 20 requests per user session (browsing, cart, checkout), that's under 2–3 requests/second at the busiest minute. This is firmly inside what a single small server can do without strain.

LayerScenario 1 architecture
Compute1× EC2 instance (e.g. t4g.small), single AZ
Database1× RDS instance (e.g. db.t4g.micro), single-AZ, no read replica
Static assetsServed directly from the app or a bucket, no CDN
DeployManual or a simple script — no pipeline needed yet

This is honestly fine

A single EC2 instance and a single-AZ RDS instance is a completely legitimate production architecture at this scale. It has an availability ceiling (Module 26 covers exactly why), but for a pilot or an early-stage store, spending engineering time on Multi-AZ failover before there's revenue to protect is effort better spent on product. The bottleneck here isn't technical — it's that this architecture has no path to survive a single EC2 instance or AZ failing without downtime. That's acceptable now; it's the first thing Scenario 2 fixes.

03 Scenario 2 — 100,000 users/day

100,000 users/day with the same assumptions pushes average load to roughly 1–2 requests/second, with realistic peaks (lunch hour, evening browsing) in the 100–200 requests/second range. That's past what one instance safely absorbs, and the Scenario 1 architecture's real problem isn't raw capacity yet — it's that a single instance and a single-AZ database are each a single point of failure, and at this volume an outage has real customers and real revenue behind it.

LayerScenario 2 architectureWhy
ComputeApplication Load Balancer + Auto Scaling Group, 2–4 instances across 2+ AZsNo single instance failure takes down checkout; capacity tracks load automatically
DatabaseMulti-AZ RDS (synchronous standby) + 1 read replicaAutomatic failover on primary failure; the read replica absorbs product-catalog browsing, which at this traffic level is 80–90% of all database reads and was starting to contend with checkout writes on the same primary
Static assetsStill origin-served — not yet a bottleneckTraffic volume doesn't justify CloudFront's added layer yet

The bottleneck that forces Scenario 3: read replicas and a bigger fleet scale linearly — add more of the same thing. That stops working once request volume is high enough that even one more relational replica can't keep up with catalog read volume, and once the checkout API's habit of calling Payment, Inventory and Notification synchronously, inline, means checkout latency is the sum of four services' latencies instead of one.

04 Scenario 3 — 10,000,000 users/day

10,000,000 users/day, same assumptions, is roughly 2,000–2,500 requests/second average and 10,000–20,000 requests/second at peak. At this volume, three separate things break at once, and each needs a different fix — this is the scenario that forces the biggest architectural jump in the module.

What breaksFixWhy this fix specifically
Origin fleet serves every product image/page request itselfCloudFront in front of the ALB and product-image bucketMost catalog/product-page traffic is cacheable and repeat — serving it from edge removes the majority of requests from the origin entirely
Even a cached relational replica can't absorb catalog + pricing read volume at this QPSElastiCache (Redis) in front of product/pricing reads, cache-asideSub-millisecond reads for hot keys (bestsellers, flash-sale items) instead of a database round-trip per request
Cart reads/writes are simple key-based lookups but at huge volume against a relational tableMove Cart to DynamoDBCart's access pattern (get/put by cart ID, no joins) is exactly what a key-value store is built for — and it scales horizontally without a single relational primary as a ceiling
Checkout calls Payment, Inventory, Notification, Shipment, Analytics synchronously — one slow dependency stalls checkout for everyoneCheckout writes an Order Created event to SQS/SNS/EventBridge and returns immediately; downstream services consume asynchronouslyCheckout latency becomes "write one order, publish one event" — not the sum of five services' response times
A single Aurora writer approaches its write-IOPS/connection ceilingPartition/shard write-heavy tables (e.g. Orders by date range or customer hash) and evaluate Aurora read scalingHorizontal write scaling only becomes necessary once a single writer is demonstrably the limit — this is the one line item in this table you should defer until you've actually hit it

Don't skip straight here from Scenario 1

Every row in that table is a direct answer to a bottleneck that only exists at this volume. Introducing DynamoDB for Cart or sharding Orders before there's 10M users/day worth of traffic adds real operational complexity (two data stores to keep consistent, eventual-consistency edge cases, a sharding key that's painful to change later) for a problem you don't have yet.

The bottleneck that forces Scenario 4: everything above assumes demand arrives smoothly enough for Auto Scaling, cache warmup and connection pools to keep pace. A flash sale's demand curve isn't smooth — it's a near-vertical spike.

05 Scenario 4 — flash sale, 1,000,000 concurrent users

A flash sale doesn't arrive as a gradual ramp Auto Scaling can track — hundreds of thousands of customers hit "Buy" in the same handful of seconds the sale opens. Auto Scaling Groups take roughly one to a few minutes to launch and warm new instances; a database has an absolute ceiling on concurrent connections regardless of instance size. No amount of "add more capacity" fixes a spike that outruns how fast capacity can be added. The fix is architectural, not just bigger: admit load at a rate the backend can sustain, and make everyone else wait safely.

TechniqueWhat it doesShopSphere application
Queue-based load levelingEvery checkout attempt is placed on a queue (SQS) instead of hitting the database directly; a fixed, tuned pool of checkout workers drains it at a sustainable rateThe database only ever sees the request rate the workers are configured to sustain — never the raw rate customers are arriving at
BackpressureWhen the queue grows beyond a threshold, new requests get a fast, explicit "try again" response instead of being silently accepted and left to time outCheckout returns HTTP 429 with a Retry-After header rather than hanging until the connection pool is exhausted
Per-customer rate limitingCaps how many requests any single customer/session can make per secondStops a single retry-storming client, bot, or scalper script from consuming a disproportionate share of the sustainable admission rate
Virtual waiting roomCustomers arriving faster than checkout can sustainably admit are held on a cheap, static, token-based holding page and let in on a scheduleA million idle "your position: 42,318" polling connections are far cheaper to hold than a million simultaneous attempts to write to Aurora

The architecture error this scenario exists to prevent

Pointing all flash-sale traffic directly at checkout and "scaling the database bigger" treats this as a capacity problem. It isn't — it's a rate problem. A database sized for 50,000 simultaneous connection attempts still falls over against 1,000,000 simultaneous attempts; the fix is controlling the rate at which attempts reach it, not the size of what receives them.

06 The four architectures, side by side

TransitionBottleneck that forces it
Scenario 1 → 2Single instance and single-AZ database are each a single point of failure once real revenue depends on uptime
Scenario 2 → 3One relational read replica can't absorb 10M/day's catalog read volume; synchronous checkout fan-out makes checkout latency the sum of five services
Scenario 3 → 4Auto Scaling and connection pools can't react faster than a flash sale's near-vertical demand spike arrives

07 Hands-on lab

  1. ShopSphere is doing 8,000 users/day and growing steadily. Using the bottleneck table above, state which scenario's architecture it should be on today, and which specific metric you'd watch to know when it's time to move to the next one.
  2. Estimate: if ShopSphere's flash sale admits checkout attempts from its queue at a sustained 2,000/second, roughly how long does it take to drain a backlog of 1,000,000 queued attempts — and what does that tell you about setting customers' expectations in the waiting room UI?
  3. A teammate proposes moving Cart to DynamoDB and adding ElastiCache right now, while ShopSphere is still at Scenario 2 traffic. Write the one-paragraph case against doing it yet, using this module's own argument against over-engineering.

08 Interview questions

Why doesn't "just add more EC2 instances and a bigger database" solve the flash-sale scenario?
Because the problem isn't steady-state capacity, it's the rate at which demand arrives. Auto Scaling takes real time (roughly a minute or more) to launch and warm new capacity, and a database has a hard ceiling on concurrent connections regardless of instance size. A demand spike that arrives faster than either of those can react will overwhelm any fixed amount of capacity; the fix is to control the admission rate (queue-based load leveling, a waiting room) rather than to keep raising the capacity ceiling.
A team scaled straight from a single EC2 instance to Scenario 3's full architecture (CloudFront, ElastiCache, DynamoDB, async fan-out) for a store doing 2,000 orders/day. What's the architectural critique?
That's premature complexity for the load involved. Each of those pieces exists to solve a specific bottleneck that only appears at much higher volume (origin overload, cache-miss read volume, a single relational writer's ceiling, synchronous fan-out latency). At 2,000 orders/day, none of those bottlenecks exist yet, but the operational cost (more systems to monitor, more failure modes, eventual-consistency edge cases between two data stores) is being paid regardless. The right move is Scenario 1 or 2's architecture, with the Scenario 3 bottlenecks named as the trigger for when to revisit it.
Why does moving Cart to DynamoDB make sense before sharding the Orders table, even though both are "scale the database" moves?
They solve different problems at different urgency. Cart's access pattern (simple get/put by cart ID, no joins, extremely high read/write volume, tolerant of eventual consistency) is a near-ideal fit for a key-value store regardless of scale, so moving it early removes load from the relational primary with low risk. Sharding Orders changes the primary system of record for a domain with strict consistency and transactional requirements (payments, inventory) and is operationally expensive to reverse once chosen — it should be deferred until a single writer is demonstrably the actual ceiling, not applied preemptively.

Level 4 · Production Architecture

Performance Engineering & Load Testing

● ~24 min read● Builds on Module 19● ShopSphere: sets a performance budget for checkout

Bottom line: you cannot improve what you haven't precisely measured, and "average latency" is the single most misleading number in production engineering — it hides exactly the slow requests that matter most. This module gives ShopSphere a vocabulary for measuring performance correctly, a way to tell different failure signatures apart, and a concrete performance budget for the checkout API.

01 Four measurements that get confused

These four terms are used loosely in casual conversation and precisely in any real performance investigation — conflating them is the fastest way to misdiagnose a production issue.

TermPrecise meaningShopSphere example
LatencyTime for one request to complete, end to end. Always reported as a distribution (percentiles), never a single numberA single POST /checkout call takes 140ms
ThroughputTotal amount of work the system completes per unit time, system-wideCheckout handles 1,800 requests/second during a peak
QPS / TPSQueries Per Second / Transactions Per Second — throughput scoped to a specific unit of workAurora handles 40,000 QPS; checkout completes 500 order-placement TPS
ConcurrencyThe number of requests in flight at the same instant — not the same as throughput5,000 checkout requests are open simultaneously, but only 500/second are completing — a sign requests are stuck, not merely numerous

High concurrency with low throughput is a symptom, not a feature

If concurrency is climbing while throughput stays flat, requests are piling up faster than they're finishing — something downstream (a database, a dependency, a connection pool) is the bottleneck, and adding more application servers won't fix it, because the new servers will fill up with stuck requests too.

02 The classic bug: a new database connection per request

This is the single most common way a service "mysteriously" falls over at moderate load, long before CPU looks stressed. Opening a database connection means a TCP handshake, (usually) a TLS negotiation, and a database-side authentication handshake — tens of milliseconds of pure overhead before a single query runs. Worse, the database has a hard ceiling on concurrent connections (an RDS/Aurora instance might allow a few hundred to a few thousand, depending on size) that has nothing to do with CPU. An app that opens a fresh connection per incoming request exhausts that ceiling at a request rate far below what the hardware could otherwise handle — new requests fail to even acquire a connection, while CPU on both the app and the database sits nearly idle.

// Anti-pattern: a new, unauthenticated connection for every request
function handleCheckout(req) {
  const conn = createNewDBConnection(); // TCP + TLS + DB auth, every single call
  const order = conn.query("INSERT INTO orders ...");
  conn.close();
  return order;
}

// Fix: a pool of already-authenticated connections, created once at startup
const pool = createConnectionPool({ min: 5, max: 50 });
function handleCheckout(req) {
  const conn = pool.acquire(); // reuses an existing connection
  const order = conn.query("INSERT INTO orders ...");
  pool.release(conn);
  return order;
}

At ShopSphere's Scenario 3/4 scale (Module 19), the pool itself can still be too small, or a burst of Lambda concurrency can each open their own pool and collectively still exhaust the database — which is exactly the problem Amazon RDS Proxy solves, by multiplexing many application-side connections onto a smaller, managed pool of actual database connections.

03 Four failure signatures — and how to tell them apart

"The site is slow" has at least four distinct underlying causes, each with a different fix. Misreading the signature wastes an incident entirely — adding application servers to a database bottleneck does nothing but add more stuck requests.

SignatureWhat you observeConfirm withFix
CPU saturationLatency degrades gradually as load rises; CPU metric sits near 100%CloudWatch CPUUtilization on app instancesScale out, or optimize the hot code path
Memory pressureLatency looks fine, then falls off a cliff — GC pauses lengthen, then an instance is OOM-killed and restartsCloudWatch agent MemoryUtilization; rising GC pause time; OOM entries in system logsFix leaks, right-size instance memory, reduce working set via caching
Network bottleneckThroughput plateaus well below what CPU/memory would allow, especially with large payloadsNetworkIn/NetworkOut near the instance type's bandwidth ceilingCompress responses, offload to CloudFront, choose a larger instance type
Database bottleneckApp CPU and memory look healthy, but request latency and timeouts climb anywayRDS/Aurora CPUUtilization, DatabaseConnections near max, Performance Insights top wait eventsAdd a read replica, add caching (Module 18), tune the query, pool connections via RDS Proxy

04 Load testing vs stress testing vs soak testing

These three answer three different questions, and running only one tells you nothing about the other two.

Test typeQuestion it answersHow it's run
Load testingDoes the system meet its performance budget under expected traffic?Simulate realistic peak traffic (e.g. ShopSphere's historical Saturday-evening peak) for a representative duration
Stress testingWhere is the actual breaking point, and how does the system fail once past it?Deliberately push load well beyond expected peak until something gives, watching whether failure is graceful (clean 429s, shed load) or catastrophic (cascading timeouts, data corruption)
Soak testingWhat breaks slowly, over hours the other two tests are too short to reveal?Run a moderate, sustained load for many hours (sometimes days) — surfaces memory leaks, connection leaks, disk fill-up, slow clock drift

ShopSphere's pre-flash-sale routine

Before a scheduled flash sale, ShopSphere runs all three: a load test against the forecast normal-day traffic to confirm no regression, a stress test well past the forecast flash-sale peak to find and document the actual breaking point, and a 12-hour soak test earlier in the week to catch any slow leak before it has a chance to surface during the sale itself.

05 ShopSphere's checkout API performance budget

A performance budget turns "fast" into something you can test against and alert on.

MetricBudget
p50 latency< 100 ms
p95 latency< 300 ms
p99 latency< 500 ms
Availability> 99.99%

Every number here is a verifiable release gate (in a load test) and a production alarm threshold (in CloudWatch) — not an aspiration.

06 Why p99 matters even though "most" users won't hit it

"Only 1% of requests miss the p99 budget" sounds negligible until you multiply it by ShopSphere's actual volume. At 10,000,000 users/day (Module 19, Scenario 3) with even a modest fraction reaching checkout, 1% of that volume is not an edge case — it's tens of thousands of real customers every single day experiencing a slow or failed checkout. Averages hide this completely: an average latency of 110ms is consistent with either a tight, healthy distribution or with 99% of requests at 50ms and 1% of requests timing out at 5 seconds — two completely different production realities that look identical on a dashboard that only plots the mean.

The tail is often your best customers, not random noise

Slow requests correlate with large shopping carts, customers on weaker mobile connections, or accounts with a long order history (bigger queries) — which means the p99 tail is frequently biased toward your highest-value or most loyal customers, not a random 1% sample. Ignoring p99 because "it's only 1%" can mean systematically failing the customers worth the most to the business.

07 Hands-on lab

  1. ShopSphere's checkout API shows average latency of 120ms, comfortably under the p50 budget. Design a load test (traffic shape, duration, what to measure) that would reveal whether the p99 budget is secretly being missed despite that healthy average.
  2. A service's CPU sits at 40% but p99 latency is 3 seconds and climbing. Using the four-signature table, name at least two failure signatures this rules out, and the one piece of evidence you'd check next to confirm which of the remaining signatures it is.
  3. Write the before/after connection-handling code (pseudocode is fine) for a service you've worked on or imagined, showing the per-request-connection anti-pattern and the pooled fix, and state roughly at what concurrent request count you'd expect the anti-pattern version to start failing.

08 Interview questions

A service's average latency looks fine, but customers are complaining about slowness. What's the first question you ask?
What do the latency percentiles look like, not just the average? An average can look healthy while a meaningful tail of requests (p95/p99) is badly out of budget — averages are mathematically pulled toward the bulk of fast requests and can completely hide a slow, real-impact tail. The first step is always to look at the distribution, not the mean.
Why can a service exhaust its database connection limit at a request rate far below what the hardware could otherwise sustain?
If the application opens a new database connection per request instead of pooling, each connection consumes a slot against the database's fixed connection ceiling for its entire (short) lifetime, and the overhead of establishing each connection (TCP, TLS, auth) adds latency that keeps slots occupied longer than the actual query needs. The ceiling is hit on connection count, not CPU or query complexity, so the service can fail at a request volume the underlying hardware would handle easily with pooling.
Why isn't a single load test, run once before launch, sufficient for a production service?
A load test only answers whether the system meets its budget under expected traffic, for the duration it was run. It says nothing about where the actual breaking point is under unexpected traffic (that needs a stress test) or about slow degradation that only appears after hours of sustained load, like memory leaks or connection leaks (that needs a soak test). Each test type answers a different question, and skipping one leaves that question completely untested.

AWS · solutionDistributed Load Testing on AWSAWS's own reference solution for running load tests at scale.docs.aws.amazon.com ↗
AWS · officialAmazon RDS ProxyThe managed fix for the per-request-connection anti-pattern at scale.docs.aws.amazon.com ↗

Level 4 · Production Architecture

Reliability Engineering

● ~26 min read● Builds on Module 20● ShopSphere: stops one failure from becoming every failure

Bottom line: every pattern in this module exists to answer one question — when something fails (and in a distributed system, something always eventually fails), does the failure stay contained to the component that broke, or does it spread? ShopSphere is designed so a slow payment provider degrades payment, not catalog browsing, checkout confirmation pages, or the whole site.

01 Cascading failure — the thing every pattern here prevents

A cascading failure starts as one component being slow or unavailable and ends with the entire system down, because nothing stopped the failure from propagating. The mechanism is almost always the same: a caller waits too long for a failing dependency, that wait ties up a limited resource (a thread, a connection, a worker), and once enough of that resource is tied up, the caller itself becomes unavailable to everything else that depends on it — including callers that had nothing to do with the original failure.

A concrete ShopSphere cascade

The external payment provider starts responding slowly. Checkout's HTTP client has no timeout, so each payment call simply waits. Checkout's thread pool is shared across all of checkout's work, so within minutes every thread is blocked waiting on the payment provider — and checkout stops responding to any request, including ones that never touch payment. A single slow dependency has just taken down the entire checkout flow.

02 Timeouts — the first and simplest fix

Every call to another network service — another AWS service, another microservice, an external provider — needs an explicit timeout. An unbounded wait on a hung dependency is never safe: it means a single failing dependency can eventually exhaust whatever finite resource (threads, connections) is waiting on it, exactly as in the cascade above. There is no universally correct timeout value; it should be set from the caller's own latency budget (Module 20) — if checkout's p99 budget is 500ms, a payment call that's part of that path cannot itself be allowed to wait 10 seconds.

A timeout alone isn't the whole fix

A timeout stops one call from hanging forever, but if the payment provider is failing broadly, checkout is still making (and timing out on) a payment call for every single incoming request — still consuming a thread/connection for the timeout's duration, still adding real latency to every checkout attempt. Timeouts bound the damage of a single call; circuit breakers (Section 04) stop the pattern from repeating once failure is already known.

03 Retries with exponential backoff and jitter

A failed call is often worth retrying — a dropped packet, a momentary blip. Exponential backoff increases the wait between retries (e.g. 100ms, 200ms, 400ms, 800ms) so repeated retries don't hammer an already-struggling dependency at a constant rate. But backoff alone has a hidden flaw: if every client backs off on exactly the same schedule, they retry in synchronized waves — a thundering herd that hits the recovering dependency at the same instant, every interval, which can keep knocking it back down right as it's trying to recover.

Jitter — adding a small random offset to each wait — spreads those retries out in time instead of letting them land in lockstep. This is the difference between a dependency recovering smoothly versus getting re-flattened every time it tries to come back up.

// Backoff without jitter — every client retries on the exact same schedule
wait = baseDelay * (2 ** attempt);  // 100ms, 200ms, 400ms... synchronized across every caller

// Backoff with full jitter — retries spread randomly across the window
wait = random(0, baseDelay * (2 ** attempt));

Retry storms make outages worse, not better

During a real ShopSphere payment-provider outage, checkout instances without jitter all backed off and retried on the same clock tick. Every retry wave arrived at the payment provider simultaneously, at exactly the moment its own recovering capacity was lowest — extending the outage well past when the provider's own fix would otherwise have taken hold. Jitter alone measurably shortened a later, similar incident.

04 Circuit breakers — stop calling a dependency that's already down

Retries assume the next attempt might succeed. Once a dependency is reliably failing, every retry is pure waste — it still consumes a thread/connection and adds latency, for a call that was never going to succeed. A circuit breaker tracks the failure rate of a dependency and, once it crosses a threshold, stops calling it entirely for a cooldown period — failing fast instead.

StateBehaviorTransition out
ClosedRequests pass through normally; failures are counted in a rolling windowFailure rate exceeds a threshold (e.g. >50% errors over 10 seconds) → Open
OpenEvery request fails immediately — no call is made to the dependency at allReset timeout elapses (e.g. 30 seconds) → Half-Open
Half-OpenExactly one (or a small number of) trial request is let through to test recoveryTrial succeeds → Closed. Trial fails → back to Open

05 Bulkheads — so one dependency can't starve an unrelated one

A circuit breaker stops a service from hammering a dependency that's already down — but even a well-timed-out, breaker-protected payment call still shares a resource pool (threads, connections) with every other kind of work the service does, unless that's explicitly prevented. A bulkhead gives each dependency its own isolated pool, the way a ship's bulkheads keep one flooded compartment from sinking the whole vessel.

Shared pool (no bulkhead)Isolated pool (bulkhead)
Payment provider goes slowPayment calls consume threads from the same pool checkout uses for everything else — catalog lookups and order confirmation slow down tooPayment calls are capped to their own small, dedicated pool; once it's exhausted, only new payment attempts queue or fail — catalog and confirmation keep working normally
ShopSphere applicationOne thread pool for all outbound calls in the checkout serviceSeparate, sized pools per dependency: payment provider, inventory service, notification service

06 Health checks that actually detect unhealthy state

A health check that only confirms "the process is running" misses the majority of real production failures — a process can be alive and listening on its port while its database connection is dead, its cache is unreachable, or its disk is full. A real readiness check actively verifies the dependencies that request handling actually needs: can it reach the database, is the connection pool below its limit, is the critical downstream dependency's circuit breaker closed. A separate, cheaper liveness check (is the process itself responsive at all, for the orchestrator to decide whether to restart it) still has its place — the mistake is treating liveness as if it were sufficient readiness evidence.

ShopSphere's checkout readiness check

Returns healthy only if: the Aurora connection pool has available connections, a trivial query succeeds within budget, and the payment-provider circuit breaker isn't Open. The ALB's target group stops routing new traffic to any instance that fails this check — before that instance starts timing out real customer requests, not after.

07 Applying all of this to real ShopSphere failures

FailureWhat saves ShopSphere
Database primary failsMulti-AZ automatic failover (Module 19); application-side connection retry with backoff while failover completes
An entire AZ failsAuto Scaling Group and database replicas already spread across AZs (Module 19); ALB health checks route around the dead AZ within seconds
Payment provider becomes unavailableTimeout bounds each call; circuit breaker stops calling it once failure is confirmed; bulkhead keeps catalog/confirmation unaffected; backoff with jitter avoids re-flattening it on recovery
Sudden traffic spikeQueue-based load leveling and rate limiting (Module 19, Scenario 4) admit load at a sustainable rate instead of every component failing at once

08 The core idea: graceful degradation over cascading failure

The destination for every pattern in this module is the same: a failure in one domain should degrade ShopSphere, not take it down. If the Recommendations service is unreachable, the product page should simply render without the recommendations widget — not fail the whole page load, and certainly not share a resource pool with checkout such that a Recommendations outage could ever touch the ability to pay for an order. A production architecture review explicitly asks, for every component: "if this fails, what still works?" — and "nothing" is the answer this module exists to eliminate.

09 Hands-on lab

  1. Design the circuit breaker configuration (failure threshold, rolling window, reset timeout) you'd set for ShopSphere's payment-provider call, and justify each number against the checkout performance budget from Module 20.
  2. A teammate adds retry-with-exponential-backoff to a service but skips jitter, reasoning "it's already better than no backoff." Write the scenario where that choice makes an outage measurably worse, using this module's retry-storm explanation.
  3. ShopSphere's Recommendations service is down. Write the exact fallback behavior the product page should show, and name which pattern (bulkhead, circuit breaker, or graceful degradation) is doing the work at each step from "Recommendations call starts failing" to "page still renders fine."

10 Interview questions

What's the difference between what a timeout protects against and what a circuit breaker protects against?
A timeout bounds how long any single call is allowed to wait, protecting against one hung call from indefinitely tying up a resource. A circuit breaker tracks failures across many calls and, once a dependency is reliably failing, stops calling it at all for a cooldown period — protecting against the waste and added latency of repeatedly retrying a dependency that's already known to be down. A service typically needs both: timeouts bound each individual call, circuit breakers stop the pattern of calls once failure is established.
Why does retrying without jitter make a real outage worse instead of just "not helping"?
Without jitter, every client that failed at roughly the same moment also retries on roughly the same schedule, so their retries arrive at the struggling dependency in synchronized waves rather than spread out. Each wave can be enough to knock the dependency back down right as its own recovering capacity is lowest, repeatedly re-triggering the failure and extending the outage well past when the dependency's own fix would otherwise have taken hold.
A service passes its health check but is still timing out real requests. What's the most likely explanation?
The health check is almost certainly a liveness check only — confirming the process is running and listening — rather than a readiness check that actively verifies the dependencies request handling actually needs (database connectivity, connection pool headroom, downstream circuit breaker state). The fix is to make the readiness check exercise those same dependencies, so the load balancer stops routing traffic to the instance before customers experience the failure, not after.

AWS · officialHealth Checks for ALB Target GroupsHow to configure a readiness check an ALB actually acts on.docs.aws.amazon.com ↗

Level 5 · Distributed Systems

CAP, PACELC, Consensus & Consistency

● ~28 min read● Builds on Module 21● ShopSphere: Inventory survives a network partition

Bottom line: the moment ShopSphere runs more than one copy of its data on more than one machine — which is every production deployment past a single instance — it has already made a CAP-theorem trade-off, whether anyone wrote it down or not. This module makes that trade-off explicit and per-domain, instead of an accident discovered during an incident.

01 The CAP theorem, precisely

Formulated by Eric Brewer and formally proven by Gilbert & Lynch, the CAP theorem says a distributed data store can provide at most two of these three guarantees at once:

GuaranteePrecise meaning
C — ConsistencyEvery read receives the most recent write, or an error — as if there were only one copy of the data (this is linearizability, a much stronger claim than SQL's "C" in ACID)
A — AvailabilityEvery request to a non-failing node receives a response — not necessarily the correct or latest one, just a response, with no error and no indefinite wait
P — Partition toleranceThe system keeps operating despite dropped, delayed or reordered messages between nodes — a partition is any failure of the network between replicas, not just a dramatic region-wide outage

CAP is a statement about behavior specifically during a network partition — not a general three-way dial you tune at all times. When the network is healthy, a well-built system can often deliver all three; CAP only forces a choice the moment nodes can't talk to each other.

02 Partition tolerance isn't really optional

Here is the detail that trips up most first encounters with CAP: in any real distributed system — multiple nodes, multiple Availability Zones, multiple regions — network partitions will happen. A switch fails, a fiber link is cut, an AZ's network degrades under load. You cannot pick "we will not be partition tolerant" and keep calling the result a distributed system; the only system that truly skips P is a single machine with no replicas at all — which trades away availability and durability in a far blunter way than anything CAP discusses.

CAP is not a menu of three, pick two

Treating CAP as "choose 2 of 3 and ignore the third" is the most common misreading. P is not a feature you opt out of in production — it's a property of operating over an unreliable network, which is every network. The actual, continuous decision CAP forces on you is C vs A, specifically during the partition window. Outside that window, both are achievable; the theorem has nothing to say about a healthy network.

03 ShopSphere's Inventory service during a partition

Make this concrete. ShopSphere's Inventory service keeps a replica in AZ-A and a replica in AZ-B. A network fault severs the link between the two AZs. Two shoppers, at the same moment, both try to buy the last unit of a product — one request lands on AZ-A's replica, the other on AZ-B's.

ChoiceWhat happens on the minority side (AZ-B)Business consequence
CP — favor ConsistencyRefuses the write until it can confirm with the majority/leader side that the stock is still availableThat shopper's checkout fails or stalls — a real, visible partial outage, but inventory count is never wrong
AP — favor AvailabilityAccepts the write against its own local, possibly stale countBoth shoppers "win" the last unit — overselling, discovered only once the partition heals and the two writes reconcile

The business cost of getting this wrong at scale

During a flash sale, an AZ fault lasting a few minutes under an AP-style Inventory design doesn't oversell one item — it can oversell hundreds, each one needing a cancellation, a refund, an apology and a support ticket. A CP-style design turns the same fault into a smaller, contained, honestly-communicated partial outage on exactly the minority side. Neither option is free; the point of this module is choosing on purpose.

ShopSphere's actual decision

The decrement itself — the operation that actually takes a unit out of stock during checkout — uses a strongly consistent, conditional write (DynamoDB's ConditionExpression against a strongly consistent read, or an Aurora leader-only write) so it behaves CP: it fails loudly on the minority side rather than silently overselling. The "in stock" badge shown while browsing is intentionally AP — eventually consistent, cheap, and briefly wrong is an acceptable trade for a non-money-moving display value. One service, two consistency choices, picked per operation, not per service.

04 PACELC — the trade-off that doesn't go away when the network is healthy

Daniel Abadi's PACELC extends CAP with the half it leaves out: Partition — Availability vs Consistency (exactly CAP), Else — Latency vs Consistency. Even with zero partition, a system that wants every read to reflect the latest write must either read from a single source of truth (adding latency, and reducing how many places can safely answer the read) or accept that a nearby, faster-to-reach replica might be slightly behind.

This isn't hypothetical for ShopSphere: AWS's own DynamoDB documentation states a strongly consistent read costs roughly double the read-capacity (and some added latency) of an eventually consistent read — a cost paid on every single request, partition or not. That's PACELC's "Else" clause, visible in a bill and a latency graph rather than in an incident report.

System / configurationPACELC categoryWhat it favors
DynamoDB, default readPA/ELAvailable & low-latency during a partition; low-latency normally — may read slightly stale data
DynamoDB, strongly consistent readPC/ECConsistent in both cases — pays a latency and throughput cost even with a healthy network
Aurora/RDS Multi-AZ, synchronous standbyPC/ECNever loses an acknowledged write; the sync round-trip adds latency even on the happy path
Aurora/RDS read replica (async)PA/ELFast reads spread across replicas; a replica can lag the primary by a measurable amount

05 Leader/follower replication & quorum reads/writes

Module 08/13's Multi-AZ RDS is the clearest leader/follower example already in this course: the primary accepts every write; the standby in a different AZ receives it synchronously before the primary acknowledges the client — a deliberate latency cost paid so that failover loses effectively no committed data. Asynchronous read replicas trade that durability guarantee for horizontal read scaling: fast to add, fast to read from, but each one can lag the primary, so a read immediately after a write on a different replica can return stale data.

Quorum generalizes this beyond one leader and one standby: with N total replicas, require W replicas to acknowledge a write and R replicas to agree on a read. Whenever W + R > N, every read is mathematically guaranteed to overlap at least one replica that saw the latest write — strong consistency without needing every single replica to participate in every operation. DynamoDB's own multi-AZ replication internally works this way even though, at the API surface, it's exposed to you as the simpler eventually-consistent-vs-strongly-consistent-read toggle described in Sections 03–04 above.

06 Strong vs eventual consistency, chosen per domain

The architecturally mature answer is never "ShopSphere uses strong consistency" or "ShopSphere uses eventual consistency" as one system-wide statement. It's chosen per domain, often per operation, based on what's actually at stake if a read is briefly wrong.

ShopSphere domain / operationConsistencyWhy
Order — payment statusStrongShowing "paid" when it wasn't (or the reverse) is a direct financial and trust failure
Inventory — the checkout decrementStrongOverselling is a business and sometimes legal problem, not a cosmetic one
Inventory — the "in stock" badge while browsingEventualBriefly wrong is invisible to the shopper and far cheaper to serve at catalog scale
Product view counters, "trending now"EventualNo individual read is ever load-bearing; aggregated and approximate by nature
Search index (OpenSearch)EventualA just-sold-out item may briefly still appear in search results — the actual purchase path re-checks Inventory strongly before confirming

07 Hands-on lab

  1. ShopSphere's Cart service is replicated across two AZs and a partition splits them. Decide, and justify in two sentences, whether Cart should behave CP or AP during that partition — and name the concrete bad outcome of the choice you didn't make.
  2. Using the W + R > N quorum rule, pick values of N, W and R for a 5-replica configuration that tolerates 2 simultaneous replica failures for writes while still guaranteeing every read sees the latest write. Show the arithmetic.
  3. A teammate says "we made Orders eventually consistent to make the API faster." Write the two or three follow-up questions a Principal Architect would ask before accepting that change.

08 Interview questions

"We're partition tolerant, available, and consistent" — what's wrong with that sentence?
CAP doesn't offer all three as a steady-state achievement; partition tolerance isn't optional in a real distributed system, so the only genuine choice is between consistency and availability, and only during an actual network partition. Outside a partition, a well-built system can look consistent and available at once, which is likely what's really being described — but the sentence as stated misunderstands what CAP constrains.
Why does a strongly consistent DynamoDB read cost more than an eventually consistent one, even with a perfectly healthy network?
That's PACELC's "Else" clause: guaranteeing the read reflects the very latest write means the request has to involve enough replicas (effectively a leader or a quorum) to be certain, rather than returning from whichever single replica answers fastest. That coordination has a latency and capacity cost that exists independent of any network partition — it's the price of consistency during normal operation, not just during a failure.
Why is a Multi-AZ RDS standby not used to serve read traffic, while a read replica is?
The standby's entire job is to replicate synchronously so it can take over with zero data loss during failover — reading from it directly would add load to a node whose only value is being an exact, instantly-promotable mirror, without actually relieving the primary's write path. A read replica is explicitly built for the opposite job: offload read traffic, accepting asynchronous (eventually consistent) replication lag as the cost of that scalability.

AWS · officialMulti-AZ DB Instance Deployments for Amazon RDSThe synchronous standby that makes RDS a leader/follower example you can point to.docs.aws.amazon.com ↗
ReferencePACELC Design PrincipleDaniel Abadi's extension of CAP to the no-partition case.en.wikipedia.org ↗

Level 5 · Distributed Systems

Sagas, Outbox & Distributed Transactions

● ~34 min read● Builds on Module 22● ShopSphere: the full checkout saga

Bottom line: once Order, Payment and Inventory are independent services with independent databases, there is no shared commit log left to make "reserve stock, charge the card, confirm the order" atomic the old way. The Saga pattern — a chain of local transactions, each with a defined compensating transaction — is how production systems get an equivalent guarantee without a lock that spans three services. This is one of the highest-leverage modules in the whole course: get it wrong and ShopSphere either double-charges customers or deadlocks under load.

01 Why ACID can't simply span Order + Payment + Inventory

A classical ACID transaction is a property of one transactional resource: one database engine, with one write-ahead log, that can atomically COMMIT or ROLLBACK every change inside the transaction boundary. The instant Order, Payment and Inventory become three independent services, each backed by its own database (possibly a different engine, in a different AWS account, run by a different team), that single shared log no longer exists. A commit to Order's Aurora cluster has zero built-in awareness of whether Payment's database will ever commit its side — there's nothing to roll back to, across a boundary ACID was never designed to cross.

02 Two-phase commit — the naive fix, and why production avoids it

Two-phase commit (2PC) is the textbook answer to coordinating multiple independent resource managers: a coordinator asks every participant to prepare (lock the affected rows, vote yes/no), and only once every participant votes yes does it send a second, final commit message to all of them.

It works — and it's almost never used across independent microservices in practice, for a reason that matters: the coordinator is a single point of failure sitting squarely in the critical path, and every participant must hold its locks and stay reachable for the entire prepare-to-commit window. If the coordinator crashes after collecting votes but before sending the final commit, every participant is stuck holding locks indefinitely, waiting on a decision that may never arrive — a state literally called "blocked." 2PC trades away exactly the independent availability that justified splitting into services in the first place.

What this looks like in ShopSphere

If checkout used 2PC across Order, Payment and Inventory and the coordinator dropped mid-transaction, Payment's database would be left holding a row lock on that customer's payment record — and depending on isolation level, potentially blocking other operations on the same table — until someone manually intervenes. At flash-sale volume, that's not a slow request; it's a growing queue of stuck transactions behind it.

03 The Saga pattern

A Saga is a sequence of local transactions, each committing immediately and independently inside its own service's database, where every step that needs one defines a compensating transaction — a separate, explicit action that semantically undoes it — to be run if a later step in the sequence fails.

Three properties fall out of that definition, and all three matter: no lock is ever held across a service boundary, so one step's database is never blocked waiting on another service; each local commit lands immediately, so availability recovers the instant that one step finishes, regardless of what the rest of the saga does; and correctness is achieved eventually, not atomically — for a real, observable window, the system can be in an intermediate state (inventory reserved, payment not yet charged) rather than all-or-nothing. That's Module 22's eventual consistency, applied to a business workflow instead of a data replica.

04 Designing the ShopSphere checkout saga, step by step

#StepOwning serviceLocal transactionCompensating transaction
1Reserve inventoryInventoryDecrement available stock into a time-boxed "reserved" state for this orderRelease the reservation back to available stock
2Charge paymentPaymentCall the payment provider with an idempotency key; record the chargeIssue a refund for the charge
3Confirm inventoryInventoryConvert the reservation into a permanent decrementRestock the confirmed decrement (restore to available, or hold pending investigation)
4Schedule shipmentShipmentCreate a shipment record; notify the fulfillment/carrier integrationCancel the shipment record if created
5Notify customerNotificationSend order-confirmation email/SMS; emit the event Analytics subscribes ton/a — side effects only, nothing to compensate

05 Walking through two failures

Case A — payment fails at step 2. Nothing downstream has happened yet: no shipment scheduled, no stock permanently committed. The saga compensates by releasing the inventory reservation from step 1 and marking the order "payment failed." The customer sees a failed checkout and is free to retry; no money moved, no stock was actually lost. This is the cheap, clean case — compensate one step back, done.

Case B — shipment scheduling fails at step 4, after payment already succeeded at step 2 and inventory was already confirmed at step 3. This is the expensive case, and it's where intuition about "rollback" breaks. You cannot simply "undo" a captured card payment the way a database rolls back a write — a refund is itself a new transaction, with its own latency, its own fees, and its own failure modes with the payment provider. The usual order of operations is: retry shipment scheduling first (it's often the safe, cheap recovery, and avoids touching money at all); only if retries exhaust, compensate by issuing a refund and, depending on business policy, restocking the confirmed decrement from step 3.

Compensations are not rollbacks — and they can fail too

A compensating transaction is a new, forward-moving business action, not a reversal the database performs for you. A refund call to the payment provider can itself time out, be rejected, or partially succeed. ShopSphere routes exhausted compensation attempts to a dead-letter queue with a human-reviewed runbook rather than retrying forever — an infinite retry loop on a compensation is just a slower version of the same problem.

06 Choreography vs orchestration

ChoreographyOrchestration
How it worksEach service publishes an event on completing its local transaction; others subscribe and reactA single coordinator explicitly invokes each step and its compensation
CouplingLoose — services only know event shapes, not each otherThe orchestrator knows every participant's contract
Where the sequence livesImplicit, spread across every service's event subscriptionsExplicit, in one versioned state-machine definition
Debuggability during an incidentHarder — reconstructing "what happened" means correlating logs across every serviceEasier — one execution history shows exactly which step is where
AWS mechanismEventBridge/SNS fan-out, each consumer an SQS queueStep Functions state machine calling Lambda/ECS tasks per step
Best fitIndependent reactions where order truly doesn't matter and nothing needs compensatingA workflow with real sequencing, timeouts and compensation logic

ShopSphere's actual split

The core checkout saga (reserve → charge → confirm → ship) runs as a Step Functions orchestration — the sequencing, timeouts and compensation branches benefit from one visible, versioned definition and a visual execution history when something goes wrong at 2am. The fan-out afterward — Notification and Analytics reacting to the finalized order — stays choreographed through the existing Order Created event, because those reactions are independent, order-doesn't-matter, and need no compensation at all.

07 The Outbox pattern — solving the dual-write problem

Writing the new order row to Order's database and publishing an OrderCreated message to SQS/SNS are two separate systems with no shared atomic operation spanning both — the dual-write problem. Commit the database write and then crash before the publish call, and the event is silently lost; the rest of the saga never starts. Publish first and have the database commit fail or roll back afterward, and you've announced an order that doesn't exist.

The Outbox pattern fixes this by writing the event as a row in an outbox table, inside the exact same local ACID transaction as the order write itself. Same database, same commit: either both rows exist or neither does, by definition of one local transaction — no cross-system atomicity required. A separate, independent relay process — a poller, or change-data-capture via DynamoDB Streams or a Debezium-style log tailer on Aurora — reads newly committed outbox rows and publishes them to SNS/EventBridge, marking them delivered once the broker accepts them.

Why the relay is allowed to be imperfect

The relay process can crash, retry, or publish the same row twice — and that's fine, because it's delivering at-least-once from a durable, already-committed row. The worst case is a duplicate publish, never a lost one. Section 08 is what makes that duplicate harmless instead of dangerous.

08 Idempotency keys — making retries safe

An operation is idempotent when applying it more than once with the same key produces the same end-state as applying it exactly once. Both the Saga and the Outbox pattern above rely on at-least-once delivery — exactly-once delivery across a real network is not achievable, only simulated by deduplication — so every step in the saga has to be written as idempotent to make a retry harmless instead of dangerous.

Concretely: Payment's "charge card" call carries a client-generated idempotency key — the order ID is a natural choice. If the saga (or the outbox relay, or a client retry) calls charge-payment twice with the same key, both the payment provider's API and ShopSphere's own Payment table treat the second call as a no-op, returning the original result rather than charging the card again.

Idempotency key choiceTrade-off
Natural key (e.g. order ID)Simple, stable across retries of the same business operation; requires one key per logical operation, not per attempt
Generated UUID per saga executionNeeded when one order can legitimately trigger the same step twice for different reasons (e.g. a manual re-charge) — requires a dedupe table keyed on it

The three techniques are one answer, not three

Sagas with compensations, the outbox pattern, and idempotency keys aren't three unrelated tools picked ad hoc — they are the single coherent answer to "there is no distributed ACID transaction here." The saga replaces the atomic commit; the outbox makes the saga's trigger event reliable despite the dual-write problem; idempotency keys make every retry that both of those guarantee will happen, safe instead of harmful.

09 Hands-on lab

  1. Draw the full compensation chain for a failure at step 3 (Confirm inventory) — after payment already succeeded. List every compensating action that must fire, in order.
  2. Write the idempotency-key strategy for ShopSphere's Shipment service: what should the key be, and what should happen if "schedule shipment" is called twice with the same key but the carrier's response differs slightly (e.g. a different tracking number) between calls?
  3. A teammate proposes using 2PC across Order and Payment "just for these two, since they're both relational." Write the two strongest arguments against it, specific to a flash-sale traffic pattern.

10 Interview questions

Why can't you just wrap the Order insert and the Payment charge in one database transaction?
They're not in the same database once the services are independent — a transaction is a property of one resource manager's commit log, and there's no shared log spanning Order's database and Payment's database (or Payment's external provider API, which isn't a database at all). A saga replaces that missing atomic commit with a sequence of local transactions plus compensations.
What specifically makes 2PC a poor fit for independent microservices, beyond "it's old"?
The coordinator is a single point of failure sitting in the critical path, and every participant must hold locks and stay reachable for the entire prepare-to-commit window. A coordinator crash between those two phases leaves participants blocked indefinitely. That directly undermines the independent availability and deployability that justified splitting into services in the first place.
Your outbox relay just crashed mid-publish and restarted. What's the worst thing that can happen, and why is it acceptable?
The worst case is the same outbox row gets published a second time — a duplicate event, never a lost one, because the relay only ever reads already-committed rows and publishing is retried, not the original write. That duplicate is made harmless by idempotency keys on every downstream consumer, which is exactly why the two patterns are always paired.
