Level 5 · Distributed Systems

Monolith to Microservices Evolution

● ~26 min read● Builds on Module 23● ShopSphere: earns the right to split a service out

Bottom line: ShopSphere does not start as microservices, and that is not a compromise — it is the correct starting point. A service only earns the right to split out once a specific, named pain (an independent scaling need, a compliance boundary, a team-ownership conflict) justifies paying the very real operational cost — the sagas, the outbox, the distributed debugging — that the last two modules just spent building the tools for.

01 The four-stage evolution, starting with a monolith

A monolith is a single deployable unit containing every domain — Order, Payment, Inventory, Catalog and the rest — one codebase, one database, one deployment pipeline. For a small team and modest traffic, this is genuinely simpler to operate: one thing to deploy, one log stream to read, one database to reason about, zero network calls between domains that live in the same process. It's also, crucially, the easiest stage to refactor internal boundaries in — moving a function from one internal module to another is a code change reviewed in a pull request, not a wire-protocol, versioning and data-migration exercise across two live services.

ShopSphere's actual Day 1

A single application on EC2 behind an ALB with an Auto Scaling Group, backed by one Aurora cluster, with Order, Payment, Inventory and Catalog as well-organized internal packages inside the same process — a monolith, deliberately, not a stopgap apologized for.

02 The modular monolith — the stage most teams skip

Still one deployable unit, one running process — but internally organized into modules with enforced boundaries: each module owns its own set of tables, and every other module talks to it only through an in-process API (a service class, an interface), never a direct SQL join into another module's tables. This is the stage where the bounded contexts in Section 03 get discovered and hardened, while still paying only a single deployment's operational cost.

Teams skip this stage constantly, because enforcing it takes real discipline with no outwardly visible payoff — "it still looks like one app" — so under delivery pressure it's tempting to jump straight from monolith to microservices. That jump inherits every distributed-systems cost from Modules 22–23 before the internal boundaries have even been proven sound, and it is exactly how teams end up with Section 08's distributed monolith.

03 Bounded contexts

A bounded context is a boundary within which a specific domain model and its terminology have one unambiguous meaning, and all access to that model's data goes through its own API or events — never a shared table. In ShopSphere: Order owns order status and line items; Payment owns transaction, charge and refund records; Inventory owns stock levels and reservations; Catalog owns product metadata and pricing copy. Order never reaches into Inventory's tables to check stock directly — it calls Inventory's API, or reacts to Inventory's events.

Bounded contextOwnsNever does
OrderOrder status, line items, totalsRead or write Inventory's stock tables directly
PaymentTransactions, charges, refundsStore or interpret product/catalog data
InventoryStock levels, reservationsKnow anything about how a price was calculated
CatalogProduct metadata, pricing copyKnow which orders exist or their payment state

04 Database-per-service — and the pain it introduces

Once a bounded context is real, its database should be reachable only through that service's own API — no other service holds a connection string to its schema. This is what actually makes a service boundary enforceable: a shared database, even behind separately deployed services, is the single most common way "microservices" quietly become a distributed monolith, because the services remain coupled through tables neither fully owns.

The real pain, named honestly

"Show me every order containing a now-low-stock item" used to be one SQL join across Order and Inventory tables inside one transaction. Database-per-service removes that option entirely. The replacement is either Order calling Inventory's API once per row (slow, an N+1 problem at scale), or a materialized, eventually-consistent read model kept in sync via events, or a dedicated reporting service holding its own copies of both. Cross-domain consistency that used to be free — one join, one transaction — now has to be engineered on purpose.

That sentence is not abstract: this exact pain is why Modules 22 and 23 exist. Sagas, the outbox pattern and per-domain consistency choices aren't independent topics — they're the direct, necessary cost of database-per-service, paid back in engineering effort instead of in a SQL JOIN.

05 Selected microservices — splitting only what needs it

ShopSphere doesn't explode into fifteen services on one migration weekend. Payment splits out first, because PCI-DSS compliance scope isolation is a strong, independent reason on its own — keeping cardholder-data-adjacent code and infrastructure in one small, tightly audited service shrinks the compliance boundary for everything else. Inventory splits out next, because its write volume spikes independently of general browsing traffic (a flash sale hammers Inventory specifically) and it benefits from scaling on its own schedule. Catalog, Pricing and Promotion, by contrast, can stay inside the modular monolith far longer — they're comparatively low-traffic and tightly read-coupled to each other.

Reason to split a service out nowApplies to Payment?Applies to Promotion (yet)?
Independent scaling needModerateNo
Independent deploy cadence needYes — compliance review gates its releasesNo
Compliance/security boundaryYes — PCI-DSS scopeNo
Separate team owns it end to endYesNot yet
Genuinely different tech fitNoNo
"Microservices are the modern way"Never a valid reason on its own

06 Service discovery & configuration at scale

Once multiple services are deployed and scaled independently, each caller needs to find the current network location of the others — instances come and go, IPs change. Service discovery (AWS Cloud Map, or ECS Service Connect, or simply a load balancer with a stable DNS name per service) lets callers address a fixed name instead of a fixed IP. Configuration — which endpoint to call, which feature flag is on, which secret to use — similarly needs a central, versioned, per-environment store (Systems Manager Parameter Store or AppConfig) rather than being baked into each service's deployment artifact, so a config change doesn't force a redeploy of every service that reads it.

07 Event-driven architecture — the destination, not the start

Once domains are genuinely separated — their own code, their own database — the natural way to keep them loosely coupled in time as well as in deployment is asynchronous events instead of long synchronous call chains. This is Module 23's choreography half, and the Order Created fan-out to Payment, Inventory, Notification, Shipment and Analytics that already runs through this course: each service reacts to an event instead of being called directly in sequence. It's presented here as the end state this evolution is heading toward, reached only after the boundaries in Sections 02–04 are solid enough to event-ify safely.

08 When NOT to split a service out yet

This is the part most material skips, and it's the one that matters most in a real review. A distributed monolith is what you get when services are deployed independently — so the architecture diagram looks like microservices — but are coupled so tightly, via synchronous calls on the hot path, a shared database, or a release train that must ship several of them together to avoid breaking, that every cost of microservices (network latency, partial failure, the saga machinery from Module 23) is paid with none of the benefit (independent scaling, independent deploys, real fault isolation).

The rule of thumb

A service that is small, low-traffic, and whose every call pattern is a synchronous call to exactly one other specific service is usually better left inside the modular monolith. Splitting it adds a network hop and a brand-new failure mode (that call can now time out, retry, or fail independently) for zero real isolation benefit — the two pieces are going to fail together and deploy together in practice regardless of the process boundary drawn between them.

ShopSphere example: Promotion/coupon logic stays inside the modular monolith long after Payment and Inventory have split out. A coupon changes a price calculation inline, synchronously, on every single cart view, and its traffic doesn't scale independently of Pricing's. Splitting it out today would add a network round-trip to every cart view, for a service with no compliance boundary and no independent scaling need — cost added, nothing bought.

09 Hands-on lab

  1. List three ShopSphere domains and classify each as "split now," "modular monolith for now," or "genuinely unsure" — using the six-row criteria table from Section 05, not gut feeling.
  2. A teammate wants to split Wishlist into its own service "for cleanliness." Using Section 08's rule of thumb, write the two questions you'd ask before agreeing.
  3. Sketch, in one paragraph, what would have to be true about Catalog's traffic or team structure for splitting it out to become the right call.

10 Interview questions

Why is a modular monolith often the right intermediate step, rather than going straight from monolith to microservices?
It forces the bounded contexts to be discovered and hardened — enforced module boundaries, no cross-module table access — while still paying only one deployment's operational cost. Jumping straight to microservices means paying the full distributed-systems tax (network calls, partial failure, sagas) on boundaries that haven't yet been proven correct, which is a much more expensive place to discover a modeling mistake.
What is a "distributed monolith," and what specifically makes it worse than an honest monolith?
It's a system deployed as independent services that are still tightly coupled — through synchronous call chains, a shared database, or a release train that ships several together. It's worse than an honest monolith because it pays every microservices cost (network latency, partial failure, operational complexity) while keeping every monolith-style coupling problem, with none of the actual benefits like independent scaling or deployment that would justify the cost.
Give a concrete, non-generic reason ShopSphere might leave Promotion inside the modular monolith even after Payment and Inventory have split out.
Promotion's coupon logic runs synchronously on every cart view as part of Pricing's calculation, its traffic doesn't spike independently of general browsing, and it carries no compliance boundary of its own. Splitting it out would add a network round-trip to the single highest-frequency read path in the app for no independent-scaling or independent-deploy benefit — pure added latency and a new failure mode.

Level 5 · Distributed Systems

Containers & Kubernetes: ECS vs EKS

● ~28 min read● Builds on Module 24● ShopSphere: containerizes the Order service● AWS service

Bottom line: Module 24 split ShopSphere into bounded-context services; containers are what makes deploying and scaling each of those services independently practical instead of painful. But "containers" and "Kubernetes" are not the same decision — ECS gives you AWS-native container orchestration with a fraction of the operational surface area, EKS gives you the Kubernetes standard with its ecosystem and portability, and real production teams get this choice wrong in both directions far more often than they get the containerizing itself wrong.

01 Problem

Before Module 24, ShopSphere's checkout ran as one deployable unit on an EC2 Auto Scaling Group (Module 05) — one AMI, one fleet, one patching cycle. Once Order, Payment, Inventory, Notification and the rest are separate services with their own databases, that pattern multiplies: a separate AMI, a separate Auto Scaling Group, a separate OS patch cycle, per domain. Most of that infrastructure is near-identical — "run this app process, with this runtime, listening on this port" — repeated with small variations across a dozen services. Containers package exactly that repeated unit once, portably, and hand the job of placing many of them efficiently across a fleet of machines to an orchestrator instead of a hand-built Auto Scaling Group per service.

02 Concept: containers vs VMs, made concrete

Module 01's infrastructure abstraction spectrum placed containers between virtual machines and serverless, described only in the abstract. Here is the concrete mechanism. A virtual machine virtualizes hardware: EC2's Nitro hypervisor (Module 05) gives every instance its own full operating system kernel, booted from scratch. A container virtualizes the operating system instead: every container on a host shares that one host kernel, and is isolated from other containers only by kernel features — namespaces (what a process can see) and cgroups (what resources it can use) — while packaging just the application, its libraries and its runtime, not an entire OS.

Virtual machine (EC2)Container
KernelOwn full kernel per VMShares the host kernel
Isolation boundaryHypervisor — strongNamespaces/cgroups — weaker, improving
Startup timeMinutes (full boot)Milliseconds–seconds
Image sizeGigabytes (whole OS)Megabytes–low gigabytes (app + deps only)
Density per hostLow — tensHigh — dozens to hundreds

Because there is no second kernel to boot, a container starts fast enough that an orchestrator can treat "kill it and start a new one" as a routine, cheap operation — the assumption every piece of Kubernetes below is built on.

Images and registries

A container image is a read-only, layered filesystem snapshot — application code, runtime, libraries, baked by a Dockerfile into immutable layers that are cached and reused across builds. A running container is just that image plus a writable layer on top. Images live in a registry; on AWS that's Amazon ECR (Elastic Container Registry) — private by default, integrated with IAM for push/pull permissions, with built-in vulnerability scanning on push.

ShopSphere's image pipeline

Order service's Dockerfile builds a minimal image on top of a slim base runtime image, tagged with the Git commit SHA, pushed to the private ECR repository shopsphere/order-service. Nothing downstream ever pulls :latest — every deployment references an immutable, specific tag, so "what's actually running in production" is always answerable from the tag alone.

03 Kubernetes core objects, plainly

Kubernetes is a container orchestrator: you describe the desired state declaratively, and a control loop continuously reconciles the cluster toward it. Five objects cover almost everything ShopSphere's Order service needs:

ObjectWhat it actually is
PodThe smallest deployable unit — one or more containers that share a network namespace (one IP) and storage volumes. Almost always one application container per Pod, occasionally plus a tightly-coupled sidecar (a log shipper, a service-mesh proxy).
DeploymentA declarative manager for a set of identical Pod replicas: "I want 3 copies of this Pod spec, always." Handles rolling updates — replacing old Pods with new ones gradually, never all at once — and restarts any Pod that dies.
ServiceA stable virtual IP and internal DNS name in front of a changing set of Pods. Individual Pod IPs come and go constantly as the Deployment replaces them; the Service is the one address everything else (other services, the Ingress) ever talks to.
IngressL7 HTTP(S) routing from outside the cluster — host/path rules mapping, e.g., /orders/* to the order-svc Service. On EKS, an Ingress is typically backed by a real ALB, provisioned by the AWS Load Balancer Controller — the same ALB concept from Module 05/09, just driven from inside Kubernetes.
ConfigMap / SecretConfiguration and credentials externalized from the container image, injected into Pods as environment variables or mounted files — so the same image runs in staging and production with different config, and database credentials never get baked into a Docker layer.

04 HPA and Cluster Autoscaler — two independent scaling loops

The Horizontal Pod Autoscaler (HPA) watches a metric — CPU utilization, memory, or a custom metric such as requests-per-second — against a target, and adjusts a Deployment's replica count within a configured min/max. It is Module 05's Auto Scaling Group policy concept, one layer down: scaling Pods, not EC2 instances.

But more Pods only help if the underlying nodes (the EC2 instances, or Fargate capacity, backing the cluster) have room to schedule them. The Cluster Autoscaler (or Karpenter, AWS's newer, faster-provisioning alternative for EKS) watches for Pods that can't be scheduled due to insufficient node capacity and adds nodes — and scales nodes back down when they're underutilized. HPA and Cluster Autoscaler/Karpenter are two separate control loops solving two separate problems: HPA asks "do I need more copies of this Pod?"; the node autoscaler asks "is there anywhere to actually put them?"

A common misconfiguration

HPA scales the Order service's Deployment from 3 to 20 Pods during a flash sale, but if the node group has no spare capacity and Cluster Autoscaler/Karpenter isn't configured (or is too slow provisioning new nodes), those extra Pods sit Pending — scheduled in name only, delivering zero additional capacity exactly when ShopSphere needs it most.

05 ShopSphere use case: containerizing the Order service

Order service, post-Module 24

Order now runs as a Deployment of Order-service Pods, each built from the shopsphere/order-service ECR image, reading its Aurora connection string from a Secret and its feature flags from a ConfigMap. A Service named order-svc gives Payment, Inventory and the other Saga participants (Module 23) a stable address to call, regardless of which Pods are currently live. An Ingress exposes /orders/* externally through an ALB. HPA scales Order's Pod count on request rate; Cluster Autoscaler/Karpenter scales the underlying nodes to match.

06 Functional & non-functional requirements

FunctionalEnabled by
Deploy Order independently of Payment/InventorySeparate container image, separate Deployment
Roll out a new Order version with zero downtimeDeployment's rolling update strategy
Route /orders/* traffic in from outsideIngress + ALB
Scale Order independently during a flash saleHPA + Cluster Autoscaler/Karpenter
Non-functional dimensionImpact
AvailabilityA Deployment with ≥2 replicas across AZs survives a Pod or node failure; the Service never points only at a dead Pod
ScalabilityHorizontal, faster than EC2 Auto Scaling at the Pod layer; still bounded by node-layer scaling speed
PerformanceContainer cold start (seconds) sits between EC2 (minutes) and Lambda (milliseconds–seconds)
ReliabilityKubernetes restarts failed Pods automatically; doesn't fix application bugs, only process-level crashes
SecuritySmaller attack surface per unit (no guest OS to patch on Fargate); image scanning in ECR; still your IAM/network config to get right
MaintainabilityHighest learning curve of the compute options so far — YAML, cluster concepts, a steeper ramp
ObservabilityNeeds cluster-aware tooling (Container Insights, or Prometheus/Grafana) beyond plain host metrics
CostEKS adds a control-plane hourly fee on top of node/Fargate compute; ECS has no separate control-plane charge

07 Alternatives: ECS vs EKS

Both orchestrate containers on AWS; they differ in standard vs native, and in how much operational surface area you take on. AWS's own framing: ECS is "the fastest path to running containers on AWS," while EKS gives you "production-grade Kubernetes without the undifferentiated heavy lifting of operating it" — note that even AWS's pitch for EKS is about removing operational burden Kubernetes itself creates, not eliminating it.

ECSEKS
Orchestration APIAWS-native (task definitions, services, clusters)Standard Kubernetes API
Control planeFully AWS-managed, invisible to youAWS-managed, but you still interact with it directly (kubectl, YAML)
Learning curveShallow — close to Module 05/09's EC2/ALB conceptsSteep — Kubernetes' own object model, RBAC, networking model
Ecosystem & portabilityAWS-onlyHuge ecosystem (Helm charts, operators); runs the same on any cloud or on-prem
Serverless compute optionFargateFargate (with limits) or self-managed/Karpenter-managed nodes
Best fitAWS-only shops wanting the simplest path to "containers, orchestrated, scaled"Multi-cloud requirements, existing Kubernetes standardization, or a need for its specific ecosystem

08 When Kubernetes is justified — and when it's unjustified complexity

EKS is justified when…

• ShopSphere genuinely needs to run the same workload across multiple clouds, or on-prem plus AWS (portability is a real, not hypothetical, requirement).
• The organization has already standardized on Kubernetes elsewhere and has the platform team to operate it.
• A specific capability only the Kubernetes ecosystem offers at the needed maturity is required — a particular operator, a service mesh, a tool that assumes the Kubernetes API.

EKS is unjustified complexity when…

• ShopSphere is staying on AWS and has no multi-cloud requirement on the roadmap — portability bought at the cost of a steep learning curve, for a feature never used, is pure cost.
• The team is small and doesn't have (or want to hire) dedicated platform/SRE capacity to own cluster upgrades, RBAC, networking plugins and the rest of Kubernetes' own operational surface.
• ECS already does the job — task definitions, services, Fargate, Auto Scaling — with a fraction of the YAML and a fraction of the ways to misconfigure it.

For a team ShopSphere's actual size at this point in the curriculum, ECS is very likely the right call: the orchestration problem (run containers, scale them, route to them) is fully solved without taking on Kubernetes' own learning curve as a second problem layered on top of the first.

09 Trade-offs

Containers trade VM-level isolation strength for density and speed — a reasonable trade for stateless application code, a much more careful one for anything handling untrusted multi-tenant workloads. Kubernetes specifically trades operational simplicity for standardization and ecosystem breadth: the same YAML that runs on EKS runs on any conformant cluster anywhere, which is valuable precisely in proportion to how much that portability actually gets used — and a real cost (control-plane fee, YAML sprawl, a steeper on-call learning curve) regardless of whether it's used.

10 Failure scenarios

FailureWhat happensMitigation
A Pod crashesDeployment's control loop notices the replica count is short and starts a new PodSet resource requests/limits so the scheduler doesn't overcommit the node that caused the crash
A node failsEvery Pod on it is gone at once; Kubernetes reschedules them elsewhere — if capacity existsMulti-AZ node groups; Pod Disruption Budgets so not all replicas land on one node/AZ
A bad image is deployedRolling update replaces healthy Pods with broken ones gradually, not all at onceReadiness probes that fail fast, pausing the rollout before it reaches every replica
EKS control plane has a regional blipRunning Pods keep serving traffic; you temporarily can't change desired state via the APIDesign for "can't deploy right now," not "service is down" — data plane and control plane fail somewhat independently
Image pull fails (ECR unreachable or permission denied)New Pods can't start; existing ones keep runningVPC endpoint for ECR (Module 07-deep) so pulls don't depend on NAT/internet path

11 Security

  • IAM Roles for Service Accounts (IRSA) on EKS, or ECS task roles — each Pod/task gets exactly the AWS permissions its own code needs, the Module 03 least-privilege pattern applied per-workload rather than per-node.
  • Image scanning in ECR on every push, catching known-vulnerable base image layers before they ever reach a cluster.
  • Network segmentation inside the cluster — Kubernetes Network Policies (EKS) or security groups per task (ECS) so Order's Pods can't reach Payment's database directly, mirroring the bounded-context database-per-service isolation from Module 24 at the network layer too.
  • Secrets never baked into images — injected at runtime from Kubernetes Secrets backed by AWS Secrets Manager, or ECS task definitions referencing Secrets Manager directly.
  • RBAC on EKS controls who (which humans, which CI pipeline) can change what inside the cluster — a second permission layer alongside IAM, and one more thing EKS requires you to design that ECS doesn't expose at all.

12 Production configuration

DevelopmentProduction
Replica count13+, spread across AZs
Resource requests/limitsUnset or generousTuned from real usage; prevents one Pod starving its node
Node capacitySingle small node groupMulti-AZ node groups, or Fargate, plus Cluster Autoscaler/Karpenter
Rollout strategyRecreate (brief downtime acceptable)Rolling update with readiness probes and a max-unavailable cap
Image tag:latest, rebuilt ad hocImmutable tag per commit SHA, built by CI (Module 27)

13 Monitoring

CloudWatch Container Insights collects CPU/memory at the cluster, node, Pod and container level for both ECS and EKS — the Module 05 "memory isn't collected by default" gotcha applies here too unless Insights (or an equivalent, like a self-managed Prometheus/Grafana stack on EKS) is explicitly enabled. Watch Deployment-level signals specifically: desired vs. available replica count (a persistent gap means something, usually capacity, is blocking scheduling), restart counts (a crash loop hiding behind "it's running"), and HPA's current vs. target metric value, not just raw CPU%.

14 Cost

Compute cost is the same EC2/Fargate pricing from Modules 05 and 09, just sliced into smaller units — the main new cost line is EKS's control-plane hourly fee, charged per cluster regardless of size, which ECS has no equivalent of. Higher Pod density per node than one-container-per-instance generally improves utilization and lowers compute cost per unit of work versus a 1-service-per-EC2-fleet design — the main financial argument for containerizing at all, independent of ECS vs EKS.

Where EKS costs sneak up

A cluster with many small, low-traffic services each demanding their own node pool for isolation ends up paying the EKS control-plane fee many times over while running mostly-idle nodes — the opposite of the density gain containers are supposed to deliver. Bin-pack compatible services onto shared node groups wherever bounded-context isolation doesn't strictly require separation.

15 Hands-on lab

  1. Write a minimal Kubernetes Deployment manifest (YAML, by hand or on paper) for the Order service: 3 replicas, one container, an environment variable sourced from a ConfigMap and a database password sourced from a Secret.
  2. ShopSphere's platform team has 4 engineers and no existing Kubernetes experience, and every workload stays on AWS for the foreseeable future. Using Section 08's framework, argue for ECS or EKS and name the one condition that would flip your answer.
  3. Order's Deployment is scaled from 3 to 15 Pods by HPA during a flash sale, but 8 of them stay Pending. List the two most likely root causes and which autoscaler is responsible for fixing each.

16 Interview questions

What is the actual mechanical difference between a container and a virtual machine?
A VM virtualizes hardware and boots its own full OS kernel on top of a hypervisor; a container virtualizes the operating system — every container on a host shares one kernel, isolated from each other only by kernel namespaces (visibility) and cgroups (resource limits), while packaging just the app, its libraries and its runtime. That's why containers start in milliseconds-to-seconds instead of the minutes a VM boot takes.
Why can HPA scale a Deployment to 20 replicas and the extra Pods still not run?
HPA only changes the desired Pod count; it doesn't create anywhere to run them. If the underlying node group has no spare CPU/memory and the Cluster Autoscaler (or Karpenter) isn't configured, or isn't fast enough, those extra Pods are scheduled in name only and sit in a Pending state, delivering zero additional capacity. Pod-level and node-level autoscaling are two separate control loops that both have to work.
A team argues "we should use Kubernetes because it's the industry standard." Is that a sufficient reason on its own?
No. "Industry standard" is a reason to consider EKS, not a reason to choose it over ECS by itself — the real question is whether the team needs the specific things that standardization buys (multi-cloud portability, a particular ecosystem tool, existing organizational Kubernetes expertise). Without one of those concretely in play, EKS adds a steep learning curve and a control-plane fee for standardization that is never actually exercised, while ECS solves the same orchestration problem with a fraction of the operational surface area.

Level 5 · Distributed Systems

Serverless Architecture with Lambda

● ~26 min read● Builds on Module 25● ShopSphere: moves order-confirmation email off a server entirely● AWS service

Bottom line: Lambda removes the server from "run this code" — no container to size, no node to patch, no capacity sitting idle between orders — but that payoff is specific to short-lived, event-shaped work. Bolt it onto an always-on API or a half-hour batch job and you're fighting the execution model instead of using it; this module is about knowing exactly which ShopSphere workloads are which.

01 Problem

Module 14/23's Order Created event fans out to Notification, whose job here is narrow: send a confirmation email. That work happens in short bursts tied directly to order volume and does nothing in between. Giving it its own EC2 fleet (Module 05) or its own container Deployment (Module 25) means provisioning capacity that mostly sits idle, sized for a guess at peak order volume the same way Module 01's pre-cloud ShopSphere had to guess a year of traffic. Lambda is built for exactly this shape of problem: code that runs only when something happens, for seconds at a time, and costs nothing the rest of the time.

02 Concept: what Lambda actually is, and statelessness

AWS Lambda runs your code in response to an event without you provisioning, sizing, or managing any server, container, or cluster. You supply a handler function; AWS supplies the execution environment, scales the number of concurrent invocations to match incoming events, and tears environments down when they're no longer needed — all of it hidden from you.

The property that makes this possible is statelessness: nothing is guaranteed to persist between invocations. Lambda may reuse the same underlying execution environment for a later invocation (that's exactly what makes a "warm" invocation fast — Section 03), but your code cannot rely on that reuse happening, or on anything written to local disk/memory surviving until the next call. Any state that actually needs to persist — an order record, a notification log, a session — goes to DynamoDB, S3, or ElastiCache, never to the function's own memory or disk.

A bug this causes constantly

Code that opens a database connection and caches it in a global variable "to save time" works perfectly in testing (one invocation, one warm environment) and then intermittently fails in production, because a fresh, cold execution environment has no such cached connection — and a reused warm one might be holding a connection that's since gone stale. Treat every invocation as possibly the first one on a brand-new environment.

03 Cold starts, and provisioned concurrency as the fix

A cold start happens when Lambda has no idle, already-initialized execution environment to reuse for an incoming invocation, so it must build one from scratch: download your code, start the language runtime, and run any initialization code outside your handler (opening a DB connection, loading a config file) before your handler logic even begins. A warm start reuses an environment that already did all of that for a previous invocation — the handler runs immediately.

Provisioned concurrency fixes this by keeping a specified number of execution environments permanently initialized and idling, ready to serve a request instantly — eliminating cold starts for the invocations that land on one. The trade-off is direct: you now pay for that provisioned capacity whether it's invoked or not, which reintroduces part of the "pay when idle" cost Lambda is otherwise supposed to eliminate. It's worth it exactly when cold-start latency is user-visible and unacceptable (a synchronous API path), and not worth it for background, asynchronous work like an order-confirmation email, where an extra few hundred milliseconds of delay is invisible to the customer.

04 Concurrency limits and throttling

Every AWS account has an account-level concurrent-execution limit, shared across every function in that account/region unless carved up. Two mechanisms carve it up per function: reserved concurrency sets both a guaranteed minimum and a hard maximum for one function (protecting it from being starved by others, and capping how much of a downstream dependency — like a database connection pool — it can hit at once); provisioned concurrency (Section 03) pre-initializes a slice of that reserved capacity so it's also always warm.

When incoming events exceed the available concurrency — account-level, or a function's own reserved ceiling — new invocations are throttled. What happens next depends on how the function was invoked: a synchronous caller (API Gateway, an ALB) gets an error back immediately; an asynchronous or poll-based source (SQS, EventBridge, S3 events) retries automatically and, after repeated failure, routes the event to a configured dead-letter queue or on-failure destination rather than silently dropping it.

05 Execution timeouts

Every Lambda invocation has a configured timeout, capped at a hard maximum of 15 minutes. This is the single clearest signal of what Lambda is not for: ShopSphere's nightly analytics aggregation across millions of orders, or any job whose runtime isn't tightly bounded and short, structurally cannot be a single Lambda invocation once it risks exceeding that ceiling — it needs a different compute model entirely, or needs to be decomposed into many short invocations (Step Functions orchestrating a sequence, or a fan-out over SQS) rather than one long one.

06 Event-driven triggers: ShopSphere's order-confirmation Lambda

Lambda doesn't run on a schedule you poll from the outside; it's invoked by a trigger — a source service that calls it directly or hands it a stream of events to process. The three ShopSphere already uses: S3 events (a new product image uploaded fires a thumbnail-generation function), SQS (Lambda polls a queue and processes messages in batches — Module 14's dead-letter-queue pattern applies directly), and EventBridge (a rule matches an event pattern and routes matching events to a target, including a Lambda function).

ShopSphere's order-confirmation email

When Order publishes its Order Created event (Module 14/23's fan-out), an EventBridge rule matching that event type invokes the order-confirmation-email Lambda. The function reads the order details from the event payload, renders the email, calls Amazon SES to send it, and writes a row to a DynamoDB notification log — then the execution environment goes idle, and ShopSphere pays nothing for it until the next order arrives.

07 Functional & non-functional requirements

FunctionalEnabled by
Send a confirmation email when an order is placedLambda triggered by an EventBridge rule on Order Created
Generate a thumbnail when a product image is uploadedLambda triggered by an S3 event notification
Process a batch of queued notification jobsLambda polling an SQS queue
Non-functional dimensionImpact
AvailabilityAWS manages the execution infrastructure's availability; your code's correctness is still entirely yours
ScalabilityScales concurrency automatically per incoming event volume, up to account/function limits
PerformanceCold starts add latency; provisioned concurrency trades cost for eliminating it
ReliabilityBuilt-in retries for async invocations; a DLQ/on-failure destination catches what still fails
SecurityEach function has its own IAM execution role, scoped narrowly — no shared instance role across unrelated workloads
MaintainabilityNo OS or runtime patching; still your dependency updates and code to maintain
ObservabilityCloudWatch Logs/Metrics automatically per invocation; X-Ray tracing available with minimal setup
CostPay per invocation and per-millisecond of duration; zero cost at zero traffic

08 The serverless cost model, contrasted directly with EC2

Lambda bills per invocation plus duration (rounded to the nearest millisecond) multiplied by configured memory. At zero traffic, the order-confirmation function costs exactly zero dollars — genuinely free when idle, not "minimum billing unit small." Compare this directly to Module 05's EC2: an m7g.large instance costs its full hourly rate whether it's processing 10,000 orders or sitting completely idle at 3am. That's the core trade Lambda makes — it's typically more expensive than a container or EC2 instance per unit of sustained, steady-state compute, but for spiky or idle-most-of-the-time workloads, "expensive per unit, but billed for almost nothing" beats "cheap per unit, but billed for 24 hours a day" by a wide margin.

09 Lambda vs containers vs EC2: three ShopSphere workloads

WorkloadShapePickWhy
Order-confirmation notificationShort, bursty, tied 1:1 to an eventLambdaIdle most of the time; cold start is invisible on an async, non-blocking path
Checkout API (Module 25's Order service)Always-on, latency-sensitive, synchronous, sustained steady trafficContainers/EC2Steady load makes per-unit cost the dominant factor, and cold starts are unacceptable on a customer-facing synchronous path
Nightly analytics aggregation jobLong-running, scheduled, not latency-sensitiveCould be eitherIf it reliably finishes well under 15 minutes and is easy to decompose, a scheduled Lambda (via EventBridge Scheduler) is simplest; if it's long, resource-heavy, or has unpredictable runtime, a scheduled container task (ECS) or AWS Batch avoids the timeout ceiling entirely

The decision in one line

Lambda wins when the workload is event-shaped and idle most of the time; containers/EC2 win when the workload is steady and latency-critical; "could be either" cases come down to whether 15 minutes is a comfortable ceiling or a risk you'd rather not carry.

10 When NOT to use it

Lambda is the wrong default for…

• Anything that can exceed 15 minutes, or whose runtime isn't tightly bounded — a timeout here is a hard failure, not a slow success.
• Steady, high-volume, always-on traffic — the checkout API's sustained load makes per-unit-of-compute cost the dominant factor, where containers/EC2 are typically cheaper.
• Workloads needing a persistent connection or in-memory state across requests (a WebSocket server holding live connections, an in-memory cache warmed over hours) — statelessness fights this directly.
• Extremely latency-sensitive synchronous paths that can't tolerate occasional cold starts, unless provisioned concurrency's extra cost is accepted explicitly.

11 Trade-offs

Lambda trades per-unit compute efficiency and fine-grained control for zero operational burden at the infrastructure layer and a cost model that tracks actual usage precisely. It's the fastest path on Module 01's abstraction spectrum from "idea" to "running in production" for event-shaped work, and the easiest to over-apply to the wrong shape of workload simply because it's the one requiring the least setup.

12 Failure scenarios

FailureWhat happensMitigation
A traffic spike exceeds concurrency limitsNew invocations are throttled; sync callers see errors immediately, async sources retryReserved concurrency sized to the real need; request a limit increase ahead of a known flash sale
A downstream dependency (SES, the database) is slow or downInvocations pile up waiting, consuming concurrency, worsening the throttling riskTimeouts set tight to the dependency's own SLA; circuit breaker pattern (Module 17)
A bug causes every invocation to failAsync sources retry repeatedly, then land in the DLQ/on-failure destination — or silently exhaust retries if none is configuredAlways configure a DLQ; alarm on DLQ depth, not just on invocation errors
Cold start latency spikes under sudden loadMany simultaneous first invocations each pay the full init cost at onceProvisioned concurrency sized for the known spike (a flash sale), not left at zero

13 Security

  • One IAM execution role per function, scoped to exactly what that function touches — the order-confirmation function gets SES send permission and DynamoDB write access to its own log table, nothing broader.
  • No embedded credentials — same Module 03 pattern as an EC2 instance role, just scoped even more narrowly per function instead of per fleet.
  • Environment variables encrypted via KMS for anything sensitive, or sourced from Secrets Manager at invocation time rather than stored in plaintext configuration.
  • VPC attachment when a function needs to reach a private resource (an RDS/Aurora instance inside the VPC) — adds a small amount of extra cold-start latency from ENI setup, a real trade-off against Section 03/04's concerns.
  • Function URLs or API Gateway in front of any Lambda invoked directly from the internet, with the same authN/authZ concerns as any other API entry point.

14 Production configuration

DevelopmentProduction
ConcurrencyUnreserved, shared account poolReserved concurrency sized per function; provisioned concurrency on latency-sensitive paths
TimeoutDefault/generousTight to the actual expected duration — a runaway invocation shouldn't burn 15 minutes of billed time
Error handlingNone configuredDLQ or on-failure destination on every async trigger, with an alarm on queue depth
DeploymentDirect console editsVersioned, deployed through the Module 27 pipeline with aliases for safe rollout

15 Monitoring

CloudWatch automatically records invocation count, duration, error count and throttle count per function with no extra setup — a meaningfully lower monitoring floor than EC2's "memory metrics need an agent" gotcha from Module 05. Watch duration against the configured timeout (approaching it on a rising trend predicts timeout failures before they happen), throttle count (a direct signal of insufficient concurrency), and DLQ/on-failure destination depth (events that exhausted retries and need a human). X-Ray tracing, enabled with one configuration flag, shows exactly where time goes inside a single invocation — init phase (cold start) vs. handler logic vs. a slow downstream call.

16 Hands-on lab

  1. Sketch the event payload ShopSphere's Order Created event would need to contain for the order-confirmation-email Lambda to render a complete email without making any additional database calls.
  2. ShopSphere's nightly analytics job currently runs in under 8 minutes but is growing 15% a month as order volume grows. At what point does continuing to run it as a single Lambda invocation become a real operational risk, and what would you redesign it into first?
  3. Write the IAM policy (JSON) for the order-confirmation Lambda's execution role: SES send permission, and DynamoDB PutItem on exactly one table, nothing else.

17 Interview questions

Why can't a Lambda function safely cache a database connection in a global variable across invocations?
Lambda may reuse an execution environment across invocations (producing a fast "warm start"), but that reuse is never guaranteed, and the function's code has no way to know, on any given invocation, whether it's running cold or warm. A cached connection on a cold environment simply doesn't exist yet; on a warm one it might have gone stale since the last invocation. State that must be reliable has to live outside the function — in DynamoDB, S3, ElastiCache — not in function memory.
A traffic spike causes Lambda invocations to be throttled. What happens next, and does it depend on how the function was invoked?
Yes. A synchronous invocation (via API Gateway or an ALB) returns an error to the caller immediately when throttled — there's no built-in retry on that path. An asynchronous or poll-based invocation (SQS, EventBridge, S3 events) retries automatically per the source's own retry behavior, and after exhausting retries, routes the event to a configured dead-letter queue or on-failure destination rather than dropping it silently — provided one was configured.
Why is Lambda often described as "more expensive per unit of compute" than EC2, and when does that stop mattering?
Lambda's per-millisecond, per-invocation pricing is genuinely higher than the equivalent sustained EC2/container compute, because you're paying a premium for on-demand provisioning and zero idle cost. That premium stops mattering once idle time dominates: a workload that's busy 2% of the day pays Lambda's higher per-unit rate on 2% of a day's compute, versus EC2's lower per-unit rate on 100% of a day's compute whether used or not — the idle 98% is where EC2 actually loses.

Level 6 · DevOps

CI/CD Pipelines on AWS

● ~27 min read● Builds on Module 26● ShopSphere: ships the containerized Order service safely● Multiple AWS services

Bottom line: every stage between a commit and production traffic is an architecture decision, not just tooling plumbing — and the deployment strategy and rollback trigger you choose at the end of that pipeline determine how expensive a bad deploy actually turns out to be. ShopSphere's containerized Order service (Module 25) is the concrete workload this module ships, safely, end to end.

01 Problem

Module 25 containerized Order; Module 26 moved notification work to Lambda. Neither module addressed how a code change actually gets from a developer's commit into production without someone manually building an image, manually pushing it, and manually telling ECS/EKS to roll it out — a process that's slow, inconsistent between people, and has no built-in gate against a broken build or a failing test ever reaching customers. A production-grade pipeline makes "ship a change" a repeatable, automated, auditable sequence of stages, each one a checkpoint a bad change has to pass through.

02 The full pipeline, stage by stage

A production CI/CD pipeline for Order looks like this, explicitly:

StageWhat happens
1. CommitDeveloper pushes to a feature branch
2. Pull RequestCode review; automated checks (lint, type-check) run before merge is allowed
3. BuildCompile/transpile the application, resolve dependencies
4. Unit testsFast, isolated tests run against the build output; a failure stops the pipeline here
5. Security scanStatic analysis and dependency vulnerability scanning on the code and its dependencies
6. Container buildDocker image built from the Module 25 Dockerfile, tagged with the commit SHA
7. Push to ECRImage scanned again on push (Module 25's ECR scanning), stored as the immutable artifact for this commit
8. DeployThe new image rolled out to ECS/EKS using a chosen strategy (Section 06)
9. Integration testsTests against the newly deployed version in a real environment, verifying it actually works end to end
10. ProductionTraffic fully shifted; the previous version's capacity is released

Each stage is a gate: a failure at any point stops the pipeline before the change reaches the next stage, let alone production.

03 How it works: CodePipeline, CodeBuild, CodeDeploy and ECR

Four AWS services map directly onto that stage list. CodePipeline is the orchestrator — it defines the stages above as a pipeline and sequences them, triggered by a source change. CodeBuild is a managed build environment that runs the build, unit test, and security-scan stages, and builds the container image, all from a buildspec.yml describing the commands to run. Amazon ECR (Module 25) stores the resulting image as the one immutable artifact every later stage references. CodeDeploy handles the actual deployment stage — shifting traffic from the running version to the new one according to a chosen strategy, and is what performs an automatic rollback if that strategy's health checks fail.

04 ShopSphere use case

Order service's pipeline

A commit to Order's repository triggers CodePipeline. CodeBuild compiles the service, runs its unit test suite, runs a dependency vulnerability scan, builds the Docker image, and pushes it to shopsphere/order-service on ECR tagged with the commit SHA. CodePipeline then invokes CodeDeploy to roll that image out to the ECS service (or EKS Deployment) using a blue/green strategy, running integration tests against the new version before shifting production traffic fully over.

05 Functional & non-functional requirements

FunctionalEnabled by
Automatically test every change before it can reach productionCodeBuild's unit test and security-scan stages as pipeline gates
Deploy a new Order version without taking checkout offlineCodeDeploy's blue/green or rolling strategy (Section 06)
Know exactly what's running in production at any timeImmutable, commit-SHA-tagged images in ECR (Module 25)
Non-functional dimensionImpact
AvailabilityA good deployment strategy keeps checkout serving traffic throughout a rollout
ReliabilityAutomated gates catch broken builds and failing tests before they ever reach customers
SecurityScanning stages catch known vulnerabilities before they're deployed, not after
ObservabilityEvery deployment is logged, versioned and traceable to the exact commit that produced it
MaintainabilityShipping a change becomes the same repeatable process every time, independent of who's doing it
CostCodeBuild bills per build-minute; blue/green briefly doubles running capacity (Section 06)

06 Blue/green vs canary vs rolling

Blue/greenCanaryRolling
How it worksNew version ("green") deployed fully alongside the old ("blue"); traffic cut over all at once (or in one scripted shift)New version takes a small traffic slice first, increasing in steps if healthyOld instances/Pods replaced by new ones gradually, a few at a time
Rollback speedInstant — just cut traffic back to blue, which is still runningFast for the affected slice; the rest of production was never exposedSlower — must redeploy the previous version across the replaced instances
Blast radius of a bad deployZero, until cutover; then 100% at once if the cutover itself isn't gradualSmallest — limited to the canary's traffic percentage until proven healthyPartial — some live traffic hits the bad version before it's caught
CostBriefly double capacity — both versions fully provisioned at onceSmall extra capacity for the canary sliceNo extra capacity — replaces in place
Speed to full rolloutFast once healthySlowest — each step waits to confirm healthModerate

ShopSphere's choice for Order

Blue/green for the checkout-critical Order service: instant rollback matters more than the brief doubled-capacity cost on a service where even a few minutes of partial bad traffic is a real revenue and trust cost. A lower-stakes internal service might reasonably use rolling instead, trading rollback speed for zero extra capacity cost.

07 Rollback triggers

None of the three strategies above are safe without an automated signal telling CodeDeploy the new version is unhealthy during the rollout, before it reaches 100% of traffic. The standard signal is a CloudWatch alarm watching a deployment-health metric — most commonly request error rate, but latency or a custom application metric works too — configured to compare the new version's behavior against a threshold (or against the old version's own baseline) while the deployment is in progress. If that alarm goes into an alarm state during the rollout window, CodeDeploy automatically halts the deployment and rolls back to the last known-good version, with no human needing to notice and react first.

The mistake that defeats this entirely

Configuring the rollback alarm on a metric that's too slow to move (a 1-hour aggregation window) or too noisy to trust (raw request count instead of error rate) means the alarm either fires too late to matter or never fires at all. The alarm's evaluation period has to be shorter than how long you're willing to serve bad traffic to real customers.

08 Alternatives: modern tooling is equally valid

CodePipeline/CodeBuild/CodeDeploy are the AWS-native path, deeply integrated with ECR, ECS/EKS, and IAM. GitHub Actions and GitLab CI solve the same staged-pipeline problem and are equally valid choices — particularly for a team already living in GitHub or GitLab for source control, where keeping the pipeline definition next to the code in the same platform avoids an extra tool and extra context-switching. None of this module's concepts (stage gates, blue/green vs canary vs rolling, alarm-triggered rollback) are AWS-specific; they're implemented differently depending on which orchestrator you pick, but the underlying architecture decision is the same regardless of tool.

09 When NOT to default to the AWS-native pipeline

Consider GitHub Actions/GitLab CI instead when…

• The team's source control and review workflow is already fully in GitHub/GitLab, and keeping pipeline config alongside the code (rather than as separate AWS resources) reduces real day-to-day friction.
• The org deploys to more than just AWS, and a single pipeline tool spanning multiple targets is worth more than AWS-native integration depth on any one of them.
• The team already has deep operational expertise in one of those tools and no compelling reason to add a second CI/CD system alongside it.

None of this is a case against CodePipeline/CodeBuild/CodeDeploy — for a team already on AWS with no strong existing CI/CD investment elsewhere, the native integration with ECR, ECS/EKS and IAM (no separate credentials/roles to wire up for a third-party tool to reach AWS) is a real, uncomplicated advantage.

10 Trade-offs

Every deployment strategy in Section 06 trades cost or speed for safety, and there's no option that's best on all three axes at once. The pipeline as a whole trades upfront setup effort (writing buildspecs, wiring IAM roles between stages, configuring the rollback alarm correctly) for a dramatically smaller blast radius per bad change once it's running — the investment pays for itself the first time it catches a broken build before a human would have.

11 Failure scenarios

FailureWhat happensMitigation
Unit tests failPipeline stops before a container image is even builtThis is the pipeline working correctly — the gate did its job
A bad deploy passes a weak canary checkTraffic ramps toward the bad version before the real problem surfacesAlarm on the metric that actually reflects customer impact, not a vanity metric
CodeDeploy rollback itself fails or is slowCustomers keep hitting the bad version longer than intendedTest the rollback path itself, not only the forward deploy path, before relying on it
The pipeline's own IAM role is over-permissionedA compromised pipeline can push/deploy far beyond what CI/CD actually needsScope each stage's role narrowly (Section 12) — least privilege applied to the pipeline itself

12 Security

  • Per-stage IAM roles, scoped narrowly — CodeBuild's role can push to ECR and nothing else; CodeDeploy's role can update the ECS service/EKS Deployment and nothing else. A compromised build stage shouldn't be able to touch production IAM or other services' infrastructure.
  • Secrets never in the buildspec or pipeline definition in plaintext — pulled from Secrets Manager/Parameter Store at build time.
  • Security scanning as a hard gate, not advisory — a critical vulnerability found in the scan stage should fail the pipeline, not just log a warning someone might read later.
  • Manual approval gate before production for changes that warrant a human sign-off (a schema migration, a payment-path change) — CodePipeline supports this as an explicit stage, distinct from full automation.
  • CloudTrail logging on every pipeline action, so "who deployed what, when" is always answerable after the fact.

13 Production configuration

Development/stagingProduction
Deployment strategyRolling or direct replace — speed over safetyBlue/green or canary — safety over speed
Approval gateNone, fully automatedManual approval before the production stage, for high-risk changes
Rollback alarmLoose thresholds or noneTight threshold tied to an actual SLO (Module 24's error budget concept), short evaluation window
Integration testsSmoke tests onlyFull suite against the newly deployed version before traffic fully shifts

14 Monitoring

Beyond the rollback alarm itself (Section 07), track the pipeline as its own system: deployment frequency (how often ShopSphere actually ships), lead time from commit to production, and deployment failure rate — three of the four widely-used DevOps performance indicators, and a direct signal of whether the pipeline is helping or just adding ceremony. CodePipeline execution history and CodeBuild logs give the raw data; a dashboard surfacing failure rate and rollback frequency over time catches a pipeline quietly degrading before it becomes an incident.

15 Cost

CodeBuild bills per build-minute consumed, scaled by the compute size chosen for the build environment — the main lever for cost control is keeping build/test time tight rather than over-provisioning build compute "to be safe." CodePipeline charges a small flat monthly fee per active pipeline. The real cost driver is usually the deployment strategy itself: blue/green's brief doubled capacity (Section 06) is a real, if short-lived, line item, multiplied across every service that deploys this way.

16 Hands-on lab

  1. Write a minimal buildspec.yml (or describe its phases in plain English) for Order's CodeBuild stage: install dependencies, run unit tests, build the Docker image, push to ECR — tagged with the commit SHA.
  2. Design the CloudWatch alarm for Order's rollback trigger: name the exact metric, the threshold, and the evaluation period, and justify why that window is short enough to matter during a real deployment.
  3. ShopSphere's Payment service is lower-traffic than Order but handles money. Argue for blue/green, canary, or rolling for Payment specifically, and say what would change your answer.

17 Interview questions

What's the core difference in blast radius between a canary deployment and a rolling deployment?
A canary deployment exposes only a small, controlled percentage of traffic to the new version first, and only increases that percentage after confirming it's healthy — the rest of production traffic is never exposed to a bad version before it's caught. A rolling deployment replaces instances/Pods gradually but doesn't control traffic percentage directly; by the time enough replicas have rolled, a meaningful share of live traffic has already hit the new version, bad or not, before anyone notices.
Why does a rollback need to be triggered by a CloudWatch alarm rather than a person watching a dashboard?
A human watching a dashboard is a single point of failure with reaction time measured in minutes at best, and no guarantee anyone's actually watching during every deployment window. A CloudWatch alarm evaluates continuously, reacts in the time it takes the metric to cross threshold plus its evaluation period, and triggers CodeDeploy's rollback automatically — turning "someone has to notice and act" into "the system reacts on its own," which is the difference between a bad deploy lasting seconds versus minutes or longer.
Blue/green deployments briefly double running capacity. Is that always worth it?
No — it's worth it when instant, zero-risk rollback matters more than the short-lived extra compute cost, which is true for customer-facing, revenue-critical paths like checkout. For a lower-stakes internal service, or one with very large fleets where doubling capacity is a real cost even briefly, a rolling deployment's slightly slower rollback may be the better trade, since the cost of a brief partial exposure to a bad version is lower there too.

Level 6 · DevOps

Infrastructure as Code: CFN, CDK, Terraform

● ~27 min read● Builds on Module 27● ShopSphere: stops clicking the console

Bottom line: once ShopSphere runs across Dev, Staging and Prod — and across the Organizations account structure from Module 04 — a human clicking through the console to build each environment stops being viable. Infrastructure as Code (IaC) makes infrastructure a versioned, reviewable, repeatable artifact instead of a one-off action nobody can reconstruct. The tool you pick (CloudFormation, CDK, or Terraform) matters less than committing to the discipline itself.

01 Problem

ShopSphere's pipeline (Module 27) now builds, tests and deploys application code automatically — but the VPC, subnets, security groups, ALB and EC2 fleet those deployments land on were still built by hand, once, by whoever was on call that week. Nobody can say with confidence whether Staging's VPC actually matches Prod's. Standing up a new environment (a second region, a sandbox account) means a human repeating a half-remembered sequence of console clicks, and every "quick fix" made directly in the console quietly diverges that environment from every other one. Infrastructure as Code treats the infrastructure definition itself as source code: written once, reviewed in a pull request, applied identically every time, and diffable against what's actually running.

02 Declarative vs. imperative infrastructure

An imperative script says how: "create a VPC, then create a subnet inside it, then create a route table, then associate it…" — a literal sequence of steps, in order, that you must get right and that must tolerate re-running without creating duplicates. A declarative definition says what: "there should exist one VPC with these two subnets and this route table" — and the tool's engine diffs that desired state against the real world and figures out the steps itself.

CloudFormation, CDK (which synthesizes to CloudFormation) and Terraform are all fundamentally declarative: you describe the end state, not the procedure. This is precisely why they're safe to run twice — applying the same template against infrastructure that already matches it does nothing, which is the property that makes IaC trustworthy enough to run unattended in a pipeline.

Where imperative scripting still sneaks in

A shell script with a sequence of aws ec2 create-… CLI calls is imperative IaC — it is still code, still versioned, but it doesn't know how to reconcile drift or safely re-run after a partial failure. Most teams that start this way migrate to a declarative tool once they feel that pain on a production incident.

03 Immutable infrastructure: replace, don't patch

Immutable infrastructure extends the declarative idea to running resources: instead of SSH-ing into a live EC2 instance to patch it, you build a new AMI (or container image) with the fix baked in, and replace the instance wholesale — the old one is terminated, never mutated. The same discipline applies at the IaC layer: changing an instance's AMI ID in the template doesn't "upgrade" the running instance, it destroys it and launches a new one from the new image.

Why ShopSphere adopted this the hard way

Before IaC, a manually patched checkout instance in Staging accumulated two years of undocumented hotfixes. When it finally failed, nobody could rebuild an equivalent instance from scratch — the fix list existed only in that one instance's memory. Immutable infrastructure makes that scenario structurally impossible: every instance is fully described by its launch template and AMI, so losing any single instance is a non-event, and "rebuild from scratch" is a normal Tuesday, not an emergency.

The trade-off: immutable replacement is slower per change than an in-place patch, and it demands that application state never live only on an instance's local disk — which is exactly the stateless-architecture discipline Module 19's scalability scenarios already required of ShopSphere's compute tier.

04 Drift and drift detection

Drift is the gap between what your IaC template declares and what's actually running — it happens the moment someone opens the console under incident pressure and tweaks a security group rule, resizes an instance, or adds a tag, without updating the template to match. From that point on, the template is a description of infrastructure that no longer exists, and the next ordinary apply can produce a surprising result: it may silently revert the manual fix, or worse, fail in a way that's hard to diagnose because the running world and the declared world disagree about the starting point.

ToolDrift detection mechanism
CloudFormationExplicit detect-stack-drift API/console action, compares live resource properties against the template per-resource and per-stack
CDKInherits CloudFormation's drift detection, since a CDK app is CloudFormation underneath
Terraformterraform plan itself is a drift check every time — it refreshes state against real infrastructure and shows exactly what changed before deciding what to do about it

ShopSphere incident, traced to drift

An engineer widened a Staging security group during a late-night debugging session and forgot to update the Terraform source. Two weeks later, an unrelated terraform apply for a different change silently narrowed that rule back — because Terraform's state still reflected the original, narrower definition. The debugging session's workaround "disappeared" with no commit, no PR, and no obvious link to the apply that caused it. The fix going forward: a scheduled drift check in CI, and a hard rule that console changes in Staging/Prod require a same-day matching commit.

05 State: the ledger that makes updates possible

A declarative tool must know what it already created, to compute the diff between "what exists" and "what should exist" — that record is state. CloudFormation manages this for you automatically and invisibly: the stack itself is the state, stored and versioned by the CloudFormation service, with no file for a team to lose or corrupt.

Terraform's state is explicit and your responsibility: a JSON file (terraform.tfstate) mapping every resource address in your configuration to its real-world ID and attributes. This is Terraform's single most important operational detail for a team: the state file must live in shared, remote storage with locking — never on one engineer's laptop.

Failure mode of local stateWhat goes wrong
State on one laptopA second engineer runs apply with no knowledge of the first engineer's changes — Terraform thinks resources it's never seen are missing, and may try to recreate them
No lockingTwo concurrent apply runs race to write the same state file, corrupting it or losing one run's changes entirely
Laptop lost or disk wipedThe only record of which real AWS resources Terraform is managing is gone; recovery means manually re-importing every resource by ID

ShopSphere's Terraform state is stored in an S3 bucket (versioned, encrypted with KMS) with DynamoDB providing state locking — a write lock taken for the duration of every plan/apply so two engineers, or two CI pipeline runs, can never touch state at the same time. This is infrastructure ShopSphere had to build once, manually, before Terraform could manage anything else: the classic IaC bootstrapping problem.

06 Modules & reusable environments

A module is a parameterized, reusable unit of infrastructure — write the VPC/subnet/ALB/EC2 pattern once, then call it three times with different inputs for Dev, Staging and Prod. This is where IaC stops being "a script that builds one thing" and becomes "the one authoritative definition of what a ShopSphere environment is," instantiated per account in the Organizations structure from Module 04 — one module, one Dev account, one Staging account, one Prod account, three different parameter sets, zero copy-pasted templates to keep in sync by hand.

ParameterDevStagingProd
VPC CIDR10.10.0.0/1610.20.0.0/1610.30.0.0/16
Instance typet4g.smallm7g.largem7g.xlarge
ASG min/max1 / 12 / 43 / 20
Multi-AZNo (cost)YesYes, 3 AZs
AWS accountShopSphere-DevShopSphere-StagingShopSphere-Prod

07 CloudFormation vs. CDK vs. Terraform

There is no universally "best" choice here — the three tools optimize for different things, and a Principal Architect picks based on the team and the estate, not a scorecard.

CloudFormationAWS CDKTerraform
AuthoringYAML/JSON, declarative, verboseReal code — TypeScript, Python, Java, C#, Go — synthesizes to CloudFormationHCL (purpose-built declarative language), with a large module ecosystem
Cloud scopeAWS-onlyAWS-onlyMulti-cloud — AWS, Azure, GCP, and hundreds of other providers in one tool
State managementManaged by AWS automatically, freeManaged by AWS automatically (it's CloudFormation underneath)Your responsibility — remote backend + locking must be deliberately set up (Module 28 §05)
CostFree (pay only for resources created)Free (pay only for resources created)Free (OSS core); HCP Terraform / Terraform Enterprise add paid state-hosting and governance
ExpressivenessLimited to what CloudFormation's intrinsic functions and conditions allow — loops and abstraction are awkwardFull programming language: loops, classes, unit tests, published constructs/packages for reuseHCL supports loops, conditionals and modules well, without needing a general-purpose language
EcosystemAWS-maintained resource coverage, usually day-one for new servicesRides on CloudFormation's coverage; huge catalog of community "L2/L3" constructsEnormous public Registry of community and verified modules, across every major cloud
Learning curveLower if you already know YAML; verbose for complex stacksLower for developers who already code; another abstraction layer to debug when synth output surprises youA new language (HCL) and a new state-management discipline to learn

Selection criteria, not a verdict

• AWS-only shop, team comfortable with YAML, wants zero extra state infrastructure to run: CloudFormation.
• AWS-only shop, engineers who'd rather write Python/TypeScript than YAML, want unit-testable infra and rich abstraction: CDK.
• Multi-cloud today or plausibly tomorrow, or already standardized on Terraform/HCL elsewhere in the org, and willing to own state management: Terraform.
ShopSphere, being AWS-only with a platform team that already maintains Terraform modules for a separate internal tool, chose Terraform — a decision driven by existing team skill and multi-cloud optionality, not by any of the three being objectively superior.

08 When NOT to use IaC (or the wrong IaC tool)

IaC isn't automatically the right call for…

• A true one-off experiment — a resource you'll delete within the hour doesn't earn back the overhead of writing and reviewing a module for it.
• Fast-moving application-level configuration that changes every deploy (a feature flag value, an image tag) — that belongs in application config or Parameter Store/Secrets Manager, not re-planned as infrastructure on every commit.
• CDK specifically, for a team with no engineers who want to own a programming-language toolchain for infrastructure — the abstraction can become a black box exactly when you most need to understand what it synthesized.
• Terraform specifically, for a tiny AWS-only team unwilling to own remote state, locking, and backend setup — CloudFormation removes that whole operational burden for free.

09 Trade-offs

DimensionTrade-off
ComplexityIaC adds an entire toolchain (plan/apply workflow, state, modules, pipelines) on top of the infrastructure it manages — complexity moved, not removed
Speed of changeA reviewed, planned change is slower than a console click — by design; that friction is what prevents undocumented drift
Lock-inCDK and CloudFormation templates are AWS-specific; Terraform configuration is somewhat portable across providers but provider-specific resource blocks still aren't
Operational overheadCloudFormation/CDK: near zero (AWS manages state). Terraform: real, ongoing (backend, locking, version upgrades, module maintenance)
Blast radiusA single bad module change, applied automatically, can misconfigure every environment it was reused into — the same reuse that keeps environments consistent also propagates a mistake everywhere at once

10 Failure scenarios

FailureWhat happensMitigation
Apply fails partway throughSome resources created/updated, others not — infrastructure left in a partially-applied, inconsistent stateCloudFormation auto-rolls-back a failed stack update by default; Terraform requires re-running apply to converge, so partial applies need idempotent modules
State file corrupted or lostTerraform no longer knows what it manages; the next plan may propose destroying/recreating everythingVersioned, encrypted S3 backend with locking; regular state backups; never hand-edit state
Module change applied org-wideA bug in the shared VPC module breaks Dev, Staging and Prod on the next apply to eachPin module versions per environment; apply to Dev first, then Staging, then Prod, never all at once
Drift causes an unexpected destroyA manually-added resource dependency isn't in the template; removing the "unmanaged" console change via apply cascades into deleting something still in useMandatory drift detection in CI before any apply; plan output reviewed by a human before apply in Staging/Prod

11 Security

  • A dedicated, least-privilege deploy role assumed by the CI/CD pipeline (Module 27) to run apply — never a human's long-lived credentials, and never broader than the resources that environment's modules actually touch.
  • State file encryption at rest (S3 + KMS for Terraform) — state often contains sensitive attributes (database passwords generated at creation time, for example) in plaintext unless explicitly marked sensitive and the backend is encrypted.
  • Policy-as-code gates (e.g. Open Policy Agent / Sentinel / CloudFormation Guard) that block an apply which would, say, create a public S3 bucket or an unencrypted database — enforced automatically, not caught in code review by chance.
  • Full audit trail via CloudTrail for every API call CloudFormation/CDK/Terraform makes on your behalf, plus your CI system's own deploy logs tying every infrastructure change back to a commit and a reviewer.

12 Production configuration

DevelopmentProduction
Apply triggerAny engineer, any time, from their laptopOnly via pipeline, on merge to main, after review
Plan reviewOptionalMandatory human approval of the plan diff before apply
State backendCan be a shared non-prod bucketDedicated, tightly-access-controlled bucket/table per environment tier
Module versionsCan track the module's latest commitPinned to an exact, tested module version/tag
Drift checksAd hocScheduled (e.g. nightly) with alerting on any detected drift

13 Cost

IaC tooling itself is nearly free — CloudFormation and CDK cost nothing beyond the resources they create; Terraform's open-source core is free, and its remote state backend (an S3 bucket plus a DynamoDB table for locking) costs a negligible few cents a month. The real cost driver is operational: Terraform's self-managed state and backend setup is engineering time CloudFormation/CDK simply don't require; conversely, a managed offering like HCP Terraform trades a subscription fee for exactly that operational burden. The highest-leverage cost optimization in this module isn't dollars — it's the incident cost IaC prevents: a reproducible environment turns "rebuild Staging from scratch" from a multi-day fire drill into a routine, unattended pipeline run.

14 Hands-on lab

  1. Write the module interface. Sketch (as a short table, not real HCL) the input parameters a ShopSphere "network + compute" module would need to support Dev, Staging and Prod from the §06 table above, plus one you think is missing.
  2. Diagnose a drift incident. A Terraform plan for Prod shows it wants to delete a security group rule nobody remembers adding to the template. Walk through, step by step, how you'd confirm whether this is safe to apply or a sign of undocumented drift, before running apply.
  3. Pick a tool with reasons. ShopSphere's data-engineering team is standing up a brand-new analytics stack that must run identically on AWS and on a client's existing GCP environment. Using the selection criteria in §07, name the IaC tool you'd pick and justify it on at least three factors.

15 Interview questions

Why is Terraform state management considered a bigger operational responsibility than CloudFormation's?
CloudFormation's state is the stack itself, tracked and persisted by the AWS service with no file for a user to manage, lose, or corrupt. Terraform's state is an explicit artifact (a JSON file) that the user must store remotely, encrypt, and lock for concurrent access themselves — losing it, corrupting it, or letting two applies race against it are all failure modes Terraform users must engineer around that simply don't exist for CloudFormation users.
A teammate says "we don't need drift detection, we never touch the console." Is that reasoning sound?
Not in practice. Drift also comes from AWS-initiated changes (e.g. a security patch applied by an AWS-managed service), from other automation outside the IaC pipeline, and from human exceptions made under incident pressure that this teammate is implicitly assuming won't happen. Drift detection is cheap insurance against an assumption ("we never touch the console") that one bad night reliably breaks.
Why would a team deliberately choose CloudFormation over CDK even though CDK is "more expressive"?
Expressiveness is a double-edged sword: a CDK app is a real program that can hide what it will actually do to your infrastructure until you read the synthesized CloudFormation template, and it adds a language runtime and dependency tree (npm/pip packages) to the deployment pipeline. A team that wants the simplest possible mental model — "this YAML file is exactly what gets created, nothing more" — and doesn't need CDK's abstraction/reuse benefits may rationally prefer CloudFormation's lower-ceiling, lower-surprise directness.
