Level 7 · Site Reliability Engineering

Observability: Metrics, Logs, Traces, SLOs

● ~29 min read● Builds on Module 28● ShopSphere: can finally see across its own microservices● AWS services

Bottom line: now that ShopSphere's checkout path is a chain of independent microservices (Module 25) deployed by an automated pipeline (Module 27) running on IaC-managed infrastructure (Module 28), a single "checkout is slow" complaint can no longer be diagnosed by looking at one server. Observability is what lets an engineer ask an unanticipated question about that failure — not just watch a dashboard of failures someone predicted in advance.

01 Problem

Before Module 25, ShopSphere's checkout was effectively one process on one instance: a slow checkout meant "SSH in, check the logs, check the CPU." After Module 25, that same checkout request crosses API Gateway, the Order service, the Payment service, and the Inventory service — four independent, independently-scaled, independently-deployed pieces, each perfectly healthy on its own dashboard, while the request that touches all four takes two seconds end to end. No single service's metrics show the problem, because the problem only exists in the gaps between services.

02 Concept: monitoring vs. observability

Monitoring is watching for failure modes you already predicted: "alert me if CPU exceeds 80%," "alert me if the health check fails." It answers questions written in advance. Observability is a property of the system itself — whether its external outputs (metrics, logs, traces) carry enough information to let an engineer construct and answer a brand-new question they didn't think to ask until the incident was already happening: "which of these four services, for this one specific slow request, added the latency?" Monitoring tells you something is wrong. Observability lets you find out why, for a failure mode nobody wrote an alarm for, because nobody could have predicted every way four independent services might interact badly.

Four categories of signal build that capability. Three are the classic "pillars"; CloudTrail is a fourth, with a fundamentally different job.

SignalAWS serviceAnswers
MetricsCloudWatch (metrics)"How much / how many, over time?" — numeric time series, cheap to store, cheap to alarm on
LogsCloudWatch Logs"What exactly happened, in this one request?" — structured records, the richest detail, the most expensive to store at volume
TracesAWS X-Ray"Where did the time go, across every service this one request touched?" — the causal chain monitoring alone can't see
API audit trailAWS CloudTrail"Who changed what, in the AWS account itself?" — security and compliance, not application behavior

03 How it works

Metrics (CloudWatch): numeric data points over time — request count, latency, error count, CPU — collected automatically for most AWS resources and emittable as custom metrics from application code. Cheap enough to retain for years and to alarm on in near real time; too coarse, alone, to explain why a specific request was slow.

Logs (CloudWatch Logs): a timestamped record per event. The difference between a useful and a useless log line, at scale, is structure: a free-text line like "order 88212 failed" is only searchable by string match, while a structured JSON line — {"orderId":"88212","service":"payment","event":"charge_failed","reason":"card_declined","latencyMs":340} — is queryable as data. CloudWatch Logs Insights runs SQL-like queries directly over structured fields, turning "grep and hope" into "filter by reason = card_declined and aggregate by service."

Traces (X-Ray): a trace follows one request across every service it touches, as a tree of timed segments (one per service) and subsegments (calls that service makes, e.g. to its database). Each service's SDK adds its own segment and propagates a trace ID header to the next service it calls, so X-Ray can reassemble the whole chain and show exactly how much of the total latency belongs to each hop.

CloudTrail: records every API call made against the AWS account — who (which IAM identity) called which API, on which resource, from where, and whether it succeeded. It answers "did someone change this security group at 2am" and "which role deleted that S3 bucket," not "why was this HTTP request slow." Conflating the two is a common mistake: CloudTrail is a security and compliance control, not an application-performance tool, and the two pipelines (app observability vs. audit trail) are usually built, retained and accessed completely differently.

04 ShopSphere use case

Instrumenting the checkout saga end to end

Every service in ShopSphere's checkout saga (Order → Payment → Inventory → Shipment → Notification) emits structured JSON logs tagged with a shared correlationId, custom CloudWatch metrics for its own RED numbers (requests, errors, duration), and an X-Ray segment for every inbound and outbound call. When a customer reports a slow checkout, an engineer pulls the trace for that one correlationId, sees the waterfall from §03, and goes straight to the Payment service's call to the external payment provider — no guessing, no SSH-ing into four different services hoping to spot the slow one.

05 Functional & non-functional requirements

FunctionalEnabled by
Diagnose which service added latency to a slow checkoutX-Ray distributed tracing across the saga
Find every log line for one specific failed orderStructured logs correlated by a shared request/order ID, queried via Logs Insights
Know who changed a production security groupCloudTrail
Alert on-call before customers notice a problemCloudWatch Alarms on metrics tied to an SLO, not raw infrastructure stats
Non-functional dimensionImpact of an observability stack
AvailabilityFaster diagnosis shortens incident duration directly — MTTR is largely an observability problem, not a fixing-the-bug problem
PerformanceTracing/logging overhead is real but small when sampled correctly; unsampled, verbose tracing can itself add latency
SecurityCloudTrail is often the only record that proves what happened during a security incident
MaintainabilityNew engineers can understand an unfamiliar service's real behavior from its telemetry, without reading all its code
CostLogs and traces are usually the fastest-growing line item in an AWS bill once microservices multiply — see §13

06 Alternatives

CloudWatch + X-Ray + CloudTrailSelf-hosted OpenSearch + Prometheus/GrafanaThird-party (Datadog, New Relic, Honeycomb, …)
Setup effortLow — native, auto-wired for most AWS resourcesHigh — you run and scale the stack yourselfLow — agent/SDK install, vendor hosts everything
Cross-account / multi-cloud viewWeak outside AWSStrong, cloud-agnostic by designStrong, built for exactly this
Query/UX sophisticationImproving, but behind dedicated toolsVery capable, steep learning curveGenerally the most polished UX
Cost modelPay-as-you-go AWS billing, can surprise at scaleInfrastructure + engineering time, not per-GB vendor feesPer-host/per-GB licensing, often the priciest at scale
Operational burdenAWS-managedEntirely yours — another distributed system to keep aliveVendor-managed

ShopSphere stays on the native AWS stack for now — it's already deeply AWS-native, and the integration cost of a third-party tool isn't yet justified by a gap the native stack can't close.

07 When NOT to use it

Full distributed tracing isn't automatically the right first move for…

• A true single-service monolith — X-Ray's value is in showing cross-service hops; a monolith's "trace" is mostly just its own call stack, which a profiler or good logging already covers.
• 100% trace sampling in a high-traffic service — tracing every single request at scale gets expensive fast for marginal extra insight once a representative sample already reveals the pattern.
• Logging everything at DEBUG level in production — verbose, unstructured logging is the single most common source of an out-of-control CloudWatch Logs bill, for information nobody queries 99% of the time.

08 Trade-offs

Observability is not free instrumentation: every structured log field, every custom metric, every trace segment is written by application code someone had to add deliberately, and every one of them costs money to ingest and store. The trade-off is between blindness (cheap, until the first hard incident) and visibility (costs real money and real engineering discipline, but turns multi-hour incidents into multi-minute ones). Sampling is the usual compromise — trace, say, 5–10% of requests plus 100% of errors, rather than everything, trading a small chance of missing one specific slow request for a large reduction in cost and overhead.

09 Failure scenarios

FailureWhat happensMitigation
X-Ray sampling misses the one slow requestThe exact request a customer complained about was never traced — dead end100% sampling on error responses and on requests exceeding a latency threshold, not just a flat percentage
CloudWatch itself degrades (rare, but AWS-wide events happen)Metrics/alarms/logs delayed or unavailable exactly when they're needed mostA minimal independent health check (even a simple external ping) that doesn't depend on the same observability stack it's monitoring
Logs correlated by nothing sharedFour services each log the same failed order with no common ID — reconstructing the story means manually matching timestampsA correlation/trace ID generated at the edge (API Gateway) and propagated through every downstream call and log line
Unbounded log/metric cardinalityA custom metric dimension keyed by, say, customer ID explodes into millions of unique time series, both costly and slow to queryKeep high-cardinality fields in logs/traces (searchable), not as CloudWatch metric dimensions (priced and indexed per unique series)

10 Security

  • CloudTrail log integrity: log file validation and a dedicated, restricted-access S3 bucket (ideally in a separate security/log-archive account, per Module 04's Organizations structure) so even an attacker with admin access to the workload account can't quietly cover their tracks.
  • PII in logs is a real leak vector — a structured log line that includes a customer's email, address or masked card details is a data-protection liability the moment it's written; redact or avoid logging sensitive fields at the source, not after the fact.
  • Encryption at rest for CloudWatch Logs groups and X-Ray trace data via KMS, consistent with every other data-at-rest control ShopSphere applies elsewhere.
  • Least-privilege dashboard/log access — IAM scoped so a given team can query its own service's logs and traces without a blanket grant across every other domain's telemetry, which often contains more sensitive detail than the metrics layer.

11 Production configuration

DevelopmentProduction
Trace sampling100% — traffic is low, cost is irrelevantFixed low base rate + 100% of errors/slow requests
Log levelDEBUG, verboseINFO and above, structured JSON only
Log retentionShort (days)Tiered — hot/queryable for weeks, archived to S3/Glacier for compliance-driven years
AlarmsOptional, loose thresholdsTied to SLOs (§12), paging on sustained burn, not single-sample blips
Metric formatAd hocCloudWatch Embedded Metric Format, so one log line yields both a searchable log and a metric, without a second emission path

12 Monitoring: RED/USE and the SLI/SLO/error-budget discipline

Two complementary metric frameworks cover ShopSphere's two kinds of thing to watch. RED (Rate, Errors, Duration) fits request-driven services: for the checkout API, that's requests/sec, the error rate, and the latency distribution. USE (Utilization, Saturation, Errors) fits finite resources: for Aurora's connection pool, that's % of connections in use, how many requests are queued waiting for a free connection, and connection errors. A service can look healthy on RED (low error rate, fine median latency) while one of its USE resources is already saturated and about to cause a RED problem a few minutes later — which is exactly why both frameworks matter together.

TermPrecise meaningShopSphere checkout API example
SLI (Service Level Indicator)The thing you actually measure — a ratio of good events to total eventsThe fraction of checkout requests that complete in under 300ms
SLO (Service Level Objective)The internal target for that SLI over a window99.9% of checkout requests complete in under 300ms, measured over 28 rolling days
SLA (Service Level Agreement)An external, often contractual, commitment — usually looser than the internal SLO, with consequences for missing itShopSphere's public status page commits to 99.5% checkout availability; the internal SLO target is deliberately stricter
Error budgetThe allowed amount of SLO-missing before freezing risky changes0.1% of requests may legally violate the 300ms target per 28-day window; once that budget is spent, feature releases pause and the team works only on reliability until the budget recovers

Why an error budget, not just "be reliable"

An error budget turns reliability into a shared, numeric currency between product and engineering: it explicitly allows a non-zero amount of failure (100% is neither achievable nor economically sensible) while giving everyone a concrete trigger — "we've spent 80% of this month's budget" — to deliberately slow down risky deploys before an SLA breach, rather than discovering the problem only after customers already have.

13 Cost

The major cost drivers, in the order teams usually get surprised by them: log ingestion and storage volume (by far the largest line item once every microservice logs verbosely), custom metric count (each unique metric, and each unique dimension combination, is billed and stored separately — this is the unbounded-cardinality trap from §09), and trace storage/retrieval (usually smaller, but scales with sampling rate and trace size). Optimization strategies: sample traces instead of recording every one; keep structured logs lean (log what you'll actually query, not everything available); set log group retention explicitly (the default is indefinite, which quietly accumulates cost forever); and use metric filters/Embedded Metric Format to derive metrics from logs instead of double-emitting both.

The most common observability cost mistake

Leaving CloudWatch Logs retention at "Never expire" (the default) across every log group. A service generating a modest volume of DEBUG-level logs, left unbounded for two years, routinely becomes a larger line item than the compute it's monitoring — with almost none of that old data ever actually queried.

14 Hands-on lab

  1. Classify the signal. For each of these, say whether it's a metric, a log, a trace, or a CloudTrail event: (a) "Payment service error rate rose to 4%," (b) "IAM user jdoe modified the checkout security group at 02:14 UTC," (c) "Order 88212's request spent 310ms inside the Payment service's call to the external provider," (d) "order_failed, orderId=88212, reason=card_declined."
  2. Write the SLO. ShopSphere wants a defensible SLO for the checkout API's availability. Propose an SLI, an SLO target and window, and a monthly error budget in minutes, then state one concrete action that should happen automatically once 100% of that budget is spent.
  3. Find the bottleneck. Given a trace waterfall like §03's, but where Inventory's subsegment (not Payment's) is the longest, describe the first three things you'd check about the Inventory service before concluding anything about root cause.

15 Interview questions

Explain the difference between monitoring and observability without using the word "dashboard."
Monitoring checks whether specific, pre-identified things are within expected bounds — it can only answer questions someone thought to ask in advance. Observability is a property of how much information a system's telemetry carries about its own internal state, which determines whether an engineer can construct and answer a genuinely new question about a failure mode nobody anticipated. A system can have excellent monitoring and still be poorly observable if its signals don't carry enough detail to explain an unexpected interaction.
Why does CloudTrail not belong in the same bucket as metrics, logs and traces for diagnosing a slow checkout request?
CloudTrail records AWS API-level control-plane activity — who called which AWS API on which resource — not application request behavior. It answers security and compliance questions ("who changed this configuration") and is essentially useless for diagnosing why a specific HTTP request was slow, which is exactly what metrics, logs and traces are built to answer. Treating it as a fourth application-observability pillar rather than a distinct security control is a common and costly category error.
A team has a 99.9% SLO and reports "we're at 99.95% this month, we're fine." What's the architect-level follow-up question?
Over what window, and how is the error budget trending within it — a monthly average can hide a day where the budget was nearly exhausted by a single incident, followed by three uneventful weeks that mathematically pull the average back up. The right follow-up asks about burn rate (how fast the budget is being consumed right now) rather than the trailing average alone, since burn rate is what should actually trigger a change freeze before the SLO is breached, not a comfortable-looking monthly number.

Level 7 · Site Reliability Engineering

Incident Management & Postmortems

● ~31 min read● Builds on Module 29● ShopSphere: what actually happens at 2am

Bottom line: observability (Module 29) gives you the signal; incident management is the discipline that turns that signal into a fast, calm, coordinated response instead of five engineers independently guessing in a panic. This module is deliberately worked backwards from the usual tutorial order — each scenario below asks you to investigate before it gives you the answer, the way a real incident actually unfolds.

01 Severity classification

Not every incident deserves the same response. A severity scheme exists so the first responder doesn't have to decide how big a deal something is from scratch under pressure — they match it against a rubric and the response scales accordingly.

SeverityDefinitionShopSphere exampleResponse
SEV-1Complete outage of a revenue-critical path, or a security/data-integrity breach, affecting all or most customersCheckout is fully down — no customer anywhere can complete an orderImmediate page to on-call + incident commander; all hands available; status page updated within minutes
SEV-2Significant degradation of a major feature, or a critical path at reduced capacity, affecting many customersCheckout works but p95 latency is 5× normal, or payment succeeds only intermittentlyPage to on-call; incident channel opened; status page updated if customer-visible
SEV-3A non-critical feature degraded or broken, workaround exists, limited customer impactThe product recommendations widget is slow or returning empty resultsTicket filed, fixed in normal working hours; no page, no status page update
SEV-4Isolated, single-customer or cosmetic issueOne customer reports a UI glitch or a single order showing a wrong statusStandard support ticket workflow; no incident process invoked at all

The classification mistake that costs the most

Under-classifying a SEV-1 as a SEV-2 because "it's probably fine" delays the page, the incident commander, and the status page update — exactly the resources a true SEV-1 needs fastest. When genuinely unsure between two adjacent severities, the correct default is to classify at the higher severity and downgrade once the blast radius is confirmed, not the reverse.

02 Incident communication

During an active incident, silence is worse than an unresolved problem — stakeholders without updates assume the worst and start asking the same question in five different channels, which pulls responders away from actually fixing it. A fixed cadence prevents that.

AudienceCadence (SEV-1/2)What to say
Incident channel (responders)Continuous, as things happenRaw working notes — what's been tried, what's being tried next
Internal stakeholders (leadership, support)Every 15–30 minutes, or on any status changePlain-language: what's broken, who's affected, what's being done, next update time — never raw technical detail or speculation about cause
Customers (status page)On open, on any material change, on resolutionScope of impact and current status; avoid premature root-cause claims that might be wrong

The single rule that matters most

Every update states what's known, what's being done, and when the next update will arrive — even if that next update is "still investigating, same plan, next update in 20 minutes." A promised cadence that's kept, even with no new news, keeps stakeholders from escalating out of anxiety alone.

03 The incident lifecycle

04 Investigate before you know: six ShopSphere incidents

Each of these is phrased the way a real page actually arrives — a symptom, not a diagnosis. Think through what you'd check, in what order, before opening the answer.

Incident 1 — Traffic increases 20× with no warning. What do you investigate first?
Investigate: (1) Is this real customer traffic, or a bot/scraper/retry storm? Check the traffic's shape — request distribution across endpoints, user-agent patterns, and whether it correlates with a marketing push or a flash-sale start time ShopSphere already knew about. (2) Which tier is actually saturating — check RED metrics (Module 29) per service: is the ALB/API Gateway absorbing it fine while a specific downstream service (often the database) is the one showing USE saturation? (3) Is Auto Scaling actually responding, or stuck — check ASG activity history for a scaling-limit ceiling or a slow-to-react scaling policy.

Root cause category: usually either a genuine demand spike outrunning Auto Scaling's reaction time, or a traffic-shape problem (retry storm, bot traffic) masquerading as organic load. Fix: for genuine load, pre-warm capacity ahead of known events and tighten scaling policy aggressiveness; for a retry storm, add client-side backoff/rate limiting at the edge (WAF rate-based rules) rather than scaling the backend to absorb traffic that shouldn't exist.
Incident 2 — Aurora CPU is pinned at 95%. What do you investigate first?
Investigate: (1) Is this a query problem or a volume problem — check Performance Insights / slow query logs for one or two queries dominating CPU (a missing index, a sudden full-table scan) versus a broad, even increase across all queries. (2) Did anything deploy recently — a new query pattern, a removed index, an ORM change — correlate the CPU graph's inflection point against the deployment timeline from Module 27's pipeline. (3) Is this read or write load, and would a read replica or connection-pool fix actually help, or is this a single hot partition/row (lock contention) that more compute can't fix at all.

Root cause category: most often one specific expensive query (frequently a new one, or an old one now running against a much bigger table), occasionally genuine organic growth outrunning the instance class. Fix: add the missing index or fix the query first (near-immediate relief); scale the instance class or add a read replica only as a parallel, slower mitigation; never scale compute as the only response to a single bad query, since the next bad query just repeats the incident at a higher cost baseline.
Incident 3 — ElastiCache Redis becomes completely unavailable. What do you investigate first?
Investigate: (1) What's actually broken behind it right now — is the application falling back to the database for every cache miss (the expected, designed behavior), or is it erroring out entirely because the code assumes the cache is always there? This determines whether this is a performance incident or an availability incident. (2) Check whether the circuit breaker around the cache call (Module 21) is open and failing fast, or whether every request is hanging on a connection timeout to a dead cache node, which is far worse than no cache at all. (3) Check the node/cluster status — single-node failure with no replica, a full cluster failover event, or a network-level AZ issue reaching the cluster.

Root cause category: a node failure the application wasn't built to tolerate gracefully. Fix: short-term, the circuit breaker should already be shedding load to the database with a short timeout rather than queueing behind a dead connection; medium-term, Multi-AZ replication with automatic failover (Module 08) for the cache cluster itself, and a load-tested confirmation that the database can actually absorb 100% of read traffic, unachieved, for the duration of a cache outage.
Incident 4 — One entire Availability Zone goes down. What do you investigate first?
Investigate: (1) Confirm it's actually an AZ-level event and not a single bad deploy that happens to be concentrated in one AZ — check AWS Health Dashboard/Service Health Dashboard alongside your own metrics, since the response differs completely. (2) Check whether the ALB and Auto Scaling Group are already routing around the failed AZ automatically (the expected Multi-AZ behavior from Module 08) or whether capacity in the surviving AZs is insufficient to absorb the shifted load, which turns a redundancy event into a capacity event. (3) Check the database — did Multi-AZ RDS/Aurora failover to a standby in a healthy AZ automatically, and how long did that failover actually take versus the documented expectation.

Root cause category: this is the scenario Multi-AZ architecture exists specifically to make into a non-event. If it's not a non-event, the real root cause is usually under-provisioned surviving-AZ capacity (running at N+0 instead of N+1 per AZ) or a dependency that was accidentally single-AZ despite the rest of the architecture being Multi-AZ. Fix: immediate: confirm failover completed and surviving AZs have headroom, add capacity manually if Auto Scaling hasn't caught up. Follow-up: audit every component in the request path for a hidden single-AZ dependency.
Incident 5 — The external payment provider becomes unavailable mid-checkout. What do you investigate first?
Investigate: (1) Is the payment provider actually down, or is ShopSphere's Payment service timing out against a provider that's merely slow — check the provider's own status page and compare against ShopSphere's own timeout configuration, since these call for different responses. (2) Check whether the circuit breaker around the payment-provider call (Module 21) has tripped open — if it hasn't, every checkout attempt may be hanging for the full timeout duration, multiplying the damage far beyond the provider's actual outage. (3) Check the state of in-flight orders at the moment of failure — did inventory get reserved (the saga's first step) for orders that never completed payment, and is the Saga's compensating transaction (Module 23) actually releasing those holds.

Root cause category: a third-party dependency failure outside ShopSphere's control, but ShopSphere's blast radius from it is entirely within its control. Fix: circuit breaker fails fast and shows customers a clear "payment temporarily unavailable, your cart is saved" message rather than a hang; the Saga's compensating transaction releases every reserved inventory hold tied to a failed payment step, automatically, so failed checkouts don't silently lock stock away from customers who could actually complete their purchase.
Incident 6 — Duplicate orders are being created. What do you investigate first?
Investigate: (1) Is this client-side (a customer double-clicking "place order," or a retried request after a slow response the client assumed had failed) or server-side (a message being processed twice downstream, or a retry in the Saga orchestration creating a second Order record)? Pull the trace (Module 29) for one duplicate pair and check whether they share a client request ID or arrived as two genuinely separate requests. (2) Check whether the Order-creation endpoint is idempotent — does it accept and dedupe an idempotency key, or does every POST unconditionally create a new row? (3) If it's message-driven, check whether the consumer acknowledges a message before or after completing order creation — an at-least-once queue (Module 14) combined with a non-idempotent consumer is the classic combination that produces exactly this symptom.

Root cause category: almost always a missing idempotency guarantee somewhere in a path that assumed "exactly once" delivery, which neither HTTP retries nor standard queues actually provide. Fix: require an idempotency key on order creation (generated client-side, checked server-side before creating anything new) and make the downstream consumer's processing idempotent (check-then-act replaced with an atomic "insert if not already processed," using the Outbox pattern's dedupe table from Module 23) — not a retry-prevention fix on the client alone, which can't cover every duplication path.

05 The blameless postmortem

A postmortem's job is to make the system more resilient to the next failure, not to identify who to discipline for this one. The moment a postmortem starts asking "whose fault was this," people stop giving full, honest accounts of what they saw and did — which is precisely the information needed to actually fix the system. "Blameless" doesn't mean "no accountability" — it means the accountability is to fixing the gap, owned by named people with deadlines, not to assigning blame for having hit it.

SectionContent
What happenedA plain-language summary, readable by someone outside the team
ImpactDuration, customers/orders affected, revenue impact if known — stated in numbers, not adjectives
TimelineTimestamped sequence: detection, each action taken, each status update sent, resolution
Root causeThe underlying system/process gap — not a person's name, even if a specific action triggered it
What went wellWhat worked during the response — explicitly included, so good practices get reinforced, not just failures examined
What to improveGaps in detection, response, or the system itself, stated honestly
Action itemsSpecific, owned by a named person, with a due date — a postmortem with no action items changed nothing

What a blameless postmortem explicitly is not

It is not "who ran the command that caused this," "who approved that deploy," or "who should have caught this in review." Those questions reliably produce the same long-term outcome: people stop reporting near-misses and start hiding mistakes, which removes exactly the early-warning signal a healthy incident culture depends on. The question is always "what about our system, process, or tooling let one person's honest mistake turn into a customer-facing incident" — because it will be a different honest mistake, from a different person, next time, unless the system itself changes.

06 Hands-on lab

  1. Classify three incidents. Assign a severity (SEV-1 through SEV-4) to: (a) the wishlist feature returns a 500 error for 2% of requests, (b) checkout succeeds but sends zero order-confirmation emails, (c) one enterprise customer's account shows someone else's order history. Justify each.
  2. Write the first three status updates. For a SEV-1 "checkout fully down" incident starting now, draft the internal-stakeholder update at T+0, T+20min (still unresolved, cause suspected but unconfirmed), and T+45min (resolved). Keep each to 2–3 sentences.
  3. Turn a scenario into action items. Using Incident 6 (duplicate orders) from §04, write three postmortem action items in the "specific, owned, dated" format §05 requires — not "investigate idempotency," but a concrete, assignable task.

07 Interview questions

Why does a sound severity-classification process default to the higher severity when a responder is unsure?
The cost of over-classifying (an incident commander spun up for something that turns out to be minor, stood down within minutes) is small and quickly reversible. The cost of under-classifying (a true SEV-1 treated as routine, the page delayed, the status page never updated while customers are actively affected) compounds for as long as the misclassification persists. Given asymmetric costs, defaulting high and downgrading once impact is confirmed is the only decision rule that doesn't systematically under-react to the worst cases.
A manager asks the postmortem to name which engineer deployed the change that caused the outage. How do you respond?
The deploy that triggered the incident is a fact worth recording in the timeline, but the postmortem's root cause should describe the system gap that let that deploy cause customer-facing impact — missing test coverage, no canary/gradual rollout, no automated rollback trigger, insufficient review process for that class of change — rather than naming the person who clicked deploy. Naming the engineer accomplishes nothing for reliability and actively damages the team's willingness to be forthcoming in the next incident.
What's the difference between an incident response's communication cadence and its actual technical mitigation work, and why do both matter?
Mitigation work is what actually stops customer impact; communication cadence is what keeps stakeholders calm, informed and out of the responders' way while that work happens. Skipping communication doesn't speed up mitigation — it just means stakeholders start independently pinging responders for status, which directly slows mitigation down. A predictable cadence, even with "no change" updates, is itself a mitigation for the secondary problem an incident always creates: organizational anxiety.

Level 8 · Data Architecture

Data Architecture: Lakes, Warehouses, Streaming

● ~24 min read● Builds on Module 30● ShopSphere: separates "run the business" from "understand the business"

Bottom line: ShopSphere's operational databases exist to serve one checkout at a time, fast and correctly. The moment someone runs a "total revenue by category, last 90 days" query against Aurora, those two goals collide — and the checkout path loses. Every production data architecture draws a hard line between operational data that keeps the business running and analytical data that helps the business understand itself, and moves data across that line asynchronously, never synchronously.

01 Five kinds of data, five different jobs

"ShopSphere's database" is already the wrong mental model — by this point in the academy, ShopSphere has at least five categories of data, each shaped by what it has to be good at, not by habit.

CategoryJobShopSphere exampleStore
TransactionalStay small, fast, and consistent — one order at a timeOrder, Payment, Inventory writesAurora (Module 08/16)
OperationalServe read-heavy, less latency-critical lookupsCatalog, Customer profilesAurora / DynamoDB
SearchRank and filter free-text and faceted queriesProduct search, relevanceOpenSearch (Module 13)
CacheServe the hottest reads in single-digit msSession, cart, price lookupsElastiCache Redis (Module 17)
AnalyticalAnswer "how is the business doing," across history, at scale — slowly is fineRevenue trends, cohort analysis, leadership dashboardsS3 data lake + Redshift (this module)

The first four exist to answer one customer's question right now. The fifth exists to answer leadership's question about every customer, ever — a fundamentally different workload: large scans instead of small lookups, joins across years of history instead of a single row by primary key, and a query that's acceptable to take 30 seconds where a checkout query taking 30 seconds would be an outage.

02 The mistake: querying Aurora for analytics

The classic mistake ShopSphere must not make

It's tempting — the data is "right there" — to point a BI tool straight at the Order service's Aurora read replica and let analysts run whatever SQL they want. This works fine in a demo and fails in production: a single poorly-written analytical join, or a dashboard that refreshes every 60 seconds across a year of order history, consumes CPU and I/O that Aurora's connection pool and buffer cache were sized for checkout traffic, not warehouse-style scans. The result is checkout latency degrading for every customer because someone in finance refreshed a spreadsheet — two completely unrelated concerns, coupled through one database instance.

Even a dedicated read replica only buys time: replica lag under analytical load can grow large enough that "real-time" dashboards are reading stale data anyway, and the replica is still billed and sized against the same instance-family trade-offs as the primary. The fix isn't a bigger replica. It's a different store, fed asynchronously.

03 The flow: operational events → streaming → lake → warehouse → BI

Order and Payment don't write to the data lake directly — they emit domain events (OrderCreated, PaymentCaptured) the same way they do for the Module 18/22 Saga choreography, onto a stream. A separate consumer lands those events, unmodified, into the data lake's raw zone in S3 — an exact, append-only record of what happened, kept for reprocessing and audit. A scheduled or streaming ETL job (AWS Glue, or a Lambda/Spark job) cleans, deduplicates and reshapes raw events into the curated zone — structured, query-ready tables, typically in a columnar format like Parquet. Redshift then loads from the curated zone (or queries it directly via Redshift Spectrum) and serves the BI tools and dashboards leadership actually looks at.

04 Kinesis vs MSK — which streaming layer

Kinesis Data StreamsAmazon MSK (Managed Kafka)
Operational modelFully AWS-native, provisioned or on-demand capacityManaged Apache Kafka — you still reason in Kafka's operational model (brokers, partitions, KRaft)
EcosystemAWS SDK, Kinesis Client Library, Firehose for direct-to-S3 deliveryFull Kafka ecosystem — Kafka Connect, Kafka Streams, ksqlDB, existing Kafka tooling
Best fitAWS-native event flows with no prior Kafka investment — the simpler defaultMigrating an existing Kafka-based system, or a hard requirement for a specific Kafka-ecosystem feature
Operational overheadLower — fewer concepts to operate day to dayHigher — broker sizing, partition rebalancing, more Kafka-specific expertise needed

ShopSphere's decision

ShopSphere has no existing Kafka footprint and no requirement for a Kafka-specific feature, so it streams OrderCreated/PaymentCaptured/InventoryAdjusted events through Kinesis Data Streams — simpler to operate, and it composes cleanly with Kinesis Data Firehose for raw-zone delivery into S3 with no custom consumer code at all. MSK stays the right answer only if ShopSphere acquires a team or a system that's already standardized on Kafka.

05 Data lake zones — why raw and curated are separate

A data lake isn't one S3 bucket of files; it's at least two zones with different contracts. The raw zone stores events exactly as received — no joins, no cleanup, no schema enforcement beyond "is this valid JSON/Avro" — so a bug in the curation job can always be fixed by reprocessing from an unmodified source. The curated zone stores the cleaned, deduplicated, business-shaped version — partitioned (commonly by date) and stored in a columnar format, because that's what makes a Redshift COPY or a Spectrum scan over a year of orders fast instead of a full-file linear read.

When NOT to skip the raw zone

Writing curated tables directly from the stream, skipping a raw zone to "save a step," is a common shortcut that removes the one thing that makes the pipeline trustworthy: an unmodified source of truth to replay from when the curation logic itself turns out to have a bug — which, over a multi-year data platform, it will.

06 Redshift's job, and where it stops

Redshift is a columnar, massively-parallel-processing data warehouse built for exactly the query pattern leadership dashboards need: aggregate a large fact table (orders, order lines) across history, sliced by dimensions (region, category, time). It is not a transactional database — no ShopSphere service ever writes a live order to Redshift directly, and nothing on the checkout path ever waits on a Redshift query. Data arrives in Redshift minutes to hours after the event happened, which is the correct trade: BI dashboards don't need millisecond freshness, and accepting that latency is exactly what buys Redshift the freedom to run expensive, wide queries without threatening anything operational.

07 Hands-on lab

  1. A product manager asks for a live dashboard showing "orders in the last 5 minutes" refreshed every 10 seconds. Decide whether this belongs on the operational path or the analytical path, and justify your answer using this module's latency/freshness trade-off.
  2. Sketch the S3 key structure (prefixes) you'd use to partition ShopSphere's curated orders table by year/month/day, and explain in one sentence why that partitioning scheme matters for Redshift Spectrum query cost.
  3. ShopSphere's analytics team wants to join order data with a third-party marketing dataset that's only ever needed for analytics, never for checkout. Name which zone of the data lake it should land in, and why it should never be loaded into Aurora even temporarily.

08 Interview questions

Why can't a read replica of the operational database simply serve as the analytics database?
A read replica inherits the operational database's schema and instance sizing, both tuned for small, indexed lookups — not for wide scans and heavy joins across years of history. Analytical queries on a replica still consume shared I/O and replication bandwidth, can push replica lag high enough to make the data unreliable anyway, and couple an unrelated concern (BI) to the operational database's capacity planning. A columnar warehouse fed asynchronously removes both the resource contention and the coupling.
What specifically does separating a raw zone from a curated zone buy you that a single "clean" table doesn't?
Replayability. If the curation logic has a bug — a bad deduplication rule, a schema misread, a timezone error — an unmodified raw zone lets you fix the logic and reprocess history to get a correct curated table. Without a raw zone, a curation bug corrupts the only copy of the data you have, and there's nothing to replay from.
When would you reach for MSK over Kinesis for ShopSphere's event stream, given Kinesis is the simpler default?
When ShopSphere already has a Kafka-based system — acquired, or built by a team that standardized on it — migrating is usually not worth the cost versus just running it on managed Kafka (MSK). The other case is a hard dependency on a specific piece of the Kafka ecosystem (ksqlDB, Kafka Streams, a Kafka Connect connector with no Kinesis equivalent) that the workload genuinely needs. Absent either reason, Kinesis's lower operational overhead wins by default.

Level 8 · Data Architecture

AI/ML Integration: Bedrock, SageMaker, OpenSearch

● ~26 min read● Builds on Module 31● ShopSphere: four AI features, zero new failure domains

Bottom line: none of ShopSphere's four AI features are a separate system bolted on the side — each one plugs into a request path that already exists, and each one has a defined, boring fallback for when it's slow, wrong, or simply down. An AI component that can take checkout offline when it times out is a design defect, not an acceptable cost of using AI.

01 The rule before the services: AI augments, never gates, the critical path

Every feature below sits beside an existing ShopSphere request — search, checkout, support — not in front of it. The architectural question for each one is the same: if this AI call is slow or returns an error, what does the customer see? The acceptable answer is always "a slightly worse experience," never "an error page" or "a blocked checkout." That constraint shapes every integration decision in this module more than the choice of model does.

02 Product recommendations

ShopSphere's recommendation model trains on the clickstream and purchase history already landing in the Module 31 data lake's curated zone — views, cart-adds, purchases, joined by customer and product. Training runs offline, on a schedule, using Amazon SageMaker AI (the model-building component of AWS's unified SageMaker data/AI platform) to train and version a collaborative-filtering or embedding-based model; the trained model is deployed behind a SageMaker AI real-time inference endpoint that the Product/Recommendation service calls at request time — "customers who viewed this also viewed…" on a product page, and a personalized rail on the homepage.

Where it plugs in

The Product service calls the Recommendation service synchronously when rendering a product page, with a short timeout (tens of milliseconds). It never calls SageMaker AI directly from the customer-facing request — the Recommendation service sits in between, holding the inference client, the timeout, and the fallback logic.

Failure mode: if the inference endpoint is slow or returns an error, the Recommendation service times out fast and returns a non-personalized fallback — a simple "best sellers in this category" list, computed cheaply and cached. The product page always renders a recommendations rail; it just isn't personalized during the degraded window.

03 Search relevance

Module 13 put ShopSphere's product search on OpenSearch for keyword/faceted matching. The modern enhancement is vector (semantic) search: products and queries are embedded into vectors, and OpenSearch's k-NN/vector search capability finds products that are semantically close to a query even when no keyword matches — "warm winter jacket" surfacing a product titled "insulated parka" is the canonical example a pure keyword index misses.

Keyword search (Module 13 baseline)+ Semantic/vector search
Matches onExact/fuzzy term overlap, field weightingMeaning — embeddings nearby in vector space
Catches"blue running shoes""shoes for jogging in the rain" → still finds running shoes
Weak spot aloneMisses synonyms/intent it wasn't told aboutCan surface plausible-but-wrong matches without keyword grounding
Production patternHybrid: run both, blend/re-rank the combined result set — neither replaces the other

Failure mode: the vector index or embedding call is just one signal in a hybrid query. If it's unavailable, OpenSearch's keyword scoring alone still returns a result set — search degrades to Module 13's baseline relevance, it doesn't go down.

04 Fraud detection on the payment path

The Order+Payment Saga (Module 23) already has a well-defined step sequence. Fraud detection adds one more step: before the Payment service calls the external payment provider to capture a charge, it calls a fraud-scoring model — trained on historical transaction, device and behavioral features — and gets back a risk score in near-real time.

The non-negotiable rule: checkout does not block on the fraud model

The fraud-scoring call carries an aggressive timeout, well under the Saga step's own budget. If the model times out or errors, the Payment service does not wait, and it does not fail the order. It falls back to a conservative default — typically treating the transaction as if it scored in the "review" band rather than "auto-approve": the charge still proceeds, but the order is flagged for asynchronous manual or secondary-rule review rather than blocked at checkout. A fraud model that can take checkout down when it's slow has a worse failure mode than the fraud it's trying to catch.

High-confidence fraud (score above a hard threshold) still blocks the charge — the fallback is specifically for the model being unavailable, not for it confidently flagging a bad transaction.

05 Customer-support chatbot

Amazon Bedrock gives ShopSphere managed, API-based access to foundation models (Anthropic Claude, Amazon Nova and others) without operating any model infrastructure. A plain foundation model, though, only knows what it was trained on — it has no idea what ShopSphere's return policy says or what's actually in a specific customer's order history. Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), via Knowledge Bases for Amazon Bedrock, closes that gap: at query time, the customer's question is used to retrieve the most relevant chunks of ShopSphere's own order data and policy documents (indexed as vectors, often in OpenSearch), and those chunks are inserted into the prompt alongside the question before the model answers — so "where's my order #48213" gets grounded in that order's real shipment status, not a guess.

Failure mode: if Bedrock or the retrieval step is unavailable, or the model's confidence/groundedness check fails, the chatbot says so plainly and hands off to a human support queue (Module 02's customer-support capability) rather than inventing an answer. A support chatbot that confidently fabricates an order status is a worse outcome than one that says "let me connect you with someone."

06 All four, on one map

FeatureAWS servicePlugs intoFallback on failure/timeout
RecommendationsSageMaker AI (inference endpoint)Product service, product-page renderNon-personalized "best sellers" list
Search relevanceOpenSearch vector/k-NN searchSearch service, alongside keyword queryKeyword-only results (Module 13 baseline)
Fraud detectionCustom model via SageMaker AI endpointPayment service, before charge captureConservative default — flag for async review, don't block the charge
Support chatbotAmazon Bedrock + Knowledge Bases (RAG)Customer-support serviceHand off to human support queue

07 Hands-on lab

  1. Design the timeout budget for the fraud-scoring call inside the Payment Saga step: given the Saga step's own budget, propose a specific timeout for the fraud call and justify why it must be meaningfully shorter.
  2. Write the retrieval query, in plain English, that a support chatbot should run before answering "why was my order #48213 delayed" — name which ShopSphere data sources (Module 31's data lake, or live operational data) it needs to retrieve from, and why those two sources have different freshness requirements.
  3. A teammate proposes blocking checkout entirely whenever the fraud model is unreachable, "to be safe." Argue against this using this module's framework, and propose the correct fallback instead.

08 Interview questions

Why must the fraud-detection call never block checkout, even though fraud is a serious risk?
Blocking checkout on an unreliable dependency converts a model-availability problem into a revenue and customer-experience problem for every legitimate transaction, not just the fraudulent ones. The correct design treats model unavailability and a confident fraud signal as two different cases: a confident high-risk score still blocks the charge, but a timeout or error falls back to a conservative default (flag for async review) so one dependency's hiccup doesn't take down checkout for everyone.
What does retrieval-augmented generation actually add that a plain foundation model call doesn't have?
A plain foundation model only knows what was in its training data, which doesn't include a specific customer's order history or ShopSphere's current return policy. RAG retrieves the relevant, current, ShopSphere-specific data at query time and inserts it into the prompt, so the model's answer is grounded in real data instead of a plausible-sounding guess.
Search relevance now uses both keyword and vector search. Why not replace keyword search with vector search entirely?
Vector/semantic search is strong at matching intent and meaning but can surface plausible-but-wrong results when there's no keyword grounding, and it depends on an embedding pipeline that keyword search doesn't need. Running both and blending/re-ranking the results gives the precision of keyword matching and the recall of semantic matching, and if the vector component fails, keyword search alone still returns a usable result set — a resilience property a vector-only design would lose.

Level 9 · Multi-Region & Disaster Recovery

Multi-AZ Architecture Patterns

● ~24 min read● Builds on Module 32● ShopSphere: the full stack, three times over

Bottom line: Module 2 introduced Availability Zones as a theory — physically separate data centers, close enough for synchronous replication, far enough apart to fail independently. This module makes that literal: every layer of ShopSphere's stack, spread across AZ-A, AZ-B and AZ-C in ap-south-1, and exactly what happens, component by component, when one of those AZs disappears.

01 ShopSphere's stack, times three

LayerHow it's spread across AZ-A/B/C
Application Load BalancerA single ALB, but its nodes run in every AZ you register a subnet for — AWS-managed, not something ShopSphere provisions per AZ itself
Application tier (containers)ECS/EKS tasks scheduled across all three AZs by the Module 20 orchestrator; Auto Scaling replaces lost capacity
Aurora (Module 08)Multi-AZ: one primary, at least one standby replica in a different AZ, synchronous replication between them
ElastiCache (Module 14)Cluster mode with replica nodes spread across AZs, so losing one AZ loses a replica, not the whole cache
NAT GatewayOne per AZ — the detail Section 02 exists to explain

02 The NAT Gateway trap

A NAT Gateway is itself an AZ-scoped resource — and a single one is a hidden single point of failure

It's easy to provision one NAT Gateway for the whole VPC, in one public subnet, and route every private subnet's outbound traffic through it — it works perfectly in testing. What that setup actually built is a piece of networking that sounds regional but lives entirely in one Availability Zone. If that AZ fails, every private-subnet instance in the other AZs loses outbound internet access too (package updates, calls to external APIs, the payment provider) even though those AZs are otherwise completely healthy — a single-AZ failure with a region-wide blast radius, purely because of how NAT was wired. The fix teams often miss: one NAT Gateway per AZ, with each AZ's private subnets routed only through their own AZ's NAT Gateway.

AWS has since introduced Regional NAT Gateways, a newer deployment mode that automatically follows your workloads across AZs and provides this high availability by default, without hand-provisioning one gateway per AZ or managing a public subnet in each one. The architectural lesson underneath either implementation is the same and is worth internalizing regardless of which mode a given VPC uses: never let one AZ's outbound path become every other AZ's dependency.

03 What happens when AZ-A fails completely

Every application task, and anything else running only in AZ-A, is gone at once — not slow, not degraded, simply unreachable. The ALB health checks mark AZ-A's targets unhealthy within its configured interval and routes 100% of new traffic to the healthy targets in AZ-B and AZ-C. Requests already in flight to an AZ-A target at the moment of failure are dropped; a well-built client (or the retry behavior of upstream callers) retries them, and because ShopSphere's application tier is stateless (Module 1's elasticity principle, carried all the way through), a retry to B or C succeeds with no session stickiness to lose. If Aurora's primary happened to be in AZ-A, Section 04 is what happens next.

04 What happens when the Aurora primary fails

Aurora detects the primary's failure and promotes the standby in another AZ to primary automatically — typically in under a minute, because the standby was already receiving synchronous replication and doesn't need to catch up before taking over. The database's endpoint DNS record is updated to point at the new primary, so applications reconnect without a config change.

The failover window still has to be handled correctly

"Under a minute" is not zero. Connection pools holding stale connections to the now-dead primary, and application code that doesn't retry a failed query with fresh connection resolution, turn a clean, fast database failover into a visible outage anyway — the database recovered; the application just never noticed. Production configuration (Module 11's development-vs-production table, applied here) means validating connection pool behavior and retry logic against an actual forced failover test, not assuming the driver "just handles it."

05 What happens when an ALB node fails

Nothing ShopSphere's team has to do anything about. The Application Load Balancer is a managed, multi-AZ service by design — AWS runs ALB nodes across every AZ it's configured for and replaces a failed node transparently, with no customer-visible action and nothing to configure for this specific failure mode. This is the clearest illustration in this module of Module 1's managed-services trade-off paying off: the layer you'd most want to not have to think about during an incident is exactly the layer AWS fully owns.

06 What happens when a NAT Gateway fails

With one NAT Gateway per AZ (Section 02's correct pattern), a NAT Gateway failure in AZ-A only removes outbound internet access for private-subnet resources in AZ-A — inbound traffic through the ALB, and AZ-B/C's outbound paths, are unaffected. With the single-NAT-Gateway anti-pattern, the same failure removes outbound access for the entire region's private subnets at once — the exact scenario Section 02 warns about, and the reason this failure mode gets its own section instead of being folded into "AZ-A fails."

07 Failure scenarios, side by side

Component failsBlast radius with correct Multi-AZ designBlast radius with the common mistake
One AZ, entirelyThat AZ's capacity; traffic shifts to the other twoSame — this is the scenario Multi-AZ is built for
Aurora primarySub-minute failover to standby; brief reconnect windowExtended outage if connection pooling/retry logic isn't failover-aware
One ALB nodeNone — AWS replaces it transparentlyN/A — not a design choice you make
One NAT GatewayOutbound access lost in that AZ onlyOutbound access lost region-wide (single shared NAT Gateway)

08 Hands-on lab

  1. Draw ShopSphere's VPC subnet layout across 3 AZs showing exactly one NAT Gateway per AZ, and label which private subnet's route table points at which NAT Gateway.
  2. A teammate's cost-cutting proposal is "we only need one NAT Gateway, the other two AZs can share it to save money." Write the one-paragraph architecture review comment explaining why this reintroduces a cross-AZ single point of failure.
  3. Design a test plan for validating that ShopSphere's application correctly survives an Aurora Multi-AZ failover, including what you'd force-trigger and what metric proves the application recovered within its latency budget.

09 Interview questions

Why is "we deployed across 3 AZs" not sufficient on its own to claim resilience to an AZ failure?
Every layer has to actually be replicated across those AZs independently — the application tier being multi-AZ doesn't help if the database, the cache, or the NAT Gateway is still a single-AZ resource underneath it. A single shared NAT Gateway is the single most common way teams believe they're multi-AZ while actually having a hidden single-AZ dependency that the rest of the stack doesn't protect against.
Aurora fails over in under a minute, but a team reports a 10-minute outage during a database failure. What's the most likely cause?
The database likely failed over correctly and quickly; the application almost certainly didn't. Stale connections in a connection pool that aren't retried or re-resolved against the new primary's DNS record, or application code without failover-aware retry logic, routinely turn a sub-minute database event into a much longer application-visible outage. The fix is in the application's connection handling, not the database.
Why does ALB node failure get handled completely differently from NAT Gateway failure in this module, when both are "networking infrastructure"?
ALB is a fully AWS-managed, inherently multi-AZ service — the customer has no node-level configuration to get wrong, so there's no failure mode to design around beyond registering subnets in multiple AZs. A NAT Gateway, by contrast, is explicitly provisioned per-AZ by the customer in the classic zonal model, so its resilience is a design decision the customer can get wrong — and a hidden single point of failure if they do.

Level 9 · Multi-Region & Disaster Recovery

Disaster Recovery: RTO, RPO & Strategies

● ~26 min read● Builds on Module 33● ShopSphere: four DR tiers, four different bills

Bottom line: disaster recovery isn't one decision, it's a dial. Module 2 named RTO and RPO as the two numbers that define a DR strategy; this module puts a real architecture and a real cost curve under four points on that dial, and makes the point most teams miss — the cost curve isn't linear, and not every ShopSphere component needs the same tier.

01 RTO and RPO, precisely

TermAnswersShopSphere framing
RTO — Recovery Time ObjectiveHow long can we be down?"Checkout must be back within <X> of ap-south-1 becoming unusable"
RPO — Recovery Point ObjectiveHow much data can we afford to lose, measured in time?"We can afford to lose up to <X> of orders/payments written just before the failure"

These are business decisions dressed up as engineering numbers — a tighter RTO/RPO is always available, at a cost, and the right answer is whatever the business is actually willing to pay for, not the smallest number engineering can imagine wanting.

02 Four strategies, mapped to four RTO targets

RTO targetStrategyWhat's running in ap-southeast-1, continuouslyRelative monthly cost
24 hoursBackup & RestoreNothing — periodic snapshots copied to S3/the DR regionLowest
1 hourPilot LightMinimal core — e.g. just the database, kept in sync, scaled up on failoverLow-moderate
15 minutesWarm StandbyA scaled-down but fully functional copy of ShopSphere, continuously runningModerate-high
Near-zeroActive/ActiveFull production capacity, serving live traffic right nowHighest — roughly double, not a modest increment

03 Backup & Restore — RTO = 24 hours

ShopSphere's databases snapshot on a schedule; snapshots and S3 data (product images, the data lake's raw zone) replicate to ap-southeast-1. Nothing runs there day to day. A real regional failure means standing up infrastructure from IaC templates (Module 25), restoring the latest snapshots, and repointing DNS — realistically measured in hours, which is exactly what this tier is for.

What this tier is honest about

It's the cheapest option and the slowest one — appropriate only for components where a day of downtime, and losing up to a backup interval's worth of data, is a tolerable business outcome. It is not an acceptable tier for ShopSphere's Order/Payment path.

04 Pilot Light — RTO = 1 hour

The minimal core — almost always the database, since a database takes the longest to bring up from cold — runs continuously in ap-southeast-1, kept current via ongoing replication (Module 35 covers exactly how). Everything else (app tier, load balancers) exists as pre-built templates/AMIs but is not running, and gets launched and scaled up only during an actual failover. The "pilot light" metaphor is literal: a small flame kept lit, ready to ignite the rest on demand.

05 Warm Standby — RTO = 15 minutes

A real, functioning copy of ShopSphere runs in ap-southeast-1 continuously — smaller instance counts/sizes than production, but able to serve real traffic right now, not just able to be launched. Failover means scaling that existing capacity up and redirecting traffic (Module 35's Route 53 failover routing), which is far faster than Pilot Light's "launch everything from scratch." This is ShopSphere's current, deliberate choice for its overall DR posture — detailed as a concrete architecture in Module 35.

06 Active/Active — RTO near-zero

Full production capacity runs in both regions simultaneously, with Route 53 actively routing customers to both. There's no "failover" step in the traditional sense — the other region is already serving production traffic, so losing one region just means the other absorbs the load it's not already handling. The genuinely hard part isn't the compute (just run two of everything) — it's keeping both regions' data consistent when both can accept writes, which reopens Module 18's CAP/consistency trade-off at a new scale, discussed concretely in Module 35.

Why most companies, including ShopSphere, don't do this by default

Active/Active roughly doubles infrastructure cost — not a 20–30% increment, a genuine doubling, since every component needs full production capacity in both regions at once — and adds the hardest unsolved problem in distributed systems (multi-region write conflict resolution) as an operational reality rather than a textbook exercise. The business case has to clearly justify both before this tier is the right call; "better DR" alone rarely does.

07 Which ShopSphere components actually need which tier

DR tier is a per-component decision, not a whole-platform one — applying the most expensive tier uniformly wastes money on components that don't need it.

ShopSphere componentRealistic DR tierWhy
Order, PaymentWarm Standby, trending toward Active/Active as the business scalesRevenue-critical, customer-facing, legally/financially sensitive data
InventoryWarm StandbyMust stay consistent with Order to avoid overselling after failover
Catalog, SearchPilot LightRebuildable from source data; brief staleness is tolerable
RecommendationBackup & Restore, or simply degrade (Module 32's fallback)Non-critical — the fallback list is an acceptable substitute, not a disaster
Analytics / data lakeBackup & RestoreNot customer-facing; a day's delay rebuilding reporting is a business inconvenience, not an outage

08 Hands-on lab

  1. ShopSphere's CFO asks why DR for the Recommendation service costs so little compared to Order/Payment. Write the two-sentence business justification, referencing Module 32's fallback behavior.
  2. Pick one ShopSphere component currently at Pilot Light and argue, with a specific business scenario, for upgrading it to Warm Standby — and separately, what it would cost to do so.
  3. Explain, in your own words, why the jump from Warm Standby to Active/Active is not "one more notch" on the same dial the way Pilot Light to Warm Standby is.

09 Interview questions

A stakeholder wants "RTO of zero" for every ShopSphere service. What's the right response?
Push back on the blanket requirement, not the number itself. RTO near-zero is achievable (Active/Active) but costs roughly double the infrastructure and introduces real multi-region data-consistency complexity — appropriate for genuinely revenue-critical paths like Order/Payment, but wasteful for components like Recommendations that already have an acceptable degraded fallback. The right answer differentiates DR tier by component, backed by the actual cost and complexity of each tier.
Why is the cost curve from Backup & Restore to Active/Active not linear?
Backup & Restore to Pilot Light to Warm Standby each add infrastructure incrementally — more of the stack kept warm, but still scaled down relative to production. Active/Active requires full production-equivalent capacity running continuously in a second region at all times, which is a step change in committed spend, not an incremental one — roughly doubling cost rather than adding a fraction more.
What's the single hardest problem Active/Active introduces that the other three tiers don't have to solve?
Multi-region write conflict resolution. In Backup & Restore, Pilot Light and Warm Standby, only one region accepts writes at a time, so there's one source of truth. In Active/Active, both regions can accept writes simultaneously, and the system needs an explicit strategy — last-write-wins, regional ownership per customer, or similar — for when the same data is modified in both regions before either knows about the other's change.

Level 9 · Multi-Region & Disaster Recovery

Multi-Region & Global Architecture

● ~26 min read● Builds on Module 34● ShopSphere: active/passive today, by choice

Bottom line: ShopSphere runs active/passive today — every write lands in ap-south-1, and ap-southeast-1 stands by as Module 34's Warm Standby tier. This module covers the AWS services that make that pattern work, and is explicit about what it would actually take to go active/active — and why ShopSphere, like most companies, deliberately hasn't.

01 Route 53 routing policies — sending each customer to the right place

Routing policyDecides based onShopSphere use
Latency-basedWhich region gives this resolver the lowest measured latencyOnce active/active: route each customer to whichever region is physically closer
FailoverHealth checks — primary vs. secondary endpointToday: route to ap-south-1 unless its health check fails, then shift to ap-southeast-1
GeolocationThe DNS query's geographic originCompliance: steer a customer segment to a specific region for data-residency reasons

ShopSphere's current DNS layer uses failover routing — Route 53 health-checks ap-south-1's endpoints and only shifts traffic to ap-southeast-1 when they fail, which is precisely what an active/passive posture needs. Latency-based routing is the policy that would matter once a second region serves live traffic, not before.

02 CloudFront and Global Accelerator solve different problems

Module 2 introduced both as edge-network services; it's worth restating precisely why they're not interchangeable. CloudFront is a CDN — it caches cacheable content (product images, static assets, cacheable API responses) at edge locations close to the customer, so a repeat request never has to travel back to ap-south-1 at all. Global Accelerator solves a different problem: for traffic that can't be cached — a checkout POST, a personalized API call — it still gets the request onto AWS's private backbone network as early as possible, at the nearest edge location, instead of traversing the public internet all the way to the region. Caching has nothing to help a POST that must reach Order every time; routing optimization has nothing to help a product image that should never leave the edge in the first place.

CloudFrontGlobal Accelerator
Problem solvedAvoid the round trip entirely, for cacheable contentShorten the round trip, for everything else
Best fit on ShopSphereProduct images, static assets, cacheable catalog responsesCheckout, payment, personalized/dynamic API traffic

03 Cross-region database replication

Aurora Global DatabaseApplication-level multi-region writes
Write modelSingle write region; the second region is read-onlyBoth regions can accept writes
ReplicationPurpose-built storage-layer replication, typically well under a second of lagWhatever the application implements — no built-in guarantee
Conflict resolutionNot needed — there's only one writerRequired — reopens Module 18's CAP/consistency trade-off
FitsActive/passive, or active/active reads with centralized writesTrue active/active writes — only when the business case justifies the complexity

ShopSphere's actual setup

ShopSphere's Order and Payment data uses Aurora Global Database for exactly the read side of its Warm Standby posture: ap-southeast-1 holds a low-lag, read-only copy, kept current for the warm capacity Module 34 describes, ready to be promoted to the write region if ap-south-1 genuinely fails. There is still only one write region at a time — which is exactly why there's no conflict to resolve today.

04 What going active/active would actually take

Going active/active means both regions accepting writes, not just serving reads — and that reopens the hardest part of Module 34's active/active discussion in concrete terms. An order placed by a customer routed to ap-southeast-1 and an order placed by the same customer routed to ap-south-1 within the same window need a resolution strategy if they ever touch the same data (the same inventory count, the same cart):

StrategyHow it worksTrade-off
Last-write-winsThe later timestamp wins; the earlier write is discarded or mergedSimple, but can silently discard a legitimate write — clock skew matters
Regional ownership per customerEach customer is "owned" by one region for writes, regardless of which region serves their readsRemoves most conflicts by construction, but requires routing writes back to the owning region — extra latency for a traveling customer

05 Why ShopSphere stays active/passive, deliberately

This is a choice, not a limitation

Active/active would roughly double ShopSphere's regional infrastructure cost (Module 34) and require building and operating a conflict-resolution strategy for every write path that could touch the same data from two regions — a genuine, ongoing engineering investment, not a one-time setup. Today's ap-south-1-first, India-focused customer base (Module 02's region decision) doesn't have enough latency-sensitive traffic originating near ap-southeast-1 to justify that cost and complexity. Most companies, including ShopSphere, make the same call: active/passive with a fast Warm Standby covers the realistic failure scenario (a regional outage) without paying the active/active tax every single day it isn't needed.

The trigger for revisiting this decision isn't "could we," it's a business case — genuine international growth with latency-sensitive demand near a second region, or a regulatory requirement for simultaneous multi-region write locality, would be the kind of justification that tips the cost/complexity trade the other way.

06 ShopSphere's global request path today

07 Hands-on lab

  1. Write the Route 53 health-check condition (in plain English) that should trigger failover from ap-south-1 to ap-southeast-1, and explain why a single failed health check shouldn't be enough to trigger it.
  2. ShopSphere launches a small customer base in the UAE, latency-sensitive to ap-south-1. Using Module 2's region-selection framework plus this module's CloudFront/Global Accelerator distinction, propose what changes and what doesn't.
  3. Pick the conflict-resolution strategy (last-write-wins or regional ownership) you'd recommend specifically for ShopSphere's Inventory domain if it ever went active/active, and justify it against the specific risk of overselling.

08 Interview questions

What's the practical difference between CloudFront and Global Accelerator, and why does ShopSphere need both rather than picking one?
CloudFront caches content at the edge so cacheable requests never reach the origin region at all; Global Accelerator optimizes the network path for requests that must reach the origin, getting them onto AWS's backbone sooner. ShopSphere's product images and static assets benefit from CloudFront's caching, while checkout and payment calls are never cacheable and instead benefit from Global Accelerator's routing optimization — two different kinds of traffic with two different bottlenecks.
Aurora Global Database replicates with sub-second lag. Does that mean ShopSphere can safely promote the secondary region to accept writes at any time?
No — low replication lag minimizes data loss on a planned or unplanned failover, but Aurora Global Database's architecture still has exactly one write region at a time. Promoting the secondary to also accept writes without first making it the sole writer (or without a conflict-resolution strategy) would create the active/active write-conflict problem Aurora Global Database is specifically designed to avoid.
Why might "regional ownership per customer" be a safer default than "last-write-wins" for an e-commerce order path?
Last-write-wins can silently discard a legitimate write when two updates to the same data happen close together across regions, which for something like inventory counts or order state can produce an incorrect result with no error raised anywhere. Regional ownership avoids the conflict by construction — a given customer's writes always go to one region — at the cost of extra latency for a customer physically located far from their owning region, which is a trade most businesses find easier to reason about and explain than silent data loss.
