Level 10 · Principal Architect

Cost Optimization Strategies

● ~26 min read● Builds on Modules 05, 11, 18/19● ShopSphere: audits its AWS bill

Bottom line: at ShopSphere's scale, cost is an architecture property, not a line item finance negotiates after the fact. The same decisions that make a system available, scalable and observable — purchasing model, network topology, caching, data tiering — are the decisions that make it cheap or expensive. A Principal Architect reviews cost the same way they review availability: by category, by driver, and by what was traded away to get the number down.

01 The cost drivers that actually dominate a system like ShopSphere

Most teams default to watching the EC2/Fargate line item, because it's the biggest single number on the bill. In practice, several quieter categories grow faster than anyone budgets for, precisely because nobody owns them the way an engineering team owns compute.

DriverWhy it's easy to missShopSphere example
Compute sizing & purchasing modelVisible, but teams default to On-Demand and over-provision "to be safe"Checkout fleet sized for flash-sale peak, running 24/7 at that size
Data transfer — NAT Gateway & cross-AZBilled per-GB, invisible until traffic grows; no single dashboard screams "NAT"Every S3 call from a private subnet routes through a NAT Gateway unless a VPC Endpoint exists (Module 11)
Storage tieringObjects default to Standard forever unless a lifecycle policy says otherwiseThree-year-old product images and order invoices, still on S3 Standard
Database instance sizingSized once at launch, rarely revisited as access patterns changeAn Aurora writer sized for Black Friday, idling at 8% CPU the other 360 days
Logging & observability volumeScales automatically with traffic — nobody deliberately decided the volumeVerbose debug-level logs from every service, ingested and retained at full price (Module 29)
Idle non-production resources"Temporary" dev/staging environments that outlive the sprint that created themA full staging replica of ShopSphere, including its own NAT Gateways, running nights and weekends

The NAT Gateway trap, specifically

A NAT Gateway bills an hourly charge and a per-GB data processing charge for everything that passes through it — and every AWS API call from a private subnet (S3, DynamoDB, Secrets Manager, CloudWatch Logs) travels through it by default, round-tripping out to the public AWS API endpoint and back. Module 11 covers the fix: a Gateway VPC Endpoint (free, for S3/DynamoDB) or an Interface VPC Endpoint (hourly charge, but far below NAT's per-GB cost at any real volume) keeps that traffic on AWS's private network entirely, bypassing the NAT Gateway's data processing fee for that traffic. For a service calling S3 constantly — ShopSphere's Catalog and Order services both do — this is routinely one of the single highest-leverage cost fixes available, and it's also a latency and security win, not just a cost one.

02 Compute: purchasing model and right-sizing

Module 05 introduced the EC2 purchasing spectrum — On-Demand, Savings Plans, Reserved Instances, Spot. At cost-optimization maturity, the question isn't "which one," it's "which one, for which slice of this specific workload":

Workload sliceRight leverWhy
Steady baseline (checkout, catalog, order services at normal load)Compute Savings Plans, 1–3 year termKnown, continuous usage; the discount compounds daily with no operational cost
Flash-sale burst capacityOn-Demand, scaled by Auto ScalingUnpredictable timing and depth; committing to it would mean paying for idle capacity most of the year
Fault-tolerant batch/CI work (report generation, image resizing, test suites)SpotInterruption-tolerant by design; up to ~90% off, with AWS's 2-minute interruption notice enough to checkpoint or retry
Non-prod (dev/staging) outside working hoursScheduled stop/startZero functional cost — nobody is using it at 2am

Right-sizing closes the gap between what's provisioned and what's used. AWS Compute Optimizer analyzes actual CPU, memory and network utilization history and recommends specific instance types/sizes (and Lambda memory settings) — routinely finding fleets running 2–4x larger than their real utilization justifies, the same pattern this module's worked example formalizes below.

03 Caching reduces compute spend, not just latency

Modules 18 and 19 framed ElastiCache and CDN caching as a performance decision. It's equally a cost decision: every catalog read served from Redis instead of Aurora is a read Aurora's provisioned capacity didn't need to absorb, and every product image served from a CloudFront edge cache instead of an origin fetch is a request ShopSphere's origin fleet never saw. A well-tuned cache layer often lets a database or compute tier be sized one or two instance classes smaller than it would need to be serving every request uncached — which is exactly why Architecture B in this module's worked example (§07) sizes its database tier down relative to Architecture A.

04 Storage tiering

S3 Lifecycle policies automatically transition objects between storage classes, or expire them, based on age — no application code involved. ShopSphere's product images stay on S3 Standard while actively merchandised, but order invoices and superseded product images rarely need millisecond retrieval after 90 days.

Object ageStorage classRetrieval
0–30 daysS3 StandardMilliseconds, frequent access expected
30–90 daysS3 Standard-IA / Intelligent-TieringMilliseconds, infrequent access
90 days – 7 yearsS3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval / Deep ArchiveMinutes to hours — fine for invoices pulled on customer request, not for live catalog assets

S3 Intelligent-Tiering is usually the pragmatic default when access patterns are hard to predict in advance — it moves objects between tiers automatically based on observed access and adds no retrieval latency penalty for the frequent-access tiers, at a small per-object monitoring fee.

05 Worked example: three illustrative ShopSphere architectures, three bills

These numbers are illustrative, not quotes

Every dollar figure below is a made-up, round illustrative estimate chosen to show how architecture decisions shift cost proportionally between categories — it is not current AWS pricing for any real workload. Always price a real design against the AWS Cost Explorer and the live AWS pricing pages before presenting a number to a stakeholder.

ArchitectureDescriptionIllustrative monthly total
AAlways-on, over-provisioned EC2 fleet sized for peak; no caching layer; a NAT Gateway in every AZ's subnet for every service, no VPC Endpoints; verbose logging at full retention; dev/staging mirrors prod 24/7~$10,000/mo
BRight-sized Auto Scaling Group (via Compute Optimizer) + Compute Savings Plans for baseline; S3 & DynamoDB Gateway Endpoints replacing most NAT traffic; ElastiCache in front of catalog/pricing reads; log retention trimmed to what SLOs actually need; non-prod on a schedule~$6,000/mo
CEverything in B, plus Spot for CI/batch/report-generation workloads and S3 Lifecycle tiering on images and invoices older than 90 days~$3,500/mo

06 What each reduction actually trades away

A Principal Architect never presents a lower number without also presenting what it cost architecturally — cost optimization is a trade-off like any other, not a free lunch.

Architecture B's trade-off

Aggressive caching (Module 18) means product pricing and inventory-availability reads can be briefly stale — a price change or last-unit sellout may take up to a cache TTL to be visible everywhere. ShopSphere accepts this everywhere except the final checkout confirmation step, which always reads the authoritative source. Right-sizing compute also means less slack for an unexpected spike between Auto Scaling reacting and new capacity coming online — a tighter margin than Architecture A's deliberately oversized fleet.

Architecture C's trade-off

Spot capacity can be reclaimed with a 2-minute warning. It's the right choice for ShopSphere's nightly recommendation-model retraining batch and CI test runners — both checkpoint-friendly and retryable — and it would be the wrong choice for the checkout service itself, where an instance disappearing mid-request is a customer-visible failure, not an acceptable interruption. S3 Lifecycle tiering to Glacier also means a customer requesting a 2-year-old invoice waits minutes, not milliseconds — acceptable for that specific, rare flow, and explicitly called out as a UX trade-off in the ADR that would formalize this decision (Module 37).

07 Hands-on lab

  1. ShopSphere has one NAT Gateway per AZ, and every service in a private subnet calls S3 and DynamoDB directly. List which of those calls can move to VPC Endpoints (Module 11) with zero code change, and which, if any, must still cross the NAT Gateway.
  2. Using AWS Compute Optimizer's public documentation, describe what data it needs observed before it can make a reliable right-sizing recommendation, and why a brand-new service with one week of traffic history is a weak candidate for its recommendations.
  3. Pick one of ShopSphere's batch workloads (nightly recommendation retraining, invoice PDF generation, CI test runners) and argue, in a paragraph, whether it's genuinely Spot-safe — what would have to be true about how it's built for an interruption to be a non-event rather than a failure.

08 Interview questions

Your team cut the AWS bill 40% by moving everything possible to Spot. What's the first question you'd ask before celebrating?
Which workloads moved to Spot, and are they actually interruption-tolerant — stateless, checkpointable or cheaply retryable — or did cost-cutting pressure quietly move something customer-facing (like checkout or payment processing) onto capacity that can vanish with 2 minutes' notice? A cost win that introduces a reliability regression on the customer path is not a win; it's a deferred incident.
Why does a NAT Gateway cost more at scale than most teams expect, and what's the fix that doesn't touch application code?
NAT Gateway bills both an hourly charge and a per-GB data processing charge for everything routed through it, and by default that includes every AWS API call (S3, DynamoDB, CloudWatch Logs, Secrets Manager) made from a private subnet, not just genuine internet-bound traffic. VPC Endpoints — Gateway Endpoints for S3/DynamoDB (free) or Interface Endpoints for most other AWS services (a flat hourly fee, no per-GB NAT markup) — route that traffic over AWS's private network instead, with no code change: only a routing/DNS-level change.
A VP asks you to cut the AWS bill 50% by next quarter. What do you refuse to do, even under that pressure?
Refuse to cut below the capacity margin that availability and performance SLOs depend on (undersizing the checkout fleet below flash-sale peak, for instance), refuse to move PCI-sensitive or customer-facing synchronous paths onto Spot, and refuse to disable the logging/tracing needed to detect and diagnose an incident just to save on ingestion cost. Cost optimization is a constrained search, not an unconstrained one — the constraints are the non-functional requirements from Module 1 onward, and a Principal Architect states them explicitly rather than quietly trading them away.

Level 10 · Principal Architect

Architecture Decision Records

● ~24 min read● Builds on Module 36● ShopSphere: formalizes two decisions already made

Bottom line: an ADR exists so a future engineer — often you, eighteen months from now — doesn't have to re-derive why a decision was made from scratch, or worse, re-litigate it and repeat reasoning that already happened. It captures the alternatives that were considered and rejected, not just the choice that won.

01 Why ADRs exist

Code tells you what the system does. A commit message tells you what changed. Neither reliably tells you why ShopSphere's shopping cart lives in DynamoDB instead of the Aurora database everything else uses, or why the primary region is ap-south-1 and not us-east-1. Without a record, that reasoning lives only in the heads of whoever was in the room — and leaves with them.

The cost of that gap compounds. A new engineer, seeing an inconsistency (why does Cart use a different database than Order?), reasonably assumes it's an oversight and "fixes" it — undoing a deliberate, reasoned trade-off because the reasoning wasn't written down anywhere they could find it. An ADR is cheap insurance against exactly that failure mode.

02 The standard ADR format

SectionWhat goes here
TitleA short noun phrase naming the decision, e.g. "Use DynamoDB for Shopping Cart"
StatusProposed / Accepted / Superseded by ADR-N — ADRs are rarely deleted, only superseded, preserving the history
ContextThe forces at play: traffic shape, consistency needs, team constraints, deadlines — the situation that made a decision necessary
Alternatives consideredEvery option seriously evaluated, including the ones rejected, and why each lost
DecisionThe one sentence that states what was actually chosen
ReasoningWhy this alternative won, tied back to the context — this is the section most worth writing carefully
Trade-offsWhat was explicitly given up by choosing this option over the others
ConsequencesWhat this decision now constrains or enables for decisions made later

This mirrors Module 31's academic framing almost exactly — it's the same discipline a Principal Architect applies to every irreversible or expensive-to-reverse choice, written down instead of kept in their head.

03 ADR-1, worked: the primary region decision

Module 2 chose ap-south-1 as ShopSphere's primary region informally, as part of learning the region-selection framework. Formalized as an ADR, that same reasoning becomes a record the whole organization can find and act on — including constraining the DR region choice made in Modules 34/35.

ADR-001: Primary AWS Region for ShopSphere

Status: Accepted

Context:
ShopSphere's initial customer base is concentrated in India. The
platform processes payment data subject to Indian data-residency
expectations, and checkout latency is a directly measured conversion
driver. A primary region must be chosen before any production
workload, networking, or database topology can be built.

Alternatives considered:
  - us-east-1 (N. Virginia) — largest service catalog, lowest
    prices for some services, but 200+ms round-trip latency to Indian
    customers and no data-residency alignment.
  - eu-west-1 (Ireland) — strong compliance tooling precedent,
    but similarly high latency to the core customer base and no
    residency alignment.
  - ap-south-1 (Mumbai) — full service parity with the above
    two, lowest latency to the primary customer base, residency
    aligned with Indian data-protection expectations.

Decision:
Use ap-south-1 (Mumbai) as ShopSphere's primary region.

Reasoning:
Latency to the primary customer base and data-residency alignment
dominate the decision (Module 2's four-factor framework, factors 1
and 2). ap-south-1 has full service availability for every AWS
service ShopSphere's roadmap currently requires, so factor 3 does
not override the first two. Compute/storage pricing differences
between candidate regions (factor 4) are a secondary, non-deciding
consideration at ShopSphere's current scale.

Trade-offs:
Smaller regional service catalog than us-east-1 for any
not-yet-launched AWS service; ShopSphere's DR region must now also
be chosen with ap-south-1 as its anchor point, constraining that
later decision to geographically appropriate candidates.

Consequences:
The disaster-recovery region (ADR to follow in Module 34/35) is
chosen for acceptable replication latency FROM ap-south-1, which is
why ap-southeast-1 (Singapore) — not a second candidate from this
list — becomes the DR region. Any future expansion into a new
customer geography is evaluated as an additional region, not a
re-litigation of this one.

04 ADR-2, worked: the shopping cart database

Modules 08 and 13 introduced DynamoDB as a candidate for the shopping cart without yet formalizing the decision. This is the ADR that formalizes it — the exact worked example the project's own curriculum points to.

ADR-002: Database for Shopping Cart

Status: Accepted

Context:
The shopping cart is read and written on nearly every page view for
a logged-in or session-identified shopper — far higher read/write
volume per item than Orders, with a much simpler access pattern:
fetch or update "this customer's cart" by a single key. Cart data
is also disposable in a way Orders is not — losing an in-progress
cart is an inconvenience, not a financial-reconciliation incident.

Alternatives considered:
  - RDS (PostgreSQL/MySQL) — relational, ACID, but this
    workload's single-key access pattern gets no benefit from joins
    or multi-row transactions, and RDS's connection-count ceiling is
    a real constraint at this request volume.
  - Aurora — higher ceiling than RDS, same mismatch between
    relational features and a pure key-value access pattern; more
    operational and cost overhead than the workload justifies.
  - ElastiCache (Redis) — matches the access pattern well and
    is faster, but is in-memory-first: a node failure or eviction
    under memory pressure can lose cart data outright without a
    persistence layer behind it.
  - DynamoDB — key-value native, scales read/write throughput
    horizontally and predictably, durable by default (unlike a pure
    cache), with pricing that tracks actual request volume.

Decision:
Use DynamoDB for the Shopping Cart service, with cart_id (keyed to
the customer/session) as the partition key.

Reasoning:
The access pattern is simple key-based lookups and updates at high
volume — exactly DynamoDB's strength — and the data does not need
relational joins or multi-item transactions. DynamoDB's durability
removes the data-loss risk that a cache-only (Redis) design would
carry, while avoiding the operational and cost overhead a relational
engine would add for no corresponding benefit here.

Trade-offs:
Query flexibility is limited to the access patterns the table's keys
and indexes were designed for — an ad-hoc "find all carts containing
product X" query, trivial in SQL, requires a separate index or a
scan. DynamoDB throughput and storage are billed by request/capacity
rather than a flat instance price, which needs monitoring as volume
scales, and a hot partition key (e.g. one promotional SKU in every
cart during a flash sale) can throttle a single key's throughput
even while the table overall has capacity to spare (Module 22).

Consequences:
Any future cart feature that needs flexible querying (e.g.
merchandising wants to query active carts by category) requires an
explicit secondary index or a stream into OpenSearch/analytics,
not an ad-hoc query against this table. A cache (ElastiCache) may
still sit in front of DynamoDB later purely for latency, without
revisiting this decision.

05 When an ADR is overkill — and when it's essential

Skip the ADR

Choosing a logging library, a code formatter, which EC2 instance size (not family or purchasing model) to start a new low-traffic internal tool on, or a config value that's one deploy away from being changed back — all reversible, all low-stakes, all quietly correctable if wrong. Writing a full ADR for each of these is process theater that trains people to stop reading ADRs.

Write the ADR

Primary region, the database engine behind a core domain, the messaging backbone (SQS vs. Kafka/MSK), the authentication provider, anything that touches PCI scope, or a decision that two teams disagree about and need a durable, citable resolution for. The test that matters most in practice: if you can picture a future engineer asking "wait, why did we do it this way?" and not finding an easy answer, that's the ADR you needed to write today.

06 Hands-on lab

  1. Write ADR-003 for Module 23's Saga pattern choice: "Use a Saga with compensating transactions for Order + Payment + Inventory, instead of a distributed (two-phase commit) transaction." Fill in Context, Alternatives, Decision, Reasoning, Trade-offs and Consequences in your own words.
  2. Find one decision in a codebase you work on (or a past project) that was never written down anywhere. Write the ADR for it retroactively, from memory or by asking whoever made the call — notice how much of the "Alternatives considered" section you have to reconstruct rather than simply recall.
  3. Take ADR-002 above and write the "Status: Superseded" version that would exist if ShopSphere later migrated the cart to a different store at 50x its current scale. What in the original ADR's Reasoning section would you expect to no longer hold?

07 Interview questions

What's the single most valuable section of an ADR, and why is it the one teams skip?
"Alternatives considered." It's the one section that requires admitting other options were real and taking the time to explain why each lost — which is more work than just stating the decision, and feels unnecessary in the moment because the decision-makers already know why. It's exactly what a future reader can't reconstruct on their own, which is why skipping it defeats much of the ADR's purpose.
A teammate says "we don't need ADRs, it's all in the Slack thread." What's wrong with that?
Slack threads are not discoverable by someone who doesn't know to search for them, are not structured (context, alternatives, reasoning, consequences all run together with jokes and tangents), and typically vanish from institutional memory within months as channels get archived or people change teams. An ADR is a durable, versioned, purpose-built artifact that lives with the codebase or architecture docs, specifically so it survives longer than any chat history.
How should an ADR's status change when a decision is later reversed — and why not just delete the old one?
Mark it "Superseded by ADR-00N" and write the new ADR referencing it, rather than deleting it. The old ADR still answers a real historical question — why the system was built that way for however long it was — and deleting it erases the very reasoning trail a future engineer might need, including whether the new decision correctly accounted for why the old one was made in the first place.

Level 10 · Principal Architect

Production Incident Simulations

● ~32 min read● Builds on Module 37● ShopSphere: six new incidents, principal-level difficulty

Bottom line: Module 30 taught the incident process — severity triage, communication, blameless postmortems. This module assumes you have that process and drills the harder skill underneath it: given a vague symptom and a system you already know well, what do you actually check, in what order, and why. Every answer below is hidden until you click — reason through each one yourself first.

01 How to use this module

Each scenario below gives you exactly what an on-call engineer gets at 2am: a symptom, not a diagnosis. Before opening the hidden answer, say out loud (or write down) your first three investigative steps and your leading hypothesis. Then compare. The goal isn't matching the answer verbatim — it's noticing which of your first three steps the answer validates, and which it reveals you skipped.

02 Six incidents

Scenario 1 — A customer support ticket reports an order was charged twice. Logs show the Payment service received two separate charge requests for the same order_id, four seconds apart. What's going on, and how do you stop it from recurring?
This is downstream of SQS's at-least-once delivery guarantee (Module 14): a message can be delivered more than once, most commonly when a consumer takes longer to process and acknowledge a message than its visibility timeout allows, so SQS assumes the first delivery failed and redelivers it to another worker. The Payment service received the "charge this order" event twice and processed it twice because nothing made the second processing a no-op. The fix is not "make SQS exactly-once" — standard queues don't offer that, and even FIFO's deduplication window has limits. The fix is an idempotency key (Module 23): the Payment service persists a record of every order_id it has already successfully charged, keyed by a unique idempotency token carried on the event, and treats a repeat with the same token as a no-op success rather than a new charge. Investigate: confirm the visibility timeout is shorter than the service's actual p99 processing time (a frequent root cause), then confirm whether an idempotency check existed and was bypassed, or never existed at all.
Scenario 2 — Checkout API p95 latency climbs from 100ms to 2 seconds over 20 minutes. Error rate is flat at baseline — nothing is failing, everything is just slow. Where do you look first?
No error-rate increase rules out the obvious "a dependency is down" story and points at saturation or a slow dependency that still eventually succeeds (Module 20). Check, in order: (1) connection pool exhaustion — if the checkout service's DB or downstream HTTP client pool is maxed out, new requests queue for a free connection before doing any real work, which looks exactly like uniform latency growth with no errors; (2) a downstream dependency slowing down without a circuit breaker (Module 21) — if the Fraud or Inventory service's own latency crept up, and checkout calls it synchronously with a generous timeout and no breaker, checkout's latency inherits the dependency's latency directly, one call at a time; (3) a database query plan regression — a statistics update, a data-distribution shift, or an index that silently stopped being used can turn a millisecond query into a hundred-millisecond one with no code change at all. The fast way to tell these apart: check connection-pool utilization and queue depth first (cheapest to check), then per-dependency latency breakdown via X-Ray traces (Module 29) to see which hop actually grew, before touching the database.
Scenario 3 — This month's AWS bill is 300% higher than last month's, with no corresponding increase in order volume or traffic. Finance wants an explanation by end of day. What do you check, and in what order?
Traffic is flat, so this isn't organic growth — something is either misconfigured or looping. Check, in order of how fast each is to rule in or out: (1) AWS Cost Explorer, grouped by service and then by usage type (Module 36) — this alone usually identifies which single service category jumped, in under five minutes; (2) if it's data transfer or VPC-related, check for a misconfigured NAT Gateway suddenly processing far more GB than usual — a new service deployed into a private subnet without a VPC Endpoint, now routing high-volume traffic through NAT where it didn't before; (3) if it's Lambda or a specific compute service, check for a runaway retry loop — a Lambda failing and retrying against a dead-lettered or misconfigured destination can silently multiply invocations (and cost) without ever showing up as "traffic" in the usual sense; (4) check for an accidentally public, data-transfer-heavy resource — an S3 bucket or endpoint made public by mistake, now serving requests from outside ShopSphere's expected traffic entirely, which is why "no increase in order volume" doesn't rule this out. Cost Explorer's breakdown should point you at which of these three it is before you touch any individual resource.
Scenario 4 — AWS reports a service disruption affecting ap-south-1. Customer traffic is failing. Walk through an actual failover execution to ap-southeast-1, step by step, and name what could go wrong in the failover itself.
Module 34/35's Warm Standby posture means ap-southeast-1 already has running (but scaled-down) capacity and continuously replicated data — the failover is a traffic cutover, not a cold start, but it's still a sequence with real failure points. Steps: (1) confirm this is a genuine region-level event, not a single service or AZ issue inside ap-south-1 — failing over for the wrong diagnosis wastes the RTO budget; (2) promote the standby database (Aurora Global Database or DynamoDB Global Tables) to primary in ap-southeast-1 — this is a one-way action with a brief write-unavailability window during promotion; (3) scale the standby compute fleet up from its idle baseline to full capacity — if this scaling step wasn't pre-tested, it's where latent capacity or quota limits surface for the first time, under pressure; (4) update Route 53 failover routing (or Global Accelerator) to shift traffic to ap-southeast-1 — DNS TTLs and client-side caching mean this doesn't cut over instantaneously for every client; (5) verify the application in ap-southeast-1 can actually reach every dependency it needs (Secrets Manager, third-party payment provider endpoints, etc.) from that region — a dependency hardcoded or scoped only to ap-south-1 is a common failover-time surprise. What goes wrong in practice: the standby's scale-up step was never load-tested at full production volume, so it fails or saturates right when it's needed most — which is exactly why Module 34's DR tiers insist on regularly drilling the failover, not just architecting for it on paper.
Scenario 5 — A deploy went out two hours ago. Error rates and latency look normal in aggregate, but a cluster of customer complaints mentions the same specific symptom: duplicate items silently appearing in the cart. What's the likely cause, and how should this have been caught before full rollout?
A bug that only manifests for a small percentage of traffic and doesn't move aggregate error-rate or latency metrics is the exact failure mode canary deployments and fine-grained observability exist to catch (Modules 27 and 29). It's plausible the bug only triggers on a specific code path — a particular cart state, a race between two near-simultaneous "add to cart" calls, or a feature flag rollout percentage — which is diluted to invisibility in an aggregate dashboard. It should have been caught by: (1) a canary deployment routing a small percentage of production traffic to the new version first, with metrics compared against the stable version specifically for that slice, not blended into the whole fleet's aggregate; (2) segmented observability — dashboards and alarms that can slice by deploy version/cohort, not just by service, so a regression confined to one cohort is visible instead of averaged away; (3) structured customer-impact signals (support ticket tagging, error boundary reporting from the client) feeding back into the same observability pipeline, since this bug produced no server-side error at all — the cart write "succeeded," just with duplicated data. The fix going forward isn't just "find and patch this bug" — it's tightening the canary's bake time and the specificity of what's monitored during it.
Scenario 6 — The third-party fraud-detection model ShopSphere calls during checkout starts timing out intermittently. Checkout is now failing for every customer, not just the ones who would have been flagged. What should have prevented this, and how do you stabilize it right now?
This is a hard dependency that should have been a soft one. Fraud scoring is a risk-mitigation signal, not a correctness requirement of checkout — unlike payment authorization, there is no reason a timeout on the fraud check should block the order outright. It should have been wrapped in a circuit breaker with an explicit timeout (Module 21): after a threshold of consecutive timeouts, the breaker opens and checkout proceeds either with a conservative default decision (e.g. route to manual review or step-up verification instead of blocking) or genuinely skips the check for a bounded window while alerting — a deliberate, pre-agreed degraded mode, not an accidental one. To stabilize right now: lower the fraud-check timeout aggressively so it fails fast instead of holding checkout requests open; manually force the circuit breaker open (or deploy a feature flag that bypasses the fraud call) so checkout succeeds immediately while the fraud provider recovers; and route orders that would have gone through fraud scoring into a queue for asynchronous, post-checkout review instead of blocking the customer-facing transaction on it. The postmortem fix is architectural: no third-party call on the synchronous checkout path should be able to turn into a hard outage for a function that was supposed to be advisory.

03 Where each failure originates

04 Hands-on lab

  1. Pick Scenario 2 (latency 100ms→2s). Write the exact sequence of CloudWatch/X-Ray dashboards you'd pull up, in order, and what result at each step would make you stop looking further and declare a root cause.
  2. Pick Scenario 6 (fraud-check timeout). Design the degraded-mode decision in one paragraph: when the circuit breaker is open, should orders route to manual review, be auto-approved up to some value threshold, or something else — and what's the business risk of whichever you pick?
  3. Write a one-paragraph blameless postmortem summary (Module 30's format) for Scenario 1 (double-charged order), assuming the idempotency key was present in code but had a bug that made two different-looking tokens hash to the same key.

05 Interview questions

What's the practical difference between "at-least-once delivery" and "exactly-once processing," and why does ShopSphere need the second without the first actually existing?
At-least-once delivery is a messaging-system guarantee: a message will arrive one or more times, never zero. Exactly-once processing is an application-level outcome: the effect of handling a message happens once, no matter how many times the message itself arrives. SQS standard queues give you the first; idempotency keys at the consumer are how you build the second on top of it — true exactly-once delivery at the transport layer is effectively a myth at scale (Module 22), so production systems get the "exactly-once effect" through idempotent consumers instead.
Why is "error rate is flat" not the same as "nothing is wrong," using Scenario 2 as the example?
Error rate only captures requests that fail outright. A request that succeeds but takes 20x longer than normal reports as a success in every error-rate dashboard, even though it's degrading the user experience and may be minutes away from becoming a cascading failure (timeouts, pool exhaustion, retries piling up). Latency percentiles (p95/p99) and saturation metrics catch this class of problem; error-rate alone does not, which is exactly why Module 20's performance budgets track both.
A dependency call is "optional" for correctness but currently has no timeout at all. Why is that worse than having no circuit breaker?
A missing circuit breaker means repeated failures aren't automatically short-circuited, but a missing timeout means a single slow call can hold a thread/connection open indefinitely, which exhausts the caller's own connection pool and turns one slow dependency into a full outage of the caller itself — the exact mechanism behind Scenario 6. A sane timeout bounds the damage of one slow call; a circuit breaker then prevents that bounded damage from repeating on every subsequent request once a pattern of failure is detected. You need the timeout first — a circuit breaker around calls with no timeout is still exposed to the single-call damage the timeout exists to prevent.

Level 10 · Principal Architect · Capstone

Capstone: Design ShopSphere at Global Scale

● ~40 min read● Builds on every module in this course● ShopSphere: the final design

Bottom line: this is the design a Principal Architect is actually hired to produce — not a diagram of every AWS service that exists, but a specific, defensible set of choices against a specific set of numbers, with the trade-offs stated out loud instead of hidden. Work the brief yourself before reading the worked design below; a model answer is only useful to calibrate against once you've attempted your own.

01 The brief

RequirementFigure
Registered customers100 million
Daily active users10 million
Peak concurrent users (flash sale)1 million
Peak order rate500,000 orders/hour
Customer baseGlobal
Availability target99.99%
Latency targetAPI p95 < 300ms
RPO< 5 minutes
RTO< 15 minutes
CompliancePCI-sensitive payment data; strong security posture required
TopologyMulti-region, with disaster recovery
Functional scopeReal-time notifications, search, recommendations, analytics, fraud detection

02 Assumptions & capacity estimation

500,000 orders/hour is ~139 orders/second sustained at peak. Each checkout touches several read-heavy supporting calls (product detail, cart, pricing, inventory check, recommendations) before the single order-write — assume a conservative 15:1 ratio of supporting API calls to order-completion calls. That puts peak API traffic in the rough neighborhood of 2,000–2,500 requests/second sustained at the system's busiest hour, with a further burst multiplier (assume 3–5x over the sustained figure for the first minutes of a flash sale, before Auto Scaling and queue-based leveling catch up) putting momentary peak demand in the 6,000–10,000 req/s range. These are planning-order-of-magnitude figures, not a committed SLA number — a real capacity plan refines them with actual observed traffic shape (Module 20) and load tests the resulting architecture against both the sustained and burst figures before trusting either.

Assumptions stated explicitly

1 million concurrent users does not mean 1 million simultaneous in-flight requests — most of those sessions are idle, browsing, or waiting on a client-side timer; 2 million DAU is the active-usage figure capacity planning should be anchored to, with concurrency as a WebSocket/notification connection-count concern (Module 29) rather than a request-rate one. "Global customers" is assumed to mean meaningfully concentrated in 2–3 broad geographies rather than perfectly uniform worldwide — which is what justifies a small number of full regional stacks plus edge caching, rather than a much larger number of regions.

03 High-level architecture

Request flow: a request resolves through Route 53 (latency-based routing, with health-check-driven failover) to the nearest healthy region's edge; CloudFront serves cacheable assets directly and forwards everything else through WAF for request filtering before it ever reaches a region's ALB. Inside the primary region (ap-south-1), the ALB routes to the relevant domain service running on ECS/Fargate; checkout specifically drives the Saga (reserve inventory → charge payment → confirm inventory → schedule shipment → notify), coordinated through SQS/EventBridge with compensating transactions on failure (Module 23). Each domain owns its own data store — Aurora for Order/Payment, DynamoDB for Cart/Inventory-availability/Catalog, OpenSearch for Search, ElastiCache in front of Catalog/Pricing reads. Order and inventory events stream into the data & AI layer (Kinesis/MSK → S3 data lake → Redshift for BI, plus SageMaker for recommendations and Bedrock for the support chatbot, both consuming from the same lake) independent of the synchronous checkout path, so analytics and AI workloads never become a dependency of order completion.

04 Layer by layer

LayerDesign
EdgeRoute 53 latency + failover routing, CloudFront for static/cacheable assets, AWS WAF + Shield in front of every public entry point
APIAPI Gateway / ALB per region, Cognito-issued JWTs validated at the edge of each service, rate limiting per customer and per IP
ComputeECS/Fargate for steady-state domain services; Lambda for event-driven work (notifications, image processing, report generation)
Checkout orchestrationSaga across Order/Payment/Inventory/Shipment/Notification, with an Outbox table per service and compensating transactions on any step's failure
DatabasesAurora (Multi-AZ, Global Database) for Order/Payment; DynamoDB (Global Tables) for Cart/Inventory/Catalog; OpenSearch for Search; Redshift for BI
CachingElastiCache in front of Catalog/Pricing reads; CloudFront at the edge for product media and static content
MessagingSQS for point-to-point work queues, SNS/EventBridge for fan-out, DLQs on every queue, idempotency keys on every consumer
Security / IAMLeast-privilege IAM roles per service, KMS encryption at rest everywhere, TLS in transit, PCI scope isolated to the Payment service and its dedicated network segment (§05), Secrets Manager for credentials
ObservabilityCloudWatch metrics/alarms tied to SLOs, X-Ray tracing across every service hop, CloudTrail for audit, structured logs with cohort/version tagging (Module 38, Scenario 5)
CI/CDCodePipeline/CodeBuild per service, canary deployments with automatic rollback on SLO breach, security scanning as a gate before production
IaCEvery environment (including DR) defined and deployed from the same CDK/Terraform modules, not hand-built, so the standby region never drifts from primary
DR postureWarm Standby in ap-southeast-1 today (§05 discusses whether this is actually sufficient at this scale)
Data & AIKinesis/MSK → S3 data lake → Redshift; SageMaker for recommendations; Bedrock-based support chatbot; fraud model called from checkout through a circuit breaker, never as a hard dependency (Module 38, Scenario 6)

05 Trade-offs and open questions a real review would raise

Is Warm Standby actually enough for a 15-minute RTO, at this scale?

Warm Standby (Module 34/35) means the DR region runs at reduced capacity and scales up during failover — and §04's walkthrough of a failover (Module 38, Scenario 4) showed exactly where that scale-up step can blow the RTO budget: if it's never been drilled at full production volume, it's the least-tested, highest-stakes step in the entire design. At 500,000 orders/hour and a hard 15-minute RTO, the honest open question is whether this scale finally justifies Active/Active — both regions continuously serving production traffic, so "failover" becomes a routing change instead of a promotion-and-scale-up sequence. The cost and complexity of Active/Active (bidirectional data replication with conflict resolution, doubled steady-state compute spend, and genuinely active-active database semantics for Order/Payment, not just Cart/Catalog) is real, and Module 34/35 is explicit that it's not a default — but a real Principal Architect review does not let "we chose Warm Standby in Module 34" stand unexamined once the capstone's numbers are this aggressive. This capstone's recommendation: pressure-test Warm Standby's actual scale-up time under a full-volume drill before accepting it; if that drill can't reliably clear 15 minutes, this is the single highest-priority architectural gap in the design.

Where does PCI scope need to be isolated?

Treating "the Payment service" as the PCI boundary is necessary but not sufficient — cardholder data that transits logging, tracing, or a shared message bus can silently pull those systems into PCI scope too, which is exactly the kind of creeping scope expansion a PCI assessor will find. The design needs an explicit, narrow network segment for whatever actually touches cardholder data (ideally tokenized at the edge of that boundary via the payment processor, so raw card data never enters ShopSphere's own services at all), with logging/tracing in that segment scrubbed or excluded from the shared observability pipeline rather than flowing through it by default.

Other open questions a thorough review raises: does Search (OpenSearch) and Recommendations (SageMaker) need regional replication at all, or can a single-region deployment with cross-region read latency be acceptable for non-critical-path features, trading some latency for meaningfully less operational complexity? Does the Saga's compensating-transaction logic have an upper retry bound before it escalates to a human/manual-review queue, so a stuck compensation doesn't silently strand an order forever?

06 Principal Architect review

DimensionScoreWhy
Functional9/10Every required capability (search, recommendations, notifications, fraud, analytics) has an explicit owner and data path
Scalability9/10Horizontal scaling and per-domain data stores throughout; no obvious single bottleneck at the stated traffic figures
Availability8/10Multi-AZ throughout and a defined DR posture, but §05's open question on Warm Standby's untested failover time is a real gap, not a formality
Reliability8/10Sagas, circuit breakers and idempotency are designed in from the start, which is the right foundation; genuine confidence requires the failure-injection drills Module 38 describes, not yet evidenced here
Security7/10The weakest score in this design. IAM, encryption and WAF are all present, but §05's PCI-scope-isolation question is unresolved on paper — a design that hasn't drawn an explicit, narrow PCI network boundary and audited what crosses it (logs, traces, shared buses) has a real, not theoretical, compliance gap. This needs a dedicated security architecture pass before it's audit-ready.
Performance8/10The p95 < 300ms target is achievable with the caching and edge strategy described, but §02's capacity figures are planning estimates, not load-test results — the number is credible, not yet proven
Cost7/10Module 36's levers (Savings Plans, right-sizing, caching, VPC Endpoints) are all present in principle, but an Active/Active pivot from §05 would materially change the cost model and hasn't been priced
Operations8/10IaC-managed environments and CI/CD with canaries give engineers a real operating model; cross-team runbooks for a region failover specifically still need to be written and drilled, not just designed
Observability9/10Tracing, structured logs and SLO-tied alarms are designed in from the start, directly informed by Module 38's incident scenarios rather than bolted on afterward
Disaster recovery7/10A real DR posture exists and is documented, but §05 is explicit that its RTO claim is unverified at this scale until drilled — the same gap as Availability, scored separately because DR is a distinct discipline with its own testing obligation
Maintainability9/10Domain-oriented services, database-per-domain, and ADRs (Module 37) for the decisions that matter give future engineers both the structure and the reasoning trail to evolve this safely

Overall

A strong, defensible design — not a perfect one, and a real review shouldn't produce a perfect one. The two dimensions that keep this from being an unreserved "ship it" are Security (the PCI boundary needs to be drawn explicitly, not assumed) and the Availability/Reliability/DR cluster (Warm Standby's RTO claim is currently a design intent, not a measured result). Both are fixable without a fundamental redesign — which is itself part of what makes this a good architecture rather than a fragile one.

07 Extend the capstone

  1. Take the Warm-Standby-vs-Active/Active question from §05 and write it up as ADR-004 (Module 37's format), including a rough illustrative cost comparison (Module 36's style) between the two options at this capstone's traffic figures.
  2. Design the explicit PCI network boundary from §05: which services, data stores, and log/trace destinations sit inside it, which sit outside, and what crosses the boundary (and in what form — tokenized, encrypted, or not at all).
  3. Pick any single dimension from §06's review scored below 9 and write the concrete three-month workplan that would raise it by two points — specific deliverables, not just intentions.

08 Interview questions

Defend the choice of Warm Standby over Active/Active for this capstone's numbers — or argue against your own design.
The honest answer argues against it, at least provisionally: a 15-minute RTO at 500,000 orders/hour leaves very little margin for a failover sequence that includes database promotion and a cold compute scale-up, and if that sequence has never been drilled at full volume, the RTO claim is unverified, not false but unproven. The defensible position is not "Warm Standby is wrong," it's "Warm Standby is the right starting posture, with an explicit, funded commitment to drill it at full volume and a pre-agreed trigger (a failed drill, or further growth past some threshold) for revisiting Active/Active" — exactly the kind of conditional, evidence-based answer a Principal Architect interview is testing for over a confident absolute one.
Why does this design keep Search, Recommendations and Analytics out of the synchronous checkout path entirely?
None of the three are required for an order to complete correctly, and each has materially different availability/latency needs than checkout itself — OpenSearch being briefly degraded should never block someone from completing a purchase they found another way. Keeping them fed by asynchronous events off the order stream, rather than called synchronously during checkout, means their failure modes (Module 38's Scenario 6 in miniature) can't cascade into the one flow the whole business depends on.
A stakeholder says the Principal Architect review's scores look "too honest" and asks you to round everything up before a board presentation. What do you do?
Keep the scores as assessed and reframe the presentation, not the review: an honest review with a credible remediation plan for its weak points is a stronger signal of engineering maturity to a board than an inflated one, and the first real incident that exposes an inflated "10/10" costs far more credibility than an honest "7/10 with a dated plan to fix it" ever would. The review's job is to be accurate; the presentation's job is to make the accurate version land well — those are different problems, and solving the second by breaking the first is a mistake a Principal Architect should refuse.
